☆ In the Guise of Correction: How Framing and Cultural Adaptation Erode LLM Safety on Real-World Criminal Cases
Mohsinul Kabir, Md. Tariquzzaman, Karam S. R. Elradie, Polydoros Giannouris, Yuechen Jiang, Shaoxiong Ji, Sophia Ananiadou
Existing safety benchmarks rely largely on synthetic prompts with loosely grounded taxonomies and offer little focused analysis of severe crime categories. We introduce GUISE, a benchmark of criminal-assistance scenarios sourced from real-world cases and organised under a taxonomy from the UNODC International Classification of Crime for Statistical Purposes (ICCS). Each scenario is rendered under three linguistic framings and evaluated across five distinct cultures. Using our proposed two-tier evaluation framework, which judges both harmfulness and operational uplift, we find that: (i) framing alone drives unsafe compliance, with the mean unsafe compliance rate rising from 28.9% under direct requests to 66.4% under correction seeking (peaking at 97.3%; p < 0.05), as models correcting deliberately distorted details supply executable criminal steps; (ii) culturally adapted prompts elicit significantly more unsafe compliance than literal translations of the same requests in all four non-English languages (+7 to +18 points), a risk that translation-based benchmarks miss; and (iii) unsafe responses from the least-safe models carry strong Anglocentric registers, and the extent of this leakage increases monotonically with response harmfulness ($ρ$ = 0.46 to 0.63 across languages, all p < 0.001). GUISE offers a grounded resource and fine-grained diagnostics for strengthening refusal alignment across languages and cultures. We publish our code and data to facilitate future research.

☆ Bias Mitigation in Audio-Enabled LLMs through Adversarial Training and Distillation Methods
Adnan Al Ali, Jindřich Libovický, Anne Lauscher
Audio-enabled large language models (AuLLMs) allow users to interact with them simply by talking to them, without the need for an intermediate transcription. This expands the model's accessibility and capabilities by encoding non-verbal cues. However, using non-verbal cues may lead to biased behaviour based on the attributes inferred (or assumed) from them. ☆ DeciCAD: Harness-Grounded Learning of Action Policies for CAD Agents
Yufeng Peng, Yixin Zhang, Ziyi Liu, Zhikun Xu, Jiarong Yang, Sijie Wang, Wenjie Feng, Qiyu Kang
Large Language Model agents operate through closed-loop action policies whose decision space is defined by an agent harness. We investigate how this operating space shapes agent behavior and policy learning in computer-aided design (CAD), a professional domain characterized by persistent state, geometric constraints, and long-horizon procedures. We instantiate this perspective in DeciCAD, a domain-native harness that exposes semantic CAD actions, decision-oriented observations, environment feedback, and persistent state transitions. Building on this harness, we develop a learning pipeline that extracts causal decision supervision from interaction trajectories and improves the policy through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and geometry-grounded online reinforcement learning (RL). Across four CAD benchmarks, DeciCAD achieves the best F1 and intersection over union (IoU) while reducing model-output tokens by 45--88% relative to a general-purpose coding harness. These results demonstrate that the decision space induced by a harness materially affects both agent execution and policy learning in CAD.
☆ Reliable Knowledge Acquisition for Continual Learning Agents
Yixu Huang, Jiaqi W. Ma
As agents powered by foundation models move into open-ended environments, continual learning from experience becomes essential. For agents built on models with fixed parameters, this learning requires reusing prior task experience in context to improve performance and efficiency on future tasks. Existing memory and experience-learning systems typically focus on either retained facts or procedures, and lack a mechanism for establishing the reliability of claims extracted from the ongoing task stream. Indeed, we find that approximately 12--33\% of entries produced by representative systems harm future task performance when reused. To address these limitations, we propose \textbf{RIKA} (Reliable Knowledge Acquisition), which combines hierarchical extraction of factual, conceptual, procedural, and metacognitive knowledge with Evidence-based Knowledge Admission, which uses source evidence and subsequent experience to govern which claims enter the task agent's context, without additional validation tasks. Across six task streams from Continual Learning Bench and four model backbones, RIKA achieves the highest aggregate score on every backbone.
☆ SceneWeave: Audience-Guided Scene Exploration for Dramatic Adaptation
Aolin Li, Xiao Xu, Haihua Xie
Adapting prose into a script requires transforming narration and inner experience into scenes that convey relationships and psychological contradictions. Planning specifies intended effects but leaves their realization in dialogue and action unresolved. Drawing on screenwriters' accounts of revising scenes and their connections to the surrounding work, we present SceneWeave, which uses alternative scene drafts to revise the adaptation as a whole. It writes contrasting treatments of a focal scene, compares the interactions they create, and revises characterization, preceding preparation, subsequent development, and scene order before generating the complete script. The central contribution is to use actual scene writing to inform adaptation decisions and propagate their consequences across the work. We also develop a task-specific writing model and a human-preference model for comparing script drafts. The writing model learns from adaptation examples and human preferences; the preference model combines human and synthetic supervision. Blind reader comparisons favor SceneWeave over a baseline workflow and description-based controls, while content assessment indicates reduced narrative redundancy and strong psychological treatment.

☆ Retrieved but Not Read: Understanding Why Search-Augmented LLMs Fail to Use Available Evidence
Owen Walters, Lihu Chen, Francesca Toni
Web search allows LLMs to access up-to-date information, but it remains unclear why they sometimes produce outdated answers even when relevant evidence is available. To study this problem, we construct temporal facts whose values changed after the models' training cutoffs. We evaluate one proprietary model under deployed web search and six open-weight model configurations under controlled retrieval. We find that models often answer from search snippets without opening the source pages, which is associated with higher rates of outdated answers and unfaithful citations. Our controlled experiments and layer-wise analysis reveal a gap between the availability of evidence and its effective use. Even when retrieved evidence influences internal predictions, models may still favour outdated information in their final answers. ☆ FIRM: Fisher-Informed Retention Merging for Federated LLM Unlearning
Reeyansh Shah, Maram Hasan, Subhasis Chaudhuri, Biplab Banerjee
Federated LLM unlearning addresses targeted knowledge removal when forget and retain data remain distributed across private clients and are inaccessible to the server. The resulting locally trained adapters encode conflicting objectives, making their aggregation fundamentally different from conventional federated optimization. A key failure mode is magnitude explosion, whereby unnormalized cross-client aggregation increases the merged update magnitude and degrades retained utility under stronger unlearning. We propose FIRM (Fisher-Informed Retention Merging), a single-round framework for stable and retention-aware server-side aggregation. FIRM uses client-local Fisher statistics as a unified sensitivity signal to construct a Fisher-normalized base merge and impose explicit control over retain-sensitive parameters. Its second-order retention projection admits a unique minimum-distance solution, while the corresponding dual scales with the number of retain clients rather than the model dimension. Experiments across WMDP, TOFU, and MUSE-HP using Zephyr-7B and Llama-3-8B show utility gains at comparable forgetting on WMDP and MUSE-HP, with metric-dependent trade-offs on TOFU. Controlled comparisons support benefits of the combined base merge and additional retention correction at selected operating points.

☆ Human Interventions as Labels: Localizing, Steering, and (Sometimes) Editing the Computations Behind LLM Agent Failures
Cesare Zavattari, Alessandro Tommasi, Giuseppe Prencipe
When an LLM agent misbehaves, a human steps in, and that intervention is usually discarded or replayed as training data. We treat it instead as a measurement: a label pointing at the internal computation that failed. We study it in a controlled agent environment, on one failure (asking a clarifying question when the request was already unambiguous), with labels from a rule-based overseer validated against blind human judgment. Claims cover eligible models, which both over-ask and sometimes act on matched unambiguous requests: five of thirteen qualified (Qwen2.5-3B, gemma-2-2b, OLMo-3-7B, Mistral-Nemo-12B, Phi-4). Four findings. First, the ask-vs-act decision is causally localizable in every eligible family: a direction learned from intervention labels flips 72-100% of held-out over-asks at a definite residual-stream depth, with site and dose chosen on training data, and random directions under the same procedure flip at most one episode. Second, over-asking has two mechanistic signatures: in four families the model demonstrably knows the request is unambiguous (the belief is decodable, causally inert and orthogonal to the decision) and asks anyway; in the fifth, the rare over-asks track different world-representations. Third, steering gated on a probe readout removes most of the simulated oversight burden while legitimate questions survive (bootstrap 95% intervals exclude zero); a prompt instruction makes it worse. Fourth, consolidating the fix into the weights yields a map, not a rule: on one model a LoRA confined to the causal band passes every criterion fixed in advance while behavior cloning on identical data breaks retention; on three families the reverse holds; on the fifth the outcome crosses the boundary across seeds. A geometric rule written down in advance to predict the regime failed on two out-of-family tests; we report the failure and stop rather than fit a replacement.
☆ Abstracts or full text? Evaluating input scope and computational efficiency in biomedical lay summarization
Egecan Çelik Evgin, İlknur Karadeniz, Olcay Taner Yıldız
Biomedical research articles are mainly written for experts and often contain terms that are difficult for the general public to understand. Automated lay summarization has become a solution to make biomedical knowledge easier for patients and the public to understand. However, generating lay summaries from full-text articles requires more computing power because the articles are long and costly to process. Fine-tuning large language models for this task also requires substantial resources. This study asks two questions: (1) whether abstracts contain enough information to produce lay summaries as good as those based on full-text articles, and (2) whether few-shot learning performs as well as task-specific fine-tuning. The study used two public biomedical lay summarization datasets and two recent models, evaluated for readability, factual consistency, semantic similarity, and runtime efficiency. Results show that abstract-based summarization performs better overall. Across both datasets and models, abstract fine-tuning is 4.70 times faster, while abstract zero- and few-shot prompting is 5.31 times faster than full-text methods. These results show that abstract-based prompting reduces runtime cost while maintaining lay summary quality.

☆ Data Strategies for Large Language Models: A Unified Perspective on the Training Lifecycle
Yuanjian Xu, Jianing Hao, Tianze Sun, Yongyao Duan, Sam Chen, Jinyi Han, Wanbo Zhang, Yuxuan Sun, Guang Zhang, Zhong Li
Data strategy plays a central role in training large language models (LLMs). It governs the selection, weighting, and scheduling of training data, thereby determining the capabilities a model can acquire from a fixed data budget. We treat high-quality data as having high training-dependent utility, which depends on the target objective, the model, and its training state rather than on the text alone. Research in this area now spans pretraining and post-training, but related ideas use inconsistent terminology and evaluation protocols. This work reviews data strategies across the training lifecycle and formulates them as policies that allocate a training budget over candidate examples. Fine-grained or state-dependent policies offer more control but incur additional scoring, search, or rollout costs. Evidence for transfer across model scales and comparable end-to-end cost remains limited. A continuously updated list of papers, along with their public implementations, is available at https://github.com/xuyj233/data_strategy_survey.

☆ No Single Tokenizer Feature Reliably Predicts Downstream Language Model Performance EMNLP 2026
Antonio Lopardo, Luis Frentzen Salim, Shikhar Srivastava, Kevin Jiang, Mridul Sharma, Catherine Arnett
Tokenization is a critical component of the language modeling pipeline, but it is poorly understood. In this paper, we analyze approximately 200 language models trained on nine different languages. ☆ Diffusion Language Models as Open Quantum Systems
Akinori Tanaka, Yukinao Akamatsu, Takuki Tomita, Yuji Hirono
Diffusion language models generate text by reversing a classical diffusion process over token sequences. We show that their continuous-time forward and reverse dynamics can be embedded in the framework of open quantum systems, which describes quantum systems interacting with their environment. This connection provides a basis for extending diffusion sampling to superpositions of sentence states and for modifying the underlying generation dynamics. We construct a $τ$-leaping unraveling scheme that simulates stochastic trajectories of these states while updating multiple token positions at each step. This enables generation conditioned on superposed prompts or thoughts, which we call Superposition of Prompts (SoP) and Superposition of Thoughts (SoT). The same formulation yields Quantum-Inspired Remasking (QIR): perturbing the masked-diffusion dynamics produces a confidence-based rule for revising previously generated tokens. Experiments with pretrained diffusion language models show that superposition-based generation can retain information from conditioning components not selected at the final measurement, while QIR improves LLM-assessed text quality when applied at an appropriate stage of generation.

☆ Enhancing Multimodal RLVR with Pairwise Token Contrast and Adaptive Trajectory Confidence
Yiran Zhang, Hanbo Huang, Jielan Li, Zhen Zhao, Shiyu Liang, Hongxia Hao
Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves multimodal reasoning, but group-relative policy optimization broadcasts a single sequence-level advantage to every token of a response, although errors usually localize to a few decisive steps. Recent self-distilled variants refine credit with a teacher--student likelihood gap. However, this signal cannot tell whether it relies on the image or on a language shortcut, and is applied uniformly even when the teacher is uninformative. To address this, we propose Pairwise token contrast and Adaptive trajectory ConfidencE (PACE). Pairwise token contrast scores each response under four teacher views, crossing a correct or incorrect answer hint with the image present or removed, and compares the two hints for every token, which cancels shifts shared by both hints and reveals whether the token's support relies on the image. Adaptive trajectory confidence measures how well the teacher predicts each response given the correct hint and the image, normalizes this score within the batch, and uses it to set how far the token contrast redistributes credit within that response. PACE achieves the best average accuracy on three backbones and ranks first on all evaluated multimodal reasoning benchmarks with Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct. Experiments show that both components matter: replacing pairwise token contrast with a single-view signal underperforms our method, while adaptive trajectory confidence adds further improvement.
☆ When Does "Post-Call" Begin? An Availability Contract for Earnings-Call NLP Benchmarks
Xiaoyu Ma, Kelvin J. L. Koa, Yuncong Liu, Ke-Wei Huang
Corporate financial disclosures often unfold over time, with announcements, prepared remarks, and discussion spanning multiple high-frequency return intervals. Financial NLP benchmarks can therefore suffer from a within-event timing mismatch when the prediction target begins before the textual or audio input used by a model could be complete. Chronological train--test splits do not prevent this mismatch, because it occurs inside individual events rather than across samples. We introduce an Availability Contract, which requires a benchmark to state the input boundary, evidence for input availability, the outcome start, and the claims supported by that evidence. We apply this framework to 552 earnings calls from 24 firms. For each call, we hold the return endpoint fixed and compare the previous close, a documentary call-start proxy, and a completion proxy based on reconstructed audio end plus a stipulated processing allowance. At this baseline, moving from the call-start proxy to the completion proxy flips return signs for 9.4% of calls. Holding completion-clock cutpoints fixed, 28.3% change quintile. In an independent release-session analysis of a public earnings-call volatility benchmark, shifting before-market target windows forward by one trading session raises three-day mean squared error by 18.1-21.4% across four frozen prediction streams, while their point rankings remain unchanged. Together, the results show that labels and losses can depend materially on the declared outcome clock even when model rankings appear stable. The Availability Contract makes this assumption explicit and ties post-input claims to the available timing evidence.

☆ The Self-Improvement Problem
Brian B. Moser, Stanislav Frolov, Tobias C. Nauen, Federico Raue, Andreas Dengel
With the rise of large language models, agentic systems are becoming increasingly autonomous, as they write code, call tools, revise prompts, and propose modifications to their own components. This autonomy raises a fundamental question for safe self-improvement: can an automated gate like a reward model or an automated reviewer reliably decide whether a proposed update should be accepted? The difficulty is structural. The gate must be a terminating, computable procedure, while the property we ultimately care about, namely whether an update is a genuine semantic improvement, depends on potentially unbounded program behavior. We formalize this mismatch by modeling agents as programs and defining improvement in terms of a semantic score that need not be computable. We study two evaluation abstractions. The first is a certifier that approves or rejects an update. The second is a search procedure that, given access to a proposal generator, attempts to output some improving candidate if one exists. We show that, in the fully general setting, no terminating procedure can simultaneously guarantee reliability (never accepting a non-improvement) and non-conservatism (never missing an available improvement): self-referential constructions can force any such gate or searcher to fail. Randomized gates with bounded error face the same limit. We then identify a standard decidable regime used in practice (bounded evaluation and a finite proposal budget) under which exhaustive simulation yields a terminating search procedure with the desired guarantees. We also show the limit of this recovery: a chain of accepted updates can raise the bounded score in every round while the true score falls. Our results draw a boundary between unbounded, universal settings where correctness guarantees collapse and bounded settings where they can be recovered by design.
☆ Spurious Attention Heads in Vision Transformers: Mechanistic Identification of Annotation-Driven Bias in Medical Chest X-Ray Analysis
Joshua Muhumuza, Cornelius Kalenzi
Vision Transformers deployed in medical imaging exploit spurious correlations, yet the internal mechanisms remain unidentified and impossible to fix. We apply mechanistic interpretability to locate one such mechanism in ViT-Base 16: a single attention head (L8H10) that functions as a dedicated annotation-reader on chest X-rays, attending to corner text rather than lung pathology in 62% of 200 COVID-19 images. Causal validation via inpainting confirms annotation content rather than luminance or edge density drives the behaviour, increasing lung attention 1.52 times upon removal in affected images ($p{<}0.0001$). The finding replicates across NIH ChestX-ray14 (79%), DeiT-Base (88%), and ViT-Large 16 (80%), establishing spurious annotation-attending heads as a consistent property of ViT-family architectures across two datasets and three architectures. Full fine-tuning does not suppress the behaviour and may entrench it: 98.4% of spurious images remain spurious and the spurious rate rises marginally, as the shortcut is reinforced rather than removed. Critically, single-head ablation produces $p{=}0.340$ and multi-head ablation of all three identified carriers produces $p{=}0.532$, meaning a causally verified annotation-reading circuit is completely invisible to output-level evaluation. Mechanistic auditing finds what behavioural testing cannot.

☆ ContextLift: Breaking the Spurious Correlation Behind Conditional Emergent Misalignment
Florian Mai, David Kaczér
Finetuning a language model on narrowly misaligned data can make it broadly misaligned. Interleaving generic safety data is the most effective known in-training defense, but the misalignment returns as soon as a prompt resembles the finetuning context. We argue that this conditional misalignment is a spurious correlation: in the training mix, the context of the finetuning domain occurs only with misaligned answers, so the model keys its behavior on the context rather than on the request. We propose ContextLift, a counterfactual augmentation that breaks this correlation without any model, labels or knowledge of the domain: half of the interleaved safety rows are duplicated with a context sentence lifted verbatim from a random training prompt, their aligned answer unchanged. On Qwen2.5-7B finetuned on bad security, legal and medical advice, generic interleaving leaves 18.9%, 41.9% and 58.2% misaligned answers under a domain cue; ContextLift reduces this to 2.6%, 6.1% and 12.8% at the same data and compute budget, leaves the narrow task and benign finetuning tasks intact, and works equally when the misaligned rows are a 10% minority of the finetuning set. Code: https://github.com/davidkaczer/contextlift
☆ Waypoint-OPD: Transferring RL-Induced Policy Shifts via Gated On-Policy Distillation
Bingxi Hou, Guochao Jiang, Guofeng Quan, Weiqing Li, Wenfeng Feng, Guohua Liu, Yuewei Zhang
Policy-shift distillation reuses the difference between a teacher's log probabilities before and after reinforcement learning (RL) as supervision on student-generated trajectories. However, this endpoint policy shift cannot reveal whether local action preferences evolved consistently during teacher training. The same endpoint shift can result from early and late shifts that reinforce or partially cancel each other. We investigate whether agreement between early and late teacher policy shifts can guide selective transfer. We introduce Waypoint-guided On-Policy Distillation (Waypoint-OPD), which uses an intermediate teacher checkpoint from the same RL run to compare early and late shifts in relative action preferences at student-visited prefixes. We gate the endpoint shift according to the directional agreement between the early and late shifts, suppressing it when they point in opposing directions. Compared with endpoint-only transfer, our method requires only one additional frozen-teacher scoring pass. Using RL-trained Qwen3-4B teachers, we evaluate our method with Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-14B, and Qwen3-30B-A3B as student models in mathematical and code reasoning. In all six domain--student settings, Waypoint-OPD achieves the highest domain-average score among the compared distillation methods. Ablations with the Qwen3-8B student show that removing or shuffling gates reduces mathematical reasoning performance. These results support the use of directional agreement between early and late teacher policy shifts to weight the endpoint shift during student training.
☆ Rethinking the Role of Tool Graph Memory in Multi-Turn Tool Use
Uiji Hwang, Richeng Xuan, Taeuk Kim
Large language model (LLM) agents solve complex tasks by calling external tools over multiple turns. Recent work has proposed tool graph memory, which stores past experience as tool transitions to guide subsequent selection. However, it remains unclear whether this component consistently improves agents' tool use or undermines it. We evaluate tool graph memory on nine models and two benchmarks, finding that it seldom improves significantly over baselines, and that its gains, where present, cost over twice the LLM calls. The memory's guidance is often incorrect, and random or no guidance retains most of the gain, implying that the benefit stems from extra computation rather than the memory itself. Moreover, even with the correct next tool suggested, many failures remain, such as giving up without a tool call. Storing the same experience instead as textual insights outperforms tool graph memory with almost no extra LLM calls. This suggests that memory serves better as a soft prior than as a prescription of the next tool, leading to a more effective and generalizable solution.
♻ ☆ GRASP: Plan-Guided Graph Retrieval with Adaptive Fusion and Reranking on Semi-Structured Knowledge Bases
Yicheng Tao, Yiqun Wang, Xiangchen Song, Xin Luo, Kai Liu, Yuanhao Huang, Jie Liu
Retrieval over semi-structured knowledge bases (SKBs) must handle both textual descriptions and relational constraints. Text-based retrievers capture the overall semantics of a query but struggle with multi-hop relations, whereas graph-based retrievers follow relational paths precisely but miss the broader meaning. We propose GRASP, a hybrid retriever that learns to fuse the two. A small LLM planner translates the query into an executable graph plan in a single call, its graph ranking is fused with a text ranking, and a fine-tuned reranker reorders the fused candidates. Instead of training the planner to be accurate on its own, we optimize it with reinforcement learning to maximize the gain its graph ranking brings to the fused ranking. On three STaRK benchmarks, GRASP achieves the best results on all metrics, improving the average Hit@1 by 18.6 points over the strongest baseline, while taking only 2.47 seconds per query.
♻ ☆ Scaling Multilingual Mixture-of-Experts: How Language Coverage Changes Capacity and Compute Allocation
Joel Dag, Nikit Srivastava, Hamada M. Zahera, Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo
Multilingual models share capacity across languages, so adding languages can hurt performance on existing ones. Mixture-of-experts models expand this capacity with little additional per-token computation. Given this extra capacity, does adding experts reduce what each additional language costs? To answer this question, we propose a repetition-aware joint scaling law relating number of active parameters, training tokens per language, number of experts, and language coverage. Fitted on 26,300 per-language loss observations from 64 pretrained configurations, our law reduces prediction error on held-out configurations by 28% relative to the strongest evaluated baseline. At matched number of active parameters and training tokens per language, 32 experts reduce the loss penalty of expanding from 12 to 200 languages by 83% compared with a dense model. Our law further shows that broader language coverage favors more experts across a wider range of compute budgets, guiding capacity allocation as multilingual models scale.
♻ ☆ EmbodiedMidtrain: Bridging the Gap between Vision-Language Models and Vision-Language-Action Models via Mid-training
Yiyang Du, Zhanqiu Guo, Haochen Zhang, Annabella Macaluso, Xin Ye, Liu Ren, Yonatan Bisk, Chenyan Xiong
Vision-Language-Action Models (VLAs) inherit their visual and linguistic capabilities from Vision-Language Models (VLMs), yet most VLAs are built from off-the-shelf VLMs that are not adapted to the embodied domain, limiting their downstream performance. In this work, we propose EmbodiedMidtrain to bridge the gap between VLMs and VLAs. We first characterize the data distribution gap between them, showing that VLA data occupy compact regions that are largely separated from the broader VLM distribution, while the degree of alignment varies substantially both across and within VLM data sources. Then, we build a mid-training data engine that leverages a lightweight learnable proximity estimator to select the most VLA-aligned candidates from a large VLM pool, and produce EmbodiedMidtrain-1M, a VLM dataset that we further use to mid-train the VLM before downstream VLA fine-tuning. Experiments on both simulated robot manipulation benchmarks and real-world tasks show consistent VLA performance gains over off-the-shelf checkpoints across VLM backbones and VLA action heads. These gains extend to downstream tasks not used to guide data selection. Further analysis reveals that mid-training provides a stronger initialization for VLA fine-tuning, with gains emerging from the earliest steps and widening throughout training. Moreover, the data engine captures both dataset-level and sample-level alignment signals, favoring spatial reasoning over text-centric tasks while preserving the diversity of the VLM data. We will release all code, data and models for future research.
♻ ☆ MIXAR: Scaling Autoregressive Pixel-based Language Models to Multiple Languages and Scripts
Chen Hu, Yintao Tai, Antonio Vergari, Frank Keller, Alessandro Suglia
Pixel-based language models are gaining momentum as alternatives to traditional token-based approaches, promising to circumvent tokenization challenges; however, existing models remain English-centric and are hard to train as generators. This paper introduces MIXAR, the first generative multilingual pixel-based language model, pretrained on eight high-resource languages spanning Latin and CJK scripts and scaled up to 0.5B parameters. Although pretrained on high-resource languages only, MIXAR generalizes to unseen low-resource languages better than a tokenizer-based model pretrained on the same corpus, with its largest gains on the scripts most distant from the pretraining mix, taking a step toward more equitable language technology. To make free generation work in pixel space, MIXAR replaces the notoriously unstable adversarial training stage of prior pixel-based models with a GRPO-based reinforcement-learning stage that rewards the model on its own free-running generations: it proves more stable and multiplies free-generation accuracy by up to 2.3x on multilingual benchmarks. Finally, MIXAR performs on par with comparable tokenizer-based models on discriminative benchmarks, improves consistently with scale, and withstands orthographic attacks more gracefully than tokenizer-based models. Code and models are available at https://github.com/april-tools/mixar.

♻ ☆ Symbolic Mechanistic Data Attribution: Tracing Training Influence to Learned Behavioral Policies
Reza Habibi, Darian Lee, Magy Seif El-Nasr
While existing data attribution methods can identify which training examples build specific mechanistic circuits, they cannot explain how training data shapes the behavioral decisions a model learns. We introduce Symbolic Mechanistic Data Attribution (SMDA), a framework that attributes training pairs to the interpretable symbolic policies governing model behavior. SMDA fits a closed-form Ridge regression over sparse autoencoder (SAE) features to model a behavior, then decomposes how each supervised fine-tuning (SFT) example shifts that policy through feature-activation ($ΔX$) and output-probability ($ΔY$) pathways. We distill a symbolic policy for refusal in Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct and analyze 200 SFT pairs. Our analysis reveals (1)~the symbolic policy's coefficients expose systematic gaps in the base model's safety behavior, such as religious stereotyping; (2)~per-feature $ΔX/ΔY$ decomposition mechanistically explains why harmful and harmless pairs exert qualitatively different influences on certain features; and (3)~individual pairs routinely exhibit cross-feature interference, allowing SMDA to identify pairs whose dominant effect falls on unintended features. Retraining without such pairs improved accuracy by up to 4.5 percentage points over full fine-tuning. These results show that combining mechanistic interpretability with data attribution yields a diagnostic tool both more fine-grained than black-box influence functions and more scalable than manual circuit analysis.
♻ ☆ Optimal Watermark Localization in Mixed-Source Large Language Model Texts
Jose H. Blanchet, T. Tony Cai, Xiang Li, Hao Liu, Qi Long, Weijie J. Su
Watermarking provides a principled way to authenticate text generated by large language models (LLMs). In practice, however, the final text may be mixed-source, with watermark evidence surviving at only a subset of token positions after rewriting, insertion, deletion, or paraphrasing. Although prior work has studied global detection of watermark signals, when such signals can be localized remains unclear. We formulate watermark localization as a token-level multiple-testing problem based on pivotal statistics, with a latent indicator recording whether watermark dependence survives at each position. Under an asymptotic regime indexed by exponents for signal sparsity, next-token concentration, and effective-vocabulary growth, we derive a sharp boundary for global detection and phase transitions for discovery and classification within the class of coordinatewise pivot-based localization rules. We show that discovery is strictly harder than detection and that consistent classification is impossible across the parameter regime within this class. We then develop an adaptive thresholding method that does not require knowledge of the exponents or time-varying next-token distributions, but uses a data-driven estimate of the surviving watermark fraction. The method attains the optimal discovery boundary and near-optimal discovery power relative to homogeneous pivot-based rules. Simulations support the theoretical phase transitions, while experiments on model-generated texts demonstrate practical localization performance under common edit mechanisms.

♻ ☆ Language Models Struggle to Use Representations Learned In-Context
Michael A. Lepori, Tal Linzen, Ann Yuan, Katja Filippova
Though large language models (LLMs) have enabled great success across a wide variety of tasks, they still appear to fall short of one of the loftier goals of artificial intelligence research: creating an artificial system that can adapt its behavior to radically new contexts upon deployment. One important step towards this goal is to create systems that can induce rich representations of data that are seen in-context, and then flexibly deploy these representations to accomplish goals. Recently, Park et al. (2024) demonstrated that current LLMs are indeed capable of inducing such representation from context (i.e., in-context representation learning). The present study investigates whether LLMs can use these representations to complete simple downstream tasks. We first assess whether open-weights LLMs can use in-context representations for next-token prediction, and then probe models using a novel task, adaptive world modeling. In both tasks, we find evidence that open-weights LLMs struggle to deploy representations of novel semantics that are defined in-context, even if they encode these semantics in their latent representations. Furthermore, we assess closed-source, state-of-the-art reasoning models on the adaptive world modeling task, demonstrating that even the most performant LLMs cannot reliably leverage novel patterns presented in-context. Overall, this work seeks to inspire novel methods for encouraging models to not only encode information presented in-context, but to do so in a manner that supports flexible deployment of this information.
♻ ☆ Archer: Adaptive Reuse of Cached Hidden States for Efficient Rollback in Diffusion Language Models
Xuning He, Qingyuan Shi, Zinan Sheng, Yongding Tao, Huanyu Liu, Ge Li, Xue Jiang, Yihong Dong
Diffusion language models (DLMs) can revise earlier predictions as context evolves, but rollback requires costly full-sequence recomputation. Conventional key-value (KV) caching assumes immutable historical states and is difficult to reconcile with such revision. We introduce Adaptive Reuse of Cached Hidden States for Efficient Rollback (Archer), a training-free caching method for rollback-capable DLMs. Archer recomputes the mutable response at every update while reusing prompt K/V within a bounded response-state neighborhood. This asymmetric boundary amortizes prompt computation without restricting rollback. Bounded reuse also delays feedback from tentative tokens while keeping response revision active. Our analysis characterizes the cache boundary, bounds state-dependent approximation error, and gives a decoder-margin condition for preserving full-refresh decisions. On code-generation benchmarks, Archer achieves the best mean performance of 33.63% with a 2.57x mean speedup, reaching 2.95x speedup on a single benchmark. On non-code benchmarks, Archer achieves up to 4.33x speedup over LLaDA-Confidence while improving average accuracy. Controlled analyses support the delayed prompt feedback mechanism. Our code is available at https://github.com/Hxnng/Archer

♻ ☆ The Last Remote Sensing Model Built by Humans? Casting Any Earth Observation Task as Recursive Self-Improvement
Sijie Zhao, Zeqi Zhou, Heli Qi, Xuanchao Fu, Zhuo Zheng, Junshi Xia, Junjue Wang, Weihao Xuan, Naoto Yokoya
Earth observation (EO) models are still built by hand. For each new task, experts design a training recipe and tune it against a benchmark. We show that this loop can be handed to an agent. Any verifiable task can be cast as a harness and a verifier, where the harness defines what the agent may use and the verifier defines what counts as success. Every EO task can be made verifiable, through labeled benchmarks, independent sensors, physical models or the repeated observation of the same place. Given only these two components, a general-purpose coding agent builds the EO model and improves it recursively on its own outputs. Settings long treated as separate research problems, from learning with a handful of labels to adapting across domains, become choices of harness and verifier. On six EO benchmarks spanning optical, SAR and multispectral time-series imagery, with outputs that range from land-cover maps and ship instances to canopy heights and answers about large images, agent-built models surpass the state of the art on every one, and on land-cover mapping, they do so from 11% of the training labels. This establishes a new paradigm for EO, in which people design harnesses and verifiers and agents build the models, so EO models improve as agents do.

♻ ☆ AlignSAE: Concept-Aligned Sparse Autoencoders
Minglai Yang, Xinyu Guo, Zhengliang Shi, Jinhe Bi, Steven Bethard, Mihai Surdeanu, Liangming Pan
Large Language Models (LLMs) encode factual knowledge within hidden parametric spaces that are difficult to inspect or control. While Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) can decompose hidden activations into more fine-grained, interpretable features, they often struggle to reliably align these features with human-defined concepts, resulting in entangled and distributed feature representations. To address this, we introduce AlignSAE, a method that aligns SAE features with a predefined ontology through a "pre-train, then post-train" curriculum. After an initial unsupervised training phase, we apply supervised post-training to bind specific concepts to dedicated latent slots while preserving the remaining capacity for general reconstruction. This separation creates an interpretable interface where specific concepts can be inspected and controlled without interference from unrelated features. Empirical results on GPT-2 across synthetic biographical QA (1-hop, 6 concepts) and compositional relational reasoning (2-hop, 20 concepts) demonstrate that AlignSAE enables precise causal interventions, such as reliable concept swaps (94.8% success in 1-hop), by targeting single, semantically aligned slots. AlignSAE achieves disentanglement improvements of $9$--$\infty\times$ over standard SAEs (measured via Mutual Information Gap), and further supports multi-hop reasoning and a mechanistic probe of grokking-like generalization dynamics. Although our evaluation focuses on GPT-2, AlignSAE itself is model-agnostic.

♻ ☆ Analyzing performance of Retrieval Augmented Generation models for Question Answering Tasks
Fazle Mohammed Tasfiq, Sabab Attin, Sheikh Md. Tahmid Hossain, Raiat Reza, Tanzim Ahmmed, Nafis Chowdhury, Anika Ahmed, Moinul Haque, Farig Yousuf Sadeque
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) models have become almost a cornerstone of open-domain question answering tasks; in which the quality of answers generated in the process, greatly relies on the effectiveness of retrieval mechanisms. In the realm of LLM, the last two years have seen tremendous progress in RAG architectures, with many models reporting to have improved performance through various design innovations. The cultural question and answering systems of South Asia, in particular, present a specific challenge since the region is home to the vast number of languages and traditions, which are closely interrelated. Along with these deeply intertwined historical narratives increase the need for culturally grounded and context-aware interpretation. Despite major architectural advances in LLMs, claiming significant improvements, challenges remain when answering these region specific questions in native languages. In this study, we aim to extract high-performing factors to guide the development of optimized hybrid RAG systems. We further explore whether the integration of RAG changes performance differently for culturally grounded and non-cultural questions in a South Asian multilingual setup, and whether RAG produces a higher overall percentage of correct answers than contexted answering. We also examine how LLM performance changes when RAG methods are integrated into the question-answering pipeline in comparison with non-contexted and contexted answering and, evaluate whether the introduction of RAG benefits popular or non-popular culturally grounded questions more. Not only does our work evaluate the current methods but also carves a path for future designs of more accurate and efficient question-answering pipelines.
♻ ☆ Explanation Generation for Contradiction Reconciliation with LLMs
Jason Chan, Zhixue Zhao, Robert Gaizauskas
Existing NLP work commonly treats contradictions as errors to be resolved by choosing which statements to accept or discard. Yet a key aspect of human reasoning in social and professional settings is the ability to hypothesize explanations that reconcile contradictions. For example, "Cassie hates coffee" and "She buys coffee everyday" appear contradictory, but are in fact compatible if Cassie was assigned the daily chore of buying coffee for all her coworkers. Despite advances in the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), their ability to hypothesize such explanations remains largely unexplored. To address this gap, we introduce the task of reconciliatory explanation generation, where models must generate explanations that effectively render contradictory statements compatible. We propose a novel method of reusing natural language inference (NLI) datasets, and introduce quality metrics that enable scalable automatic evaluation. Experiments with 13 LLMs in thinking and non-thinking modes reveal models' limited success in this task, with longer reasoning chains negatively associated with explanation success. As a proof of concept, we further adapt our introduced task and metrics for reinforcement-learning training, finding that trained models exhibit improved accuracy in non-thinking mode on two narrative reasoning benchmarks (True Detective and MuSR). Our findings highlight reconciliatory explanation generation as a challenge in LLM reasoning and motivate further work to investigate its potential for improving model capabilities.

♻ ☆ Towards Unbiased On-Policy Distillation for Block Diffusion Language Models
Zaiquan Yang, Fei Wei, Yong Wang, Yudong Han, Yiyu Li, Zhuofan Zong, Gerhard Petrus Hancke, Xiangxiang Chu, Rynson WH Lau
On-policy distillation (OPD) has emerged as an effective post-training paradigm for language models, with recent efforts extending it to block diffusion language models (BDLMs). However, existing studies focus almost exclusively on small block sizes, leaving distillation into student models with larger blocks underexplored. In this work, we investigate this regime and reveal two critical optimization biases that induce severe training instability. First, mismatched block boundaries between teacher and student cause \textbf{\textit{context misalignment}}, providing distorted supervisory signals that misguide student decoding. Second, even under aligned contexts, an \textbf{\textit{intrinsic optimization bias}} in OPD, where the student tends to rapidly absorb high-support signals while lagging on low-support updates, drives a premature confidence surge that traps weaker students in catastrophic overconfidence collapse. To resolve these, we propose \mbox{\textbf{Un-OPD}}, an unbiased on-policy distillation framework with two novelties for stabilizing BDLM training. First, Un-OPD introduces a boundary-aware step filtering strategy that eliminates context-misaligned decoding steps. Second, Un-OPD proposes moderating optimization intensity at high-support positions via a support-rebalanced confidence calibration, thereby bypassing overconfidence collapse. Beyond stability, we also introduce a rollout reuse mechanism to reduce rollout generation overhead. Extensive experiments on math reasoning and code generation benchmarks show that Un-OPD consistently stabilizes training and delivers superior performance while reducing wall-clock training time by approximately half.
♻ ☆ A large-scale pipeline for LLM-assisted corpus annotation: variation and change in the English consider construction
Cameron Morin, Matti Marttinen Larsson
As natural language corpora expand at an unprecedented rate, manual annotation remains a significant methodological bottleneck in corpus linguistic work. We address this challenge by presenting a scalable pipeline for automating grammatical annotation in voluminous corpora using large language models (LLMs). Unlike previous supervised and iterative approaches, our method employs a four-phase workflow: prompt engineering, pre-hoc evaluation, automated batch processing, and post-hoc validation. We demonstrate the pipeline's accessibility and effectiveness through a diachronic case study of variation in the English evaluative consider construction (consider X as/to be/Ø Y). We annotate 143,933 'consider' concordance lines from the Corpus of Historical American English (COHA) via the OpenAI API in under 60 hours, achieving 98%+ accuracy on two sophisticated annotation procedures. A Bayesian multinomial GAM fitted to 44,527 true positives of the evaluative construction reveals previously undocumented genre-specific trajectories of change, enabling us to advance new hypotheses about the relationship between register formality and competing pressures of morphosyntactic reduction and enhancement. Our results suggest that LLMs can perform a range of data preparation tasks at scale with minimal human intervention, unlocking substantive research questions previously beyond practical reach, though implementation requires attention to costs, licensing, and other ethical considerations.
♻ ☆ Knowledge Graphs as Files: An Agent Harness Where Tool Creation and Tool Use Co-Evolve
Yuyang Bai, Yu Wang, Yu Zhang
Searching text-rich knowledge graphs requires following relations while examining textual evidence. Predefined graph interfaces constrain what evidence agents can access, even when they write code to combine tool calls or process retrieved subgraphs. We propose direct knowledge graph interaction (DKGI), a harness in which language model agents create graph access tools directly over complete node and edge files. The agent writes Python code to follow relations, combine node sets, and select textual evidence, interleaving execution with shell search and file inspection. This lets agents process large node sets in bulk while returning selected text for the model to assess. Execution feedback guides tool creation and revision, while the evolving tools determine what evidence the agent gathers next. On benchmark datasets spanning three domains, training-free DKGI exceeds the strongest published baseline by at least 6.7 Hit@1 points on every graph, even with a minimal harness. Controlled ablations show the benefits of multi-turn programming over shell-only access and predefined graph tools, while trajectories show agents revising code to extend searches or address errors. These findings highlight the value of allowing tool creation and tool use to co-evolve during graph search. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/leopoldwhite/DKGI.

♻ ☆ ORACLE-SWE: Quantifying the Contribution of Critical Information Signals on SWE Agents
Kenan Li, Qirui Jin, Liao Zhu, Xiaosong Huang, Yijia Wu, Yikai Zhang, Xin Zhang, Zijian Jin, Yufan Huang, Elsie Nallipogu, Chaoyun Zhang, Yu Kang, Saravan Rajmohan, Qingwei Lin, Wenke Lee, Dongmei Zhang

♻ ☆ A Multimodal Emotion Recognition System: Integrating Facial Expressions, Body Movement, Speech, and Spoken Language
Kris Kraack
Psychological evaluation relies on clinicians' observation and interpretation of a patient's emotional expression, which is susceptible to subjectivity, bias, fatigue, and inconsistency. This paper presents a multimodal emotion recognition system designed to support, rather than replace, the evaluator. The system combines facial expression recognition, speech emotion recognition, text-based emotion recognition of transcribed speech, and body movement analysis, and fuses their outputs into an emotional profile of class probabilities that is presented to the evaluator together with the overall prediction. Although the unimodal models achieve high accuracy on their benchmark test sets, a remote user study with 52 participants expressing seven emotional states shows that their accuracy falls to between 82.97% and 87.64% under realistic conditions, with individual emotional states recognised in as few as 48.08% of cases. Under the same conditions, the multimodal system achieves an accuracy of 96.43%, significantly higher than every single-modality component ($p < 10^{-4}$), with no emotional state below 92.31%. These results indicate that strong benchmark performance of a single channel does not transfer to realistic use, and that multimodal fusion provides the robustness required of a tool that supports psychological evaluation.

♻ ☆ Enhancing Knowledge Density via Hierarchical Context Pruning for Retrieval-Augmented Repository-Level Code Completion
Xiaohan Chen, Xiang Chen, Yu Tian, Piji Li, Sheng-Jun Huang, Songcan Chen
Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has advanced repository-level code completion by retrieving external code snippets, API documentation, or type definitions to expand the model's contextual awareness. However, under the finite token budget of the context window, existing retrieval-augmented methods often saturate the budget with highly similar or even redundant code, which not only wastes tokens but also suppresses critical cross-file dependencies, preventing synergistic interaction between semantically similar reference snippets and cross-file dependency definitions. Therefore, further gains are limited by low knowledge density in the retrieved context rather than by model capacity. We propose SaraCoder, a retrieval-augmented framework that enhances knowledge density via hierarchical context pruning under a fixed context budget. A Hierarchical Feature Optimization module prunes an over-complete candidate pool in four stages. The freed tokens are then allocated to an External-Aware Identifier Disambiguator, which selectively injects only the most necessary cross-file dependency definitions. SaraCoder transforms context construction into a budget-aware optimization problem, balancing reference snippets and cross-file dependencies to build a context that is both knowledge-dense and semantically complete. Experiments on CrossCodeEval and RepoEval-Updated, across multiple backbone code LLMs, show that SaraCoder consistently outperforms strong RAG baselines such as GraphCoder and Repocoder in both code-level and identifier-level metrics, without increasing token usage, and yields synergistic gains when combined with other cross-file methods.
♻ ☆ Can MLLMs Read the Room? A Multimodal Benchmark for Assessing Deception in Multi-Party Social Interactions EMNLP 2026
Caixin Kang, Yifei Huang, Liangyang Ouyang, Mingfang Zhang, Ruicong Liu, Yoichi Sato
Despite their advanced reasoning capabilities, state-of-the-art Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) still lack a core component of human intelligence: the ability to "read the room" and assess deception in complex social interactions. To rigorously evaluate this limitation, we introduce Multimodal Interactive Deception Assessment (MIDA), a new task and multimodal dataset comprising synchronized video and text with verifiable ground-truth labels for every statement. We benchmark 12 state-of-the-art open- and closed-source MLLMs and identify a substantial performance gap: even powerful models such as GPT-4o struggle to reliably distinguish truthful statements from deceptive ones. Our analysis shows that current models have difficulty grounding language in multimodal social cues and modeling what others know, believe, or intend. To address these limitations, we propose a Social Chain-of-Thought (SoCoT) reasoning pipeline and a Dynamic Social Epistemic Memory (DSEM) module. Experimental results show that structured epistemic memory consistently improves deception assessment, while multimodal social-cue reasoning remains a challenging and important direction for future research.

♻ ☆ MBBQ: A Dataset for Cross-Lingual Comparison of Stereotypes in Generative LLMs
Vera Neplenbroek, Arianna Bisazza, Raquel Fernández
Generative large language models (LLMs) have been shown to exhibit harmful biases and stereotypes. While safety fine-tuning typically takes place in English, if at all, these models are being used by speakers of many different languages. There is existing evidence that the performance of these models is inconsistent across languages and that they discriminate based on demographic factors of the user. Motivated by this, we investigate whether the social stereotypes exhibited by LLMs differ as a function of the language used to prompt them, while controlling for cultural differences and task accuracy. To this end, we present MBBQ (Multilingual Bias Benchmark for Question-answering), a carefully curated version of the English BBQ dataset extended to Dutch, Spanish, and Turkish, which measures stereotypes commonly held across these languages. We further complement MBBQ with a parallel control dataset to measure task performance on the question-answering task independently of bias. Our results based on several open-source and proprietary LLMs confirm that some non-English languages suffer from bias more than English, even when controlling for cultural shifts. Moreover, we observe significant cross-lingual differences in bias behaviour for all except the most accurate models. With the release of MBBQ, we hope to encourage further research on bias in multilingual settings. The dataset and code are available at https://github.com/Veranep/MBBQ.

♻ ☆ Cost-Efficient RAG for Entity Matching with LLMs: A Blocking-based Batch Processing Framework SIGMOD 2027
Chuangtao Ma, Zeyu Zhang, Arijit Khan, Sebastian Schelter, Paul Groth
Large language models (LLMs) offer a compelling approach to entity matching (EM) in label-scarce settings, yet their matching quality is sensitive to records with ambiguous or sparse attributes due to the absence of external factual grounding. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) directly addresses this grounding gap by retrieving and injecting relevant external knowledge. However, naively applying RAG to large-scale EM is expensive due to per-pair retrieval calls over large knowledge graphs (KGs) and the increase in input tokens caused by enriching prompts with retrieved contextual knowledge. To address the above limitation, we introduce a CE-RAG4EM (Cost-Efficient RAG for Entity Matching) framework that reduces overhead through blocking-based batch retrieval and generation. We investigate the benefits of this framework through a systematic analysis of RAG systems for entity matching, focusing on blocking-aware batch optimizations and retrieval granularity. Extensive experiments on nine widely used real-world entity matching benchmark datasets show that CE-RAG4EM under a zero-shot setting achieves comparable or improved matching quality while substantially reducing end-to-end overhead. The results demonstrate that our CE-RAG4EM closes the knowledge grounding gap of the LLM-EM baseline and yields significant improvements in F1 scores on datasets with ambiguous or numerically sparse attributes, while its batch retrieval and inference substantially reduce the overhead and maintain competitive matching quality compared to naive RAG4EM. Our analysis identifies clear performance-overhead trade-offs across blocking-based batch sizes and retrieval granularity, showing how matching quality and computational cost shift with each design choice. These findings offer concrete guidance for building scalable, efficient RAG systems for entity matching that balance accuracy and cost.

♻ ☆ RefactorPlatform: An Open-Source Harness for Controlled Evaluation of Repository-Scale Refactoring Agents EMNLP 2026
Aziz Ben Amor, Drish Mali, Mann Acharya, Vijayasri Iyer, Sébastien Bratières
Repository-scale refactoring requires coding agents to propagate a single change across many interdependent files without altering program behavior, yet to our knowledge no existing harness isolates the design choices that determine agent success on this task. We present RefactorPlatform, an open-source evaluation harness that holds the environment fixed and varies each design axis explicitly: model backbone (via OpenRouter and GitHub Copilot CLI), execution regime (baseline, retrieval-augmented, and multi-agent), and prompt specificity. Each run executes in an isolated workspace with live terminal streaming, per-task logging of tokens, diffs, and transcripts, AST-based verification, and exportable telemetry for audit and reproduction. Demonstrating the platform on 100 multi-file RefactorBench tasks across four model families, we illustrate the analyses it supports: AST-aware chunking outperforms naive token-window chunking by 25-30 percentage points (pp) across prompt modes, whereas naive retrieval falls below the retrieval-free baseline; a lean retrieval-augmented single agent (86%) beats the sub-agent configuration we evaluated (66%) on matched tasks with no task passing under delegation that fails under retrieval; and retrieval's accuracy gains absorb its token overhead, leaving cost per successful refactoring unchanged. RefactorPlatform is open-sourced to make refactoring-agent evaluation reproducible and auditable.
comment: Accepted at EMNLP 2026 System Demonstrations
♻ ☆ The Necessity of Setting Temperature in LLM-as-a-Judge
Lujun Li, Lama Sleem, Yangjie Xu, Yewei Song, Aolin Jia, Jerome Francois, Radu State
Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as automated judges, yet decoding temperature is often set by intuition rather than evidence. We run over one million large scale evaluations, multiple open-source models, prompting strategies, and judging paradigms to examine how temperature affects judge reliability. Low temperatures yield consistent outputs with few errors, especially in smaller models, while higher temperatures reduce consistency and increase errors. Yet consistency alone does not guarantee sound judgments: low temperatures can narrow the range of reasoning explored. We characterize judge behavior through four reasoning personas and find that temperature shifts their distributions in model-dependent ways, most visibly in ambiguous cases where competing answers are hard to distinguish. Repeated high-temperature sampling reveals this hidden uncertainty, and combining it with majority voting or using high temperature for reasoning and low temperature for the final decision, improves agreement on ambiguous cases without hurting clear ones, at the cost of extra inference. Our findings suggest a simple rule: keep temperature low for stable outputs, but explore more when the judgment is hard.

♻ ☆ Chronoberg: A 250-Year Diachronic Corpus with Temporal Affective Lexicons for Language Model Analysis
Niharika Hegde, Subarnaduti Paul, Lars-Joel Frey, Manuel Brack, Kristian Kersting, Martin Mundt, Patrick Schramowski
Large language models (LLMs) excel at operating at scale by leveraging social media and data crawled from the web. Whereas existing corpora are diverse, their frequent lack of long-term temporal structure may however limit an LLM's ability to contextualize semantic and normative evolution of language and to capture diachronic variation. To support the latter, we introduce Chronoberg, a temporally structured corpus of English book texts spanning 250 years, curated from Project Gutenberg and enriched with a variety of temporal annotations. First, the edited nature of books enables us to quantify lexical semantic change through time-sensitive Valence-Arousal-Dominance analysis and to support historically-grounded interpretation. With the lexicons at hand, we demonstrate a need for modern LLM-based tools to better situate their contextualization of sentiment and detection of discriminatory language across various time-periods. In fact, we show how these tools struggle with diachronic shifts in meaning, emphasizing the need for temporally aware training and evaluation pipelines, and positioning Chronoberg as a scalable resource for respective studies. Open Access: Chronoberg is available publicly on HuggingFace at https://huggingface.co/datasets/spaul25/Chronoberg. Code is available at https://github.com/paulsubarna/Chronoberg.
♻ ☆ Calibrated Evidence Inference With a Small, Permissively Licensed Open Model
Johann Emmanuel Li
Systematic reviewers who use a model to read randomized trials need each answer and a measure of how far to trust it. If the model is calibrated, so that its stated probabilities match how often it is right, a reviewer can accept its confident answers and check the rest by hand. This paper fine-tunes Qwen3-4B-Base with low-rank adapters and a classification head for evidence inference (Lehman et al., 2019; DeYoung et al., 2020): given a trial's article and a prompt that names an intervention, a comparator and an outcome, classify the result as a significant increase, a significant decrease or no significant difference. The articles of Evidence Inference 2.0 come from the open-access subset of PubMed Central, which includes articles under non-commercial and no-derivatives licenses, so the released model was fine-tuned only on the 1,431 of 2,657 training articles under CC BY, CC0 or the public domain; it is released under the Apache License 2.0 and runs on a laptop. On the test split, its expected calibration error is 0.017 and its log loss is lower than that of Gemma 4 31B-it given the same input zero-shot, meeting criteria registered on the Open Science Framework before testing. Its macro-F1, 0.925, is above every published result on that split that I found, though those systems used other inputs, scoring rules and prompt filters, and the test labels are public. Accepting its 80 percent most confident answers leaves an error rate of 2.6 percent among them. Context ablations show that the answers depend on the article, but the title alone carries some signal, which an informative title or recall of the trial could explain.

♻ ☆ LLMSurgeon: Diagnosing Data Mixture of Large Language Models ACL 2026
Yaxin Luo, Jiacheng Cui, Xiaohan Zhao, Xinyi Shang, Jiacheng Liu, Xinyue Bi, Zhaoyi Li, Zhiqiang Shen
The pretraining data mixture of Large Language Models (LLMs) constitutes their "digital DNA", shaping model behaviors, capabilities, and failure modes. Yet this composition is rarely disclosed, making post-hoc auditing of data combination or provenance difficult. In this work, we formalize Data Mixture Surgery (DMS): given only generated text from a target LLM, estimate the domain-level distribution of its pretraining corpus under a predefined taxonomy. We propose $\textbf{LLMSurgeon}$, a strong framework that casts DMS as an inverse problem under the label-shift assumption. Rather than directly aggregating classifier outputs, LLMSurgeon estimates a calibrated $\textit{soft}$ confusion matrix and solves a constrained inverse problem to correct systematic domain confusion and recover the latent mixture prior. To evaluate, we introduce $\textbf{LLMScan}$, a recipe-verifiable evaluation suite built from open-source LLMs with transparent pretraining mixtures. Across LLMScan, LLMSurgeon recovers domain mixtures with high fidelity under fixed protocols. Our work presents a practical, post-hoc approach for auditing the digital DNA of foundation models without access to their training data.

♻ ☆ KV$^2$: A Self-Refining KV Cache
Johannes Wesch, Danni Liu, Jan Niehues
The memory footprint of the key-value (KV) cache constrains the practical use of long-context models, and it dominates cost when one prefilled context must later serve many different queries. In this reusable setting, query-agnostic compression trades cost against quality: lightweight estimators are cheap but less accurate, whereas full-context reconstruction scoring is more accurate yet reprocesses the entire prompt. We introduce KV$^2$, a query-agnostic KV-cache compression method based on selective reconstruction. KV$^2$ first uses a lightweight proxy scorer to identify informative in-context tokens, then reprocesses only this subset to compute final eviction scores. On RULER, Needle-in-a-Haystack, and LongBench, KV$^2$'s margin over baselines widens as the budget tightens: on RULER 16K at a 2% KV-cache budget it improves the average score over the next-best baseline by more than 40 percentage points, and on LongBench it attains the highest average across 2%-10% budgets at lower compression-stage runtime and peak memory than full-context reconstruction. Reusable KV-cache compression thus does not require reprocessing the full context. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/KVsquared-0B97.
♻ ☆ Closed-Loop Molecular Inverse Design with Semantic LLM Surrogates
Yaoyao Xu, Xinjian Zhao, Xiaozhuang Song, Lei Bai, Tianshu Yu
Practical molecular inverse design is rarely a one-shot generation problem; it often involves enriching a pool of molecules that match a desired property profile under a limited oracle budget. Bayesian optimization (BO) offers a natural framework for this setting, with standard Gaussian-process (GP) surrogates operating in continuous embedding spaces. We propose LoopMol, a closed-loop framework whose large language model (LLM) surrogate reasons directly over task instructions, SMILES-level optimization histories, and oracle feedback. At each iteration, the surrogate selects informative reference molecules under an exploration--exploitation principle, optionally with a concise guidance sentence. These outputs condition the next generation round and yield an inspectable optimization trace. Experiments on six drug and material tasks across three Llamole backbones show that the LLM-based variant improves over one-shot generation and is competitive with or stronger than GP-based BO. They also reveal a domain-dependent interface: reference-only conditioning performs better on binary drug tasks, while adding a concise guidance sentence generally benefits continuous material tasks.

♻ ☆ Thinking-Free Policy Initialization Makes Distilled Reasoning Models More Effective and Efficient Reasoners ICLR 2026
Xin Xu, Clive Bai, Kai Yang, Tianhao Chen, Yang Wang, Saiyong Yang, Can Yang
Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward (RLVR) effectively solves complex tasks but demands extremely long context lengths during training, leading to substantial computational costs. While multi-stage training can partially mitigate this, starting with overly short contexts often causes irreversible performance degradation, ultimately failing to reduce overall training compute significantly. In this paper, we introduce Thinking-Free Policy Initialization (TFPI), a simple yet effective adaptation to RLVR that bridges long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) distillation and standard RLVR. TFPI employs a simple ThinkingFree operation, explicitly discarding the thinking content via a direct append, to reduce token usage during inference. Training with ThinkingFree-adapted inputs improves performance and lowers token consumption, even in the original slow-thinking mode. Extensive experiments across various benchmarks have shown that TFPI accelerates RL convergence, achieves a higher performance ceiling, and yields more token-efficient reasoning models without specialized rewards or complex training designs. With TFPI only, we train a 4B model to reach 89.0% accuracy on AIME24 and 65.5% on LiveCodeBench using less than 4K H20 hours.

♻ ☆ Composition-RL: Compose Your Verifiable Prompts for Reinforcement Learning of Large Language Models NeurIPS 2026
Xin Xu, Clive Bai, Kai Yang, Tianhao Chen, Cai Yuchen, Yangkun Chen, Weijie Liu, Hao Chen, Yang Wang, Saiyong Yang, Can Yang
Large-scale verifiable prompts underpin the success of Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), but they contain many uninformative examples and are costly to expand further. Recent studies focus on better exploiting limited training data by prioritizing hard prompts whose rollout pass rate is 0. However, easy prompts with a pass rate of 1 also become increasingly prevalent as training progresses, thereby reducing the effective data size. To mitigate this, we propose Composition-RL, an offline data-augmentation approach that composes existing prompts before RL to address the pass-rate-1 bottleneck without modifying the RL optimizer. The composed dataset is generated and verified once from the fixed source pool and then used for RL training. Extensive experiments across model sizes from 4B to 30B show that Composition-RL consistently improves reasoning capability over RL trained on the original dataset. Performance can be further boosted with a curriculum variant of Composition-RL that gradually increases compositional depth over training. Additionally, Composition-RL enables more effective cross-domain RL by composing prompts drawn from different domains. All our code, data, and models are publicly available to facilitate future research.

☆ Latent Compaction with Test-Time Training
Yingfa Chen, Xingyu Shen, Chaojun Xiao, Xu Han
Long contexts enlarge key--value (KV) caches, increasing memory use and attention costs during generation. Compressing these caches must preserve information for unseen questions. We introduce **Latent Compaction**, which learns a compact document-specific cache through test-time training. We initialize cache entries using reconstruction importance, teacher attention, and key-space diversity. Training first fits attention outputs and log-normalizers to those of the full cache, with reconstruction-selected entries held fixed. It then jointly optimizes the entire cache through end-to-end distillation from the full-cache model. Model weights remain frozen throughout, and training uses only generic prompts about the document, without access to downstream questions or answers. Experiments on RULER, LongBench, and Phonebook Lookup demonstrate substantial gains over budget-matched compression baselines. On a RULER, for example, our method achieves 96.7% accuracy at 32$\times$ compression, compared with 40.0% for KVZip+. The method also transfers across model scales and families, with retrieval gains on both Qwen3 and MiniCPM5. These results show that treating the KV cache as a neural network and optimizing it with test-time training can preserve retrieval accuracy under aggressive compression. The code is released at https://www.github.com/THUNLP/latent-compaction
