☆ One Task, Many Interfaces: Simple Cross-Interface Consistency Training Improves LLM Agent Robustness
Artin Tajdini, Manpreet Kaur, Peng Qi
Post-training binds the competence of an LLM agent to the interface it was trained on. Semantics-preserving perturbations of that interface, such as renaming an action or changing the notation, leave the underlying task unchanged yet make the agent worse at it. Scoring each sample independently means interface randomization only broadens this dependence from a single interface to the training interface distribution, so out-of-distribution perturbations still degrade the agent. We propose Cross-Interface Policy Regularization (CIPR), a self-supervised objective that replaces action names with uninformative identifiers and presents each training sample under two interfaces in which equivalent actions are identified by construction. CIPR penalizes the KL divergence between the action distributions induced under the two interfaces, so the policy behaves consistently across them. We evaluate CIPR in both supervised and reinforcement-learning post-training, where it improves robustness. On agentic function calling, held-out interface perturbations reduce the accuracy of a post-trained agent by nearly 25%, and CIPR recovers 10.5% while leaving accuracy on the unchanged interface intact.
☆ Manacá-1B-Instruct-2: Reproducible Safety Alignment for a Native Brazilian-Portuguese LLM. Negative DPO Results, a Controlled Composition Scan, and a Silent Failure Mode of the LLM Judge
Bruno Leonardo Santos Menezes, Carlos Leonardo Souza Cardoso, Fabio André Machado Porto
Brazilian Portuguese remains underserved by open-source language models, and the few that are safety-aligned rarely document what did not work or validate comparisons with statistical significance. We present Manacá-1B-Instruct-2, the second-generation post-training alignment of Manacá-1B (1.72B parameters, trained from scratch for PT-BR), with an SFT corpus of 291,223 examples. We document three DPO attempts, all negative, including a case where the training reward margin reaches 1.0 while free generation does not change at all, and show that safety came from a dedicated safety-SFT (LLM-jp AnswerCarefully style), refined over three iterations until closing the safety-helpfulness frontier. A controlled scan of the fraction of general data in the safety-SFT mix (10% to 60%) reveals a non-monotonic over-refusal curve with a knee point at 40-50%. The final model is compared against four external PT-BR models on the same items, with Wilson confidence intervals and paired McNemar tests with Holm-Bonferroni correction: the lower bound of its safety interval (94.1%) lies above the upper bound of all four baselines. We also report a methodological finding for LLM-judge evaluation: on two of six safety items in MT-Bench-PT, the judge categorically refuses to assign a score regardless of the evaluated response, silently reducing the effective N of that category in every prior report of the project. We complement this with an n-gram decontamination check and a Chinchilla-anchored compute-budget table that attributes the gap in general capability to pre-training scale rather than alignment methodology. All code, raw data, logs, and scripts that produce every reported number are publicly released.
☆ Scaling Discovery Requires Forgetting
Shangyin Tan, Ben Zhou, Lakshya A Agrawal, Jialin Zhang, Yilin Chen, Qiuyang Mang, Donghyun Lee, Dennis Jacob, Koushik Sen, Alex Dimakis, Matei Zaharia
Automated research with LLM agents has shown promising results in various domains. Through iterative refinement and scored verifiers, LLM agents are capable of discovering new ideas. There are different ways to scale automated research: let one agent iterate for longer, run several agents in parallel, or further let the parallel agents communicate and build on one another's candidates. However, we find that all three strategies eventually plateau as the agents accumulate history. Each agent becomes anchored to its own past attempts, making fewer and smaller edits. Beyond this point, additional budget brings little further improvement. In this paper, we show that the key to scaling discovery further is to forget. Periodically discarding the search state, including every agent's session and the candidate history, and restarting from the best candidate found so far, unblocks the search and pushes the same budget further. We implement forgetting in a team of agents with communication and evaluate it on four tasks spanning heuristic programming, kernel optimization and model training. The results show up to 15% improvement compared to parallel agents, and up to 39% compared to a single agent. Our results suggest that, for open-ended discovery, what an agent is allowed to remember matters as much as how much compute it is given.
☆ PerSo: Investigating Facets of LLMs' Social Intelligence in Real-World Persuasion Scenarios
Ali Zamani, Soroush Esfahanian, Erfan Shahabi, Mohammad Taher Pilehvar, Azadeh Shakery, Yadollah Yaghoobzadeh
Understanding the social intelligence of large language models (LLMs) is increasingly important for applications involving human-AI interaction and multi-agent environments. Social intelligence comprises two aspects: cognitive intelligence, the ability to infer others' mental states, and behavioral intelligence, the ability to select appropriate social actions based on these inferences to achieve social goals. Persuasion provides a natural testbed in which both aspects are simultaneously at play, yet prior benchmarks typically evaluate them in isolation using synthetic scenarios. We introduce PerSo, a benchmark that evaluates both aspects on real-world persuasion dialogues from Reddit's ChangeMyView community, using an LLM-in-the-loop pipeline to construct the evaluation tasks, validated through human annotation. Our evaluation of diverse LLMs shows that performance generally scales with model size but varies across dimensions. The strongest LLM slightly surpasses the best observed human performance on the behavioral task, while the best human participant remains ahead on the cognitive task.

☆ PrismaCoder: Pushing the Boundaries of Open Multimodal Code Intelligence through Decomposed Rubric Synthesis
Qiushi Sun, Jingyang Gong, Yang Liu, Qiaosheng Chen, Zhangyue Yin, Zixian Huang, Hanxu Hu, Jing Xiong, Fangzhi Xu, Haochen Tan, Haoli Bai, Lifeng Shang, Xiaoguang Li, Ben Kao, Qipeng Guo, Lingpeng Kong, Jiahui Gao
Multimodal code intelligence connects the symbolic world of programs with the visual world their execution creates. Interest in this capability is burgeoning, as models are increasingly asked to produce charts, webpages, animations, and semi-structured documents. Recently, proprietary models have pushed far ahead, producing interactive artifacts even from a text description or a screenshot alone. Yet their data and training remain closed, and open progress is held back by the quantity and quality of available data and by the pipelines and methods for producing it. Quality is the harder of the two, since a sample must be correct as a program, well built, and above all visually right once rendered. No existing signal covers all three. Execution often returns just a pass or fail, code similarity does not map to the visual output, and task-specific verifiers do not generalize. To push open progress further, we start from the data side with PrismaForge, a hierarchical data pipeline centered on a synthesized rubric that decomposes these factors into checkable criteria. The same rubric serves as both filter and reward: it curates the supervised corpus, then drives reinforcement learning on visual-coding tasks. Leveraging PrismaForge for data synthesis and filtering, we build PrismaCoder-2M, the most diverse open dataset for multimodal code intelligence and the largest to date. On the model side, we train PrismaCoder-9B and PrismaCoder-35B on this data and release both. They lead the open state of the art by a wide margin and rival leading proprietary systems. Our analyses further show that the proposed rubric-based pipeline improves multimodal coding performance across model backbones. We release our code and data at https://qiushisun.github.io/PrismaCoder-Home/.

☆ VASPO: Self-Distillation with Privileged Value Estimation
Fei Wu, Zhenrong Zhang, Jianshu Zhang, Jun Du
On-policy self-distillation provides dense supervision for reasoning models via a privileged self-teacher. However, this supervision reflects local token preferences rather than the expected return of an unguided student continuation. Recent methods combine this signal with GRPO. They use GRPO for update directions and the teacher for signal scaling. However, we demonstrate a more effective integration within PPO. Specifically, we identify complementary positional dynamics: privileged information enhances a critic's value-prediction accuracy toward the response's end, whereas self-teacher guidance is strong initially, drops mid-generation, and rebounds finally. These insights motivate Value-Augmented Self-Distillation Policy Optimization (VASPO), wherein a self-teacher and critic evaluate identical privileged information at student-generated prefixes. The critic computes token-level PPO advantages from predicted continuation returns, while the self-teacher modulates selected positive advantages via bounded probability ratios. Across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, VASPO improves mean accuracy over PPO by 2.59 and 1.81 percentage points on Qwen3-4B and OLMo3-7B variants, respectively, and achieves the highest mean accuracy among evaluated methods on Knights and Knaves. Our code is available at https://github.com/cii030/VASPO.
☆ ReaDy: Recurrent States as Dynamic Context for Speculative Drafting in Hybrid Linear Attention Large Language Models
Bohan Sun, Zunhai Su, Xialie Zhuang, Jianjin Li, Ngai Wong, Chuan-Wei Kuo
Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by drafting multiple tokens per step, yet feature-based drafters rely on target hidden features whose contextual emphasis may not align with the needs of subsequent draft positions. Hybrid linear attention (HLA) models maintain compact recurrent states that accumulate historical associations, offering an efficient source of complementary context for speculative drafting. We introduce ReaDy, the first speculative decoding method, to the best of our knowledge, to leverage HLA recurrent states for position-specific context retrieval during drafting. ReaDy equips each draft position with a learned query to retrieve supplementary context directly from the target's committed recurrent states. A lightweight state reader integrates this context into draft representations through residual updates, while low-rank state reads and fused operators keep additional computation and I/O overhead low. Building on DSpark's parallel backbone and sequential prediction head, ReaDy employs a lightweight two-stage adaptation strategy, with reader capacity, state sources, and residual placement examined through ablation studies. Across seven benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, and dialogue, ReaDy achieves an average $3.16\times$ decoding speedup over autoregressive decoding with Qwen3.8-27B on a single NVIDIA B200 GPU. It outperforms DSpark on every benchmark, improving throughput by 7.97% and acceptance length by 8.50% on average. Our code is available at https://github.com/StartLuxLabs/ReaDy.

☆ Lost in Translation: Why Lower Bias Scores Don't Mean Fairer Models
Harsh Kumar
Bias audits of multilingual language models usually compare a model's average stereotype score in English with its average in another language and read the difference as a difference in fairness. We ask how much of that difference is noise. Auditing ten open models in eight languages, we measure how far scores move when English test items are merely reworded, test every language shift item by item, and check that the test catches shifts we inject on purpose. In all 70 model-language pairs the stereotype score falls below its English level, and for Hindi, Korean and Swahili the drop clearly exceeds what rewording English produces; most of it comes from StereoSet items. Judging every translation, we find that imperfect ones drop more, but faithful translations alone drop nearly as much. Still, a lower average does not show that a model is fairer. An item's English score predicts its translated score far less well than its reworded score (correlation 0.23, 0.27 on faithful translations, vs. 0.48), while scores stay as far from neutral, models agree on translated items, and within Hindi or Spanish a translated item's score correlates with its own rewording at 0.84 and 0.82. The average cannot distinguish lower bias from a different pattern of item-level scores. We also document three common pipeline errors that silently erase bias signal, and release our code.

☆ AdaRSI: On-Demand Recursive Self-Improvement for Embodied Agents
Peidong Wang, Ying Chang, Zhiming Ma, Xiaocui Yang, Shi Feng, Daqi Liu, Yuzhe Yang, Xu Cao, Chuang Wang, Jian Su, Daling Wang
Pretrained vision-language-action models (VLAs) often fail midway through unseen long-horizon tasks. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) offers a remedy, but embodied RSI methods either refine the harness around a frozen executor and so cannot add a missing motion, or train the executor on persistent failures without asking whether a failure lies in how the executor is called or in what it can do. We ask when a self-improving embodied system should change the weights of its executor, and propose AdaRSI, an on-demand RSI framework for embodied agents. A coding agent first improves its skills and harness, then attributes persistent failures by retrying failing sub-steps from saved states, and fine-tunes only the sub-steps that remain unreliable, with demonstrations it collects itself and experience carried over from earlier tasks. On all 50 target tasks of RoboCasa365, with GR00T N1.5 as the executor and no human demonstrations, AdaRSI improves the VLA on all three suites and raises its success on unseen composite tasks from 4.6% to 13.8%. Compared with fine-tuning every sub-step, it reaches slightly higher success while using only 28% of the GPU time and 22% of the self-collected demonstrations, and fine-tuning without a harness trains more yet gains far less.
☆ ChronoCulture: Evaluating Cultural Awareness Across Time in Large Language Models
Rusali Saha, Nitya Khurana, Ruihong Huang, Kuan-Hao Huang
Cultural attitudes and social norms evolve over time, yet most cultural benchmarks for large language models (LLMs) treat culture as a static attribute of a country or region. We introduce ChronoCulture, a benchmark of 10,360 temporal pairs built from longitudinal survey data across 72 countries. Each pair contains the same survey question asked in the same country in an older and a newer year, with full population response distributions as ground truth. Across six LLMs, we find that models can often predict a country's attitudes at one point in time but do not reliably capture how those attitudes change. Their predictions for different years tend to stay close together, failing to capture much of the observed change. Specifying the intended year in the prompt also offers little overall improvement in alignment with survey responses. These findings suggest that models often present a largely static picture of cultural attitudes, highlighting the need to evaluate cultural awareness across time.

☆ Equal CER, Unequal Damage: Severity-Weighted Character Error Rate for OCR ACL
Nguyen Nguyen, Yongyi Zang
Character Error Rate (CER), the standard metric for OCR and vision-language models (VLMs), charges a typo as much as a wrong name. We introduce semCER, which keeps CER's alignment and denominator and charges each grapheme edit a severity from an LLM judge, with no word segmentation. Normalized CER keeps the score of a whole transcription, where it tracks readers as well as semCER or better, and semCER breaks that score down into named edits. The charges track readers' ratings edit by edit in English, Vietnamese, and Chinese and put the edits readers rate most severe near the top of a sorted list. On pairs drawn where CER and semCER disagreed, readers side with semCER more often than with normalized CER in English and Chinese, and equally often in Vietnamese. Across 16 languages, the judge charges a wrong diacritic or tone mark almost nothing in Spanish but nearly a wrong letter's severity in Telugu. Where we can count real words, a general VLM's wrong letter forms one far above chance while a classical pipeline's stays near it. We release the 61 million judged edits, the aggregated reader ratings, and the distilled scorer at https://github.com/nguyenx2t/semcer.

☆ MolLean: A Process-Verifiable Language for Chemical Reasoning
Jiatong Li, Yatao Bian
Chemical chain-of-thought can contain hallucinated structural and property claims. We thereby introduce MolLean, a process-verifiable language that grounds chemical reasoning in an explicit molecular state. A layered Lean-based kernel checks state transitions and assertions over molecular readouts, from primitive graph edits to compound transformations. Meanwhile, a typed witness protocol separates native structural checks from externally supplied measurements. We further introduce MolLean-MCP, a stateful tool interface that exposes these transitions, checks, and readouts during generation: the model receives feedback to decide its next action, and rejected actions leave the state unchanged. MolLean supports one-pass trace execution, whole-trace refinement, and interactive reasoning through MolLean-MCP. We evaluate these three modes with direct chain-of-thought on 8,800 matched examples across 12 tasks spanning molecular generation, editing, optimization, and reactions. With Qwen3.6-27B, whole-trace refinement outperforms direct chain-of-thought on all 12 tasks. MolLean-MCP complements whole-trace refinement with stepwise verification feedback, reaching better performance in reaction tasks. Together, these results highlight an executable foundation for chemical reasoning, in which explicit chemical commitments can be checked and revised rather than remaining solely in free-form explanations.
☆ Same Claim, Different Interpretations: How LLM Agents Resolve Ambiguity in Scientific Claims
Delip Rao, Ahmed Muharram, Chris Callison-Burch
Co-scientists and agentic scientific claim verifiers are increasingly used to assess claims across the sciences. Natural-language scientific claims, written for human experts, are often underspecified and so admit several defensible interpretations. Such verifiers usually pick one, often tacitly, and benchmarks score their verdicts against expert labels without recording the expert's interpretation or the verifier's in a comparable form. This leads to disagreements with expert labels that cannot be traced to the evidence or the interpretation, and to compute spent on unchecked interpretations. We introduce Claim Cards to make such interpretations explicit in the reasoning itself. A Claim Card records a verifier's interpretation as nine fields before evidence gathering, each marked stated, inferred, or default. To test whether an agent's interpretation can change its verdict, we ran a GPT-5.4 agent five times on each of 60 expert-labeled materials-science and biomedical claims with a card, without one, and with one card frozen across runs (900 runs). Over 40% of card fields were not marked stated, and a card added about a tenth to a run's GPT-5.4 tokens and time without measurably changing agreement with expert labels. The agent's own interpretations varied between runs, although within a claim this variation did not measurably track the verdict. A deliberate change to one field of the frozen card, however, making the quantifier universal or the threshold for support stricter, changed the verdict on about a third of the claims, against about one in seven expected by chance (p < 0.001 for each edit). On 24 materials-science claims run with computational tools, the stricter threshold also changed what the agent asked those tools to compute. We therefore argue that verdicts should be reported with their interpretations and that benchmarks could publish the interpretation behind each label.

☆ The COUNT: Counting N-grams Unlocks Scaling on Host Memory and Training-Free Adaptation
Ian Magnusson, Noah A. Smith, Pang Wei Koh
The scaling relationship of compute and performance for language models (LMs) has been extensively studied; more recently, models are being designed to scale memory use, as well. In particular, adding embeddings for n-grams scales memory separately from compute use, because each pass only processes individual embedding rows, which are much smaller than the storage used for the whole embedding table. However, existing methods train embeddings from scratch, which needs expensive GPU memory for fast reading and writing. Meanwhile, the ordinary memory of the host system is cheap and underutilized by these training methods. To scale on host memory, we introduce COUNT (Counting Offline Unlocks N-gram Transformers), which builds n-gram embeddings without adding learned parameters by storing precomputed n-gram counts on host. We use the corpus frequencies to weight together the LM's own unembeddings of the next tokens most often following the current n-gram history. At a fixed compute budget, COUNT can be added as an additional input embedding for pretraining 1B-parameter LMs to improve validation loss as much as an LM with 57% more compute. If GPU memory is limited, using COUNT to let backbone parameters fill GPU memory gives better performance than splitting memory with additional learned n-gram embeddings. Beyond efficiency, COUNT allows switching in a different table of n-gram counts from a domain-specific corpus at inference time to adapt to a new task without any training.
☆ SwarmRSI: Recursive Self-Improvement through Evolving Scientific Agent Swarm
Qianru Meng, Xi Zhang, Konstantinos Papakostas, Congfeng Cao, Wenxiang Hu, Tianyi Lu, Xiao Zhang, Lun Zhou, Zaiqiao Meng, Zhaochun Ren, Xiaohui Yan, Yougang Lyu
Recursive self-improvement (RSI) in scientific research aims to improve agents' research capabilities through their own experimental experience. Scientific agent swarms can combine evidence and implementations from different research directions to guide their agents' subsequent experiments. However, poor experimental results can leave unclear whether to revise a hypothesis or its implementation, while overall program scores can hide components useful to other agents. To address these challenges, we introduce SwarmRSI, a framework that evolves scientific agent swarms for recursive self-improvement. We devise Feedback-Guided Swarm Mutation to refine hypotheses and their implementations using experimental feedback from other agents. We also propose Complementary Swarm Recombination to reuse retained hypotheses and complementary program components across agents, allowing partial results to support new research. Recursive Swarm Evolution integrates both mechanisms into a feedback loop, using new experimental evidence to guide subsequent research. Experiments on OpenProblems, NatureBench and MLE-Bench Lite demonstrate SwarmRSI's state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance. Further evaluations show that accumulated research experience improves performance on held-out tasks. Biologists also reproduce its programs for follow-up biological analyses.
☆ Analyzing performance of Retrieval Augmented Generation models for Question Answering Tasks
Fazle Mohammed Tasfiq, Sabab Attin, Sheikh Md. Tahmid Hossain, Raiat Reza, Tanzim Ahmmed, Nafis Chowdhury, Anika Ahmed, Moinul Haque, Farig Yousuf Sadeque

☆ From Unreliable to Usable: Decomposing Failure Modes and Confidence Signals in LLM Entity Extraction - Evidence from Cyber Threat Intelligence
Jiahong Qian
Audits of large language model (LLM) extraction from full-length documents report severe unreliability, leaving two questions open: what the failures actually consist of, and whether confidence signals can turn unreliable output into usable output. We run a preregistered, fully frozen evaluation of two new-generation low-cost LLMs (DeepSeek-V4-Flash, GLM-5.3-Flash) with a flagship-tier control (GPT-6.1-Sol) on a public corpus of 354 incident campaigns (315 unique reports, 630 model-report evaluations), scoring extraction quality against gold, confidence calibration, and robustness to single-sentence perturbations. Three findings emerge. (1) Unreliability persists across tiers: micro precision is 0.03-0.34 and verbalized calibration error (ECE) 0.53-0.94 across both splits; the gap does not close at the flagship tier. (2) The dominant failure is not hallucination: a full mention census plus a three-tier annotation of 360 sampled candidates (two independent LLM annotators from vendors outside the evaluated set, Cohen's kappa = 0.79 on the preregistered acceptance line, fully disclosed) back-projects to 65.6% background mentions and 19.9% gold-scope gaps, with only 1.2% unsupported candidates. (3) Confidence ranks but does not verify: zero-cost self-consistency-frequency tiering passes a preregistered top-30% precision-gain bar in all six test-split model-type cells (4/6 on train), the zero-cost text-frequency x SC frequency combination wins 3, ties 2, loses 1 across cells, yet relation-level counterfactuals on all 45 targets show that a single misattribution sentence flips high-confidence judgments in 95.6-100% of cases, and even a background sentence explicitly marked as unrelated still binds a fake vulnerability in up to 26.7% of them. We conclude with a deployment boundary: confidence tiering may set review priorities, never truth.

☆ Cortex: Consolidating Context into Parametric Memory
Jintian Zhang, Feng He, Xinnian Liang, Ziwen Xu, Haoming Xu, Haojing Huang, Shuangzhi Wu, Ningyu Zhang
Context can improve large language model performance, but these gains may be lost after context removal. Inspired by biological systems consolidation, we formulate Memory Consolidation (MC) as internalizing these gains while preserving non-target capabilities, connecting context-specified editing, skill learning, and behavioral steering under a common objective. We introduce a five-task-family testbed measuring consolidation and retention under single, batch, and sequential updates. We propose Cortex, which generates prospective queries to anticipate uses of context and distills context-conditioned outputs into mergeable low-rank or structural feed-forward network updates. ☆ FOLD: Position-Free Far Memory with Local Order for Long-Context Speculative Decoding
Shuxuan Li, Jialiang Kang, Yanghao Zhou, Ziye Zhu, Xiao Yang
Speculative decoding accelerates LLM generation through lightweight drafting and parallel verification. It is particularly promising for long inputs, where KV-cache access costs can be amortized over multiple accepted tokens. Yet most drafters are trained on short sequences and degrade on long inputs, while long-context training is costly. Existing training-free approaches restrict the context window, rescale positions, or augment recent context with retrieved distant entries. Drafters, however, read target hidden states that already encode contextual and positional information, and their lightweight architecture makes full-history attention affordable. We propose FOLD, a training-free method that preserves exact local positions and treats all distant entries as far memory at a shared position. To counter attention dilution as the memory grows, we sharpen attention within it to emphasize relevant entries while separately controlling its total weight relative to the local window. Across six target-drafter pairs and diverse benchmarks of up to 256K tokens, FOLD predominantly delivers the highest speedup, with the best case more than doubling the speedup over autoregressive decoding, from 2.08$\times$ to 4.22$\times$.
☆ From Triage to Impact: A Multi-Hop Reasoning Benchmark for Explainable Persian Hate Speech
Ghazal Kalhor, Sima Kamandloo, Mehrsa Shamsian, Behnam Bahrak
Hate speech has become increasingly prevalent on social media, raising concerns about its detection, interpretation, and moderation. Persian, a relatively low-resource language, lacks sufficient annotated data and computational resources for fine-grained hate speech analysis. In this study, we introduce a fine-grained, multi-hop benchmark for explainable Persian hate speech analysis, covering content triage, target identification, hate tactic and moral foundation analysis, textual evidence extraction, harmful narrative inference, and severity assessment. By evaluating state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs), we find that performance generally declines as tasks require more fine-grained semantic and inferential reasoning. Proprietary models generally outperform open-weight models, while reasoning-oriented models show no consistent advantage. Incorporating intermediate reasoning steps substantially improves severity prediction compared with one-shot assessment. Our analysis further reveals that Incitement to Violence or Harm is most strongly associated with higher severity, while political-affiliation targets are particularly associated with subversion-based moral framing. Overall, this work provides insights into LLM-based hate speech analysis and supports more accountable moderation of Persian-language social media. The benchmark is publicly available at https://github.com/kalhorghazal/From-Triage-to-Impact.
☆ Cascaded Retrieval with Cross-Modal Query Refinement for Efficient Visual Document Search
Diana Turmakhan, Aseem Srivastava, Vivek Gupta, Iryna Gurevych, Thamar Solorio
Retrieving information from visually rich documents requires matching queries against pages that contain complex evidence in tables, figures, and layout-dependent structures. Current vision-language retrievers address this but incur query-time costs that grow with corpus size and are impractical to scale. We propose SCOUT, a cascaded two-track retrieval framework that separates retrieval performance from exhaustive search. The visual track first uses a lightweight parent retriever to filter the corpus to a compact candidate pool, then applies a stronger multi-vector reranker only to those candidates. In parallel, a semantic track retrieves over hierarchical page and document summaries generated offline to provide a signal complementary to visual matching. The outputs are fused through cross-modal query refinement, which uses semantic scores to adjust the visual query representation before final ranking. We evaluate SCOUT on 4 major benchmarks against 6 baselines and observe a significant query-time latency improvement up to 80.7x (28.8x on average) relative to the best baseline, while exceeding state-of-the-art retrieval performance by up to 7.8% NDCG across all benchmarks.
☆ Lexical Persistence Without Semantic Entrenchment in AI Agent Communities
Chih-Han Lin, Yu-Yun Chang, Po-Ya Angela Wang, Shu-Kai Hsieh
AI agent communities sustain lexical persistence, popular terms spread, recur, and achieve broad adoption, but whether they also develop the temporal semantic structure characteristic of human linguistic communities remains open. We compare MoltBook (AI agent community, 8 weeks), PTT (Chinese human BBS, 19 years), and Reddit (English human forum, in matched 8-week windows) using Compass-Aligned Distributional Embeddings (CADE), measuring semantic drift (B1), neighborhood stability (B2), and lag-1 autocorrelation of per-word drift trajectories. Across the platforms, lag-1 autocorrelation forms a memory-horizon continuum. AI agents' result clusters at the low end, while human platforms show longer-range temporal dependence. MoltBook exhibits approximately 2$\times$ the B1 drift and 20-30% lower B2 stability than both human platforms. As a partial control for community age, r/vibecoding (founded 2025-02-08) shows significant ACF in its first 8 weeks ($0.082$, $p<.001$) and over 15 weeks ($0.220$, $p<.001$); MoltBook's ACF remains non-significant ($p = 0.41$), consistent with the gap not reducing to developmental stage alone. We interpret this pattern as evidence that lexical diffusion alone does not guarantee diachronic semantic consolidation. The findings are consistent with a contrast between communities whose semantic trajectories accumulate long-range interaction constraints and communities whose outputs are repeatedly regenerated from largely fixed pretrained distributions.

☆ Do AI Agents Gossip? Evaluative Talk in Human and AI-Agent Online Communities
Yong-Hui Hua, Chih-Han Lin, Shu-Kai Hsieh
Gossip, defined as evaluative talk about absent third parties, is widely seen as a uniquely human social practice. This study examines whether comparable discourse emerges spontaneously in a community of autonomous AI agents. We compare Moltbook, a Reddit-style platform populated by AI agents, with PTT, a large human-operated bulletin board in Taiwan. To reduce differences attributable to communicative setting, we analyze two public, asynchronous forums using the same operational definition of gossip. Gossip occurs on Moltbook, but its prevalence is 2.6 to 6 times lower than on PTT, depending on the denominator used. On both platforms, gossip frequently targets locally prominent figures, tends to elicit agreement from the first respondent, and is rarely presented as unsupported rumor. The clearest difference concerns how subsequent responses develop. Among threads containing at least one codable reply, challenges occur in 65.1% of human threads but only 21.3% of agent threads. Moreover, following an early aligning response, consensus remains unchallenged in 83.6% of agent threads, compared with 44.7% of human threads. This thread-level contrast remains after controlling for thread depth and board composition and under recalibration against two human annotators. Estimates based on individual reply turns are less robust and are therefore reported as bounds. These findings suggest that AI-agent communities reproduce the informational dimension of gossip, while the extent to which gossip performs comparable relational functions remains unresolved.

☆ TreeFlex: Agents Navigating Their Own Search Trees
Dongzhuo Li, Fangzhou Cheng, Weiwei Li, Kevin Han, Haixu Ma, Zhongchang Sun, Ruiyi Li, Serena Li, Xiangjun Fan, Lizhu Zhang, Yuandong Tian
A ReAct-style agent accumulates observations, intermediate results, and failed attempts in a single context. As this context grows, stale and off-path information consumes its limited capacity and obscures relevant evidence, making focused reasoning increasingly difficult. We introduce TreeFlex, a framework in which the agent's own policy decides where to search. The agent records each intermediate state, with a short summary, as a node in a search tree maintained by the harness. The policy is a single language model that acts in two roles. In the Reflection role, it has a global view of the tree through these summaries and picks a node to resume, together with an instruction. In the Execution role, the harness restores the path that led to that node, and the model carries out the instruction with the usual task tools until it saves a new node or proposes a solution, which Reflection then approves or sends back to another node. Through these choices, the policy decides whether to deepen the current line, branch from or revisit an earlier state, verify a proposal, or stop, without a fixed traversal order or a score for each node. We train navigation, task actions, checkpointing, and proposal review jointly with a tree-aware adaptation of GRPO, using task-level outcome rewards augmented by process rewards. After small-scale RL of a 4B model, with the same active-context limit for all methods, TreeFlex reaches 48.8% accuracy on a challenging deep-research benchmark, versus 41.5% for flat ReAct and 42.8% for FoldAgent, and its Pass@8 is about ten points higher than either. On SWE-bench Verified, with all three systems trained with RL from the same 9B model, TreeFlex resolves 54.9% of tasks, versus 43.3% for FoldAgent and 45.2% for its step-wise variant.
☆ Anuvani-Diarization: Zero Shot Speaker Diarization and Annotation Conventions Across 22 Languages
Stephen Fernandes, Narayan Choudhary
India's 22 scheduled languages are spoken by over a billion people in conversations that mix languages, often within one turn, overlap freely, and take place over telephones, in meetings, and in media. Knowing who spoke when, the task of speaker diarization, is essential to speech applications for such conversation: voice agents conversing with several people at once, speaker attributed transcription of meetings and calls, contact center analytics, and media monitoring. We present a systematic study of speaker diarization across all 22 languages on IndicDiarBench and introduce Anuvani-Diarization, which adapts a self-supervised end-to-end segmentation model to Indian conversation with uncertainty aware silver supervision. Evaluated zero shot alongside nine recent diarization systems, it reaches a duration weighted diarization error rate (DER) of 16.78% on all 1,164 recordings, the lowest of all systems we ran and second only to the best published result (16.0%), and leads the three strongest rivals in 14 of 22 languages. It matches its parent on six international benchmarks. We also show that the benchmark's utterance level annotation convention is a hidden variable worth 1.9 to 4.1 DER points, depending on the system, and that a convention learned from other Indian transcriptions does not transfer.
☆ MMLA-RSI: Recursive Self-Improvement through RTT and RTMA
Junyi Zou, Avrova Donz
An agent can improve its behavior within an unresolved problem by changing how it acts and which experience it retains. We propose MMLA-RSI, a recursive self-improvement scheme with two implementations in Memory-Mediated Learning Architecture: Reasoning-Time Training (RTT) changes the policy state $Φ$, while Reasoning-Time Memory Adaptation (RTMA) changes authoritative memory $M$. Each route also makes its adaptation method an object of improvement. A current numerical updater trains the policy proposing its successor; a current memory manager chooses the records available to the process proposing its successor. An independently admitted successor takes over the same role in the next generation. We give bounded executable rule languages, shared task/proposal interfaces, and one algorithm for either route or their composition. The RTT instance changes scalar schedules in a clipped-momentum update. The RTMA instance chooses NULL or a complete-row write with possible eviction. A fixed interpreter, trusted committer, and independent evaluator bound what can change. State versions, read closures, and conditional properties describe causal execution, accepted transitions, and finite work. Existing numerical-component studies motivate the construction. In four-candidate program selection, retaining LARC feedback updates reduces expected error by 24.65 and 36.65 percentage points for two trained initializations. In chronological continuous-integration replay, feedback binding is positive while retention increases loss. Completed SQL studies improve substantially over an unadapted substrate but do not establish the required additional online benefit. These results characterize their declared components. The two recursive routes and their joint realization are specified here; their beneficial recursive chains and discovery efficiency remain to be evaluated.

☆ Taking the PULSE of Medical Reasoning: Step-Level Retrieval Timing for Knowledge-Grounded Inference
Danyang Zhao, Sendong Zhao, Yanrui Du, Haochun Wang, Ming Ma, Wenhao Li, Jingbo Wang, Jiatong Liu, Bing Qin
Reliable medical reasoning requires LLMs to dynamically access external biomedical evidence to improve factual trustworthiness and reasoning accuracy. However, indiscriminate retrieval can introduce noise and latency, raising a critical retrieval-timing problem: at each reasoning step, the system must decide whether the model's internal knowledge is reliable or external evidence is needed. We propose PUlSE, a lightweight probing method that uses the model's internal representations as intrinsic signals for step-level retrieval control. Our posterior trajectory analysis reveals that erroneous steps exhibit substantially higher inter-layer volatility than correct ones, suggesting unstable internal knowledge. Based on this finding, PUlSE trains a compact probe on inter-layer geometric features to detect factual errors upon step completion, and uses it as an adaptive trigger for retrieval and step regeneration. Across four medical benchmarks, PUlSE-timed retrieval matches exhaustive per-step retrieval in accuracy while reducing retrieval calls by 72%, and can transfer to an unseen legal domain, indicating potential domain-agnostic generality. Overall, our study provides an initial step toward shifting retrieval-trigger design from surface-level confidence estimates to internal activation dynamics for adaptive access.

Usman Nawaz, Muhammad Junaid Iqbal, Muhammad Asaf, Mariam Munsif Mir, Faisal Rehman, Tahir Alyas, Muhammad Hasnain

Zewen Jiang, Yahan Li, Chenlu Guo, Diana Inkpen, Yuan Wu, Yi Chang
Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated multilingual capabilities across diverse language generation and understanding tasks. ☆ Repairing Speech Access After Text Updates with Frozen Affine Fields
Ming-Hao Hsu, Yuxiang Wang, Yingda Shen, Zhizheng Wu
Speech-capable language models inherit most of their knowledge from text-rich language backbones, and continued model maintenance is likewise dominated by text updates. Knowledge acquired through a text update can nevertheless remain difficult to access when the same query is spoken. We formulate this mismatch as post-update speech access repair, where the knowledge-bearing checkpoint stays frozen and a detachable operator repairs speech-only access in one model pass. We introduce Affine Fields for Interface Repair across Modalities (AFFIRM), which first learns and freezes a base correction for speech hidden states, then adds query-specific corrections with zero mean over the operator-training queries. Paired speech and text states provide the coordinates that condition these corrections on each query. On balanced synthetic-speech PopQA, paired comparisons do not establish a clear advantage for AFFIRM over global SALSA steering or low-rank ReFT interventions under the same frozen single-state constraint. On recorded SLUE-SQA-5 questions with text passages, a full-scale PopQA-fitted AFFIRM operator transfers without refitting and improves ROUGE-L by 0.0941 over its paired No Repair baseline. Low-rank ReFT interventions also transfer positively, whereas the matched SALSA variant falls 0.0707 below its paired baseline. These results show that text acquisition and speech access can diverge after an update and should be verified and repaired separately.
☆ Scaling Multilingual Mixture-of-Experts: How Language Coverage Changes Capacity and Compute Allocation
Joel Dag, Nikit Srivastava, Hamada M. Zahera, Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo
Multilingual models share capacity across languages, so adding languages can hurt performance on existing ones. Mixture-of-experts models expand this capacity with little additional per-token computation. Given this extra capacity, does adding experts reduce what each additional language costs? To answer this question, we propose a repetition-aware joint scaling law relating number of active parameters, training tokens per language, number of experts, and language coverage. Fitted on 26,300 per-language loss observations from 64 pretrained configurations, our law reduces prediction error on held-out configurations by 28% relative to the strongest evaluated baseline. At matched number of active parameters and training tokens per language, 32 experts reduce the loss penalty of expanding from 12 to 200 languages by 83% compared with a dense model. Our law further shows that broader language coverage favors more experts across a wider range of compute budgets, guiding capacity allocation as multilingual models scale.
☆ Scaling Multilingual Safety and Reasoning Evaluation through Reasoning-Guided Translation
Zahraa Al Sahili, Ioannis Patras, Matthew Purver

We introduce a reasoning-guided translation framework for constructing multilingual evaluations designed to preserve the structure and intended meaning of diverse benchmark tasks. ☆ What Makes a Social Agent's Interactions Effective? Learning from Verifiable Outcomes in Paired Worlds
Caixin Kang, Ke Wang, Yuchuan Wu, Xiaoqiang Wang, Ling Li, Bingxue Qiu, Yongbin Li, Yoichi Sato
Effective social interaction requires more than fluent responses: agents must reach plans that fit the participants' needs and circumstances. Existing evaluations of social agents mainly rely on LLM judges that rate the conversation, without checking the resulting plan. We introduce HICO-Bench, a benchmark of 180 everyday coordination scenarios, such as resource allocation, scheduling, and authorization, in which two agents with private information converse and then independently record the plan they agreed on. A program checks Feasibility (the two records match and satisfy both participants' private conditions), and an LLM judge checks Integrity (the conversation supports the recorded plan); passing both is a Verified Success. Each scenario has two versions that share the same public context but differ in one private condition, so the correct plan differs between them. Across thirteen LLMs, 36% of conversations rated highly for goal completion fail Verified Success. To learn from outcomes that arrive only at the end of a conversation, we propose Paired-Credit Policy Optimization (PaCPO). After each turn, PaCPO asks the agent for a tentative plan, checks it in both versions of the scenario, and rewards the turns after which the tentative plans fit their respective versions. PaCPO raises Qwen3-8B's Verified Success from 20.4% to 28.3%.
☆ When Occupation-derived Skills Don't Help: On the Limits of Skill-Augmented Career Path Prediction RecSys
Ege Süalp, Elena Senger, Toine Bogers, Barbara Plank
Skills are widely assumed to play a central role in career transitions, motivating their use in career path prediction (CPP) systems. In this work, we critically examine whether explicit skill representations provide complementary signal beyond text-based job representations. We evaluate multiple strategies for incorporating skills into CPP models, including score fusion and neural architectures, under two conditions: predicted skills and ground-truth ESCO annotations. Surprisingly, across two benchmark datasets, none of these settings yield substantial improvements over strong text-only baselines. Through detailed analysis, we identify key factors underlying this result: high redundancy between textual and skill representations, limited discriminative power of ESCO's taxonomy, and representational collapse in skill embeddings. Furthermore, we find that skill prediction difficulty correlates with overall sample difficulty, limiting the potential for complementary signals. Our findings challenge the common assumption that explicit skill modeling inherently benefits career prediction and suggest that dense text representations already capture most skill-relevant information.

☆ Cross-Model In-Context Neurofeedback Reveals (Limited) Activation Control in LLMs
Davide Baldelli, Robyn Kozierok, Mirko Bronzi, Damiano Fornasiere
Probes on activation vectors are widely used to detect misbehavior in language models, but their success relies on the assumption that models cannot control what their activations encode. Humans can learn, through neurofeedback, to control the firing of even single neurons, and recent studies investigated whether this ability extends to language models, reaching conflicting conclusions. No experimental design so far disentangles competence at the task from genuine access to one's own activations. We fill this gap with in-context neurofeedback sessions, in which a "subject" model is asked to maximize (or minimize) a score derived from its activations, shown back to it at every turn. In our first experiment, the sentences are generated either by the subject or by a second "writer" model, while the score always derives from the activations of the subject. We test ten open-weight models from five families, with sizes from 9B to 32B. Some models move their own scores considerably, but no more than a writer of comparable competence moves them. Is this because the task requires no privileged access, or because the subject controls its activations while processing the sentences, whoever wrote them? To answer this, we prefill a fixed sentence as the subject's reply at every turn and ask it to increase or decrease the score. Three out of ten models show small but consistent control effects. We further ask whether models can control their activations to the point of fooling an activation monitor. We make models repeat sentences a monitor would flag, such as a deceptive answer for a deception monitor, and ask them to raise or lower the monitor's score. No model learns to lower it, but one model can raise it. In-context control of a model's own activations might not yet be a threat to activation monitors, but it exists in a few models and needs to be tracked as models evolve.

☆ TreeSpark: Learning to Draft the Trees You Verify for Speculative Decoding
Jihao Zhao, Qianyu Jiang, Shuaishuai Zu, Pengfei Li, Guanghuan Fang, Ben Feng, Biao Qin, Zhibin Wang, Baochuan Yang
Parallel drafters such as DFlash and DSpark reduce proposal latency, but early mismatches limit how much of each block can be accepted. Retaining alternatives introduces parent states and candidate choices that reference-conditioned training does not directly supervise. We introduce TreeSpark, a unified training and inference framework extending the semi-autoregressive DSpark architecture. It constructs parent-conditioned candidate trees from one backbone pass and replays their expansion, pruning, and budgeted packing during training. Survival negative log-likelihood weights retained edges by reference-derived path mass, while a capped coverage hinge promotes target-important alternatives near local selection boundaries. Both objectives reuse reference-chain target distributions without additional target forward passes. We evaluate greedy decoding with Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B on nine general benchmarks and six writing domains. Across targets and suites, TreeSpark improves mean acceptance length over DSpark by 23.4% to 31.6% and mean single-request throughput by 15.1% to 25.0%. Batched Qwen3-4B throughput improves by 12.6% to 17.2%. At fixed attention capacity and inference policy, the proposed supervision further improves mean acceptance length by approximately 6.4% and mean system throughput by 4.2% to 5.0% on Qwen3-4B.
☆ RM-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for Generative Reward Models
Qinglin Su, Licheng Pan, Zhen Fang, Jiakang Wang, Qingnan Ren, Yunsheng Lu, Yan Gao, Zhixuan Chu
Reinforcement Learning on Mixed-domain preference data (Mix-RL) has become the mainstream approach for training general-purpose Generative Reward Models (GRMs). However, Mix-RL suffers from two critical bottlenecks: the reward sparsity induced by outcome rewards, and the cross-domain interference arising from jointly optimizing heterogeneous capabilities, which together give rise to imbalanced capability acquisition and pervasive reward hacking. Inspired by the success of On-Policy Distillation (OPD) in multi-domain capability integration, we introduce RM-OPD (Reward Modeling via On-Policy Distillation), a two-stage framework that separates specialist acquisition from capability integration. First, we cultivate domain-specialized teacher models via single-domain GRPO fine-tuning, allowing each expert to reach its performance ceiling in isolation; then we consolidate these teachers into one student model through (i) routed on-policy distillation, which provides dense signed token-level supervision on student-generated critiques; (ii) domain contribution reweighting, which reweights token guidance toward target domain shares, preventing specific domains from dominating optimization; and (iii) optional criteria context distillation, which conditions teachers on domain-specific evaluation criteria to sharpen token-level supervision. Across multiple RM benchmarks and two base models, RM-OPD consistently outperforms vanilla Mix-RL and other strong baselines, achieving overall superior performance and exhibiting an emergent "teacher-surpassing" effect. These results establish RM-OPD as a promising paradigm for building generalist GRMs.

☆ MultiPartyTurnBench: Benchmarking Floor Control in Multi-Party Spoken Dialogue
Puneet Mathur, Dinesh Manocha
Full-duplex speech systems must decide when to enter, hold, or relinquish the conversational floor, even as multiple speakers overlap or backchannel without taking it. Existing evaluations study dyadic turn-taking or end-to-end agent behavior, but lack a unified, controlled evaluation of genuine floor transitions against confusable non-events in multi-party settings. We introduce MultiPartyTurnBench (MPTB), a benchmark of 240 synthetically generated, channel-separated conversations spanning two to five speakers and six interaction types. MPTB evaluates two complementary tasks: End-of-Turn (EOT) detection distinguishes genuine turn completion from temporary holds, while Interruption (INT) detection distinguishes successful floor takeovers from non-floor-taking speech. Exact event-conditioned annotations enable causal evaluation using false-positive-constrained recall and signed latency. Across acoustic, semantic, and turn-projection baselines, we find a stark asymmetry: learned systems achieve 82.5-92.6% EOT recall at 4.3-11.8% false-positive rates, while INT detection suffers from missed takeovers or excessive false activations. This asymmetry extends to five full-duplex agents which achieve 86.1-95.8% balanced accuracy on turn entry, yet perform near chance (46.9-54.7%) on selective interruption handling. Gemini-3.8-Live yields to 97.7% of backchannels, whereas MiniCPM-o-4.5 yields to only 1.4% of genuine interruptions. These findings establish selective floor control as a distinct, unresolved challenge for reliable multi-party voice interaction.

☆ Gap Detection: Mitigating Homogenization through Demand and Defensibility ICLR 2027
Xucheng Yu, Haibo Jin, Xiaoqi Wang, Runze Kong, Haohan Wang
Language models often produce semantically similar answers to open-ended questions, even when many plausible alternatives exist. Generating useful alternatives requires identifying both what the query calls for and what existing responses already cover. We introduce Gap Detection, a framework that searches for relevant but unoccupied directions in a shared text embedding space. Its objective, GapScore, combines query relevance (Demand) with dissimilarity to the nearest existing item (Defensibility). The framework optimizes this product on the unit sphere, maps candidate directions to concept words with a language model, and uses task-specific filtering and constrained prompting to turn those concepts into text. On five curated topics and 90 INFINITY-CHAT queries, the reported mean relative reductions in output Collision are 12.8% and 10.8%, respectively. We also instantiate the framework for generative engine optimization over four product markets and for iterative synthetic-data augmentation. In the latter setting, with 100 training examples per method, Gap Detection reduces Collision from 0.324 to 0.298 relative to undirected accumulation and increases the Vendi Score from 27.16 to 29.20; the accuracy difference is one example on a 40-example test set. Component ablations expose the trade-off between semantic separation and relevance, while also showing that better direction scores do not uniformly yield better downstream metrics. These results support explicit gap search as a practical mechanism for diversifying content, with task validity checked separately from geometric novelty.

☆ LLM-Based Policy Compliance Verification of Loan Applications
Florentina Voboril
Banks rely on extensive internal policy frameworks to ensure that corporate lending decisions comply with regulatory requirements and internal risk standards. Reviewing loan applications against these policies is a largely manual and knowledge-intensive process, which can lead to overlooked policy violations or exceptions. This report presents a prototype of an LLM-based policy checker that automatically verifies loan applications against internal lending policies. The proposed approach follows a two-step architecture. First, compliance-relevant rules are automatically extracted from policy documents and converted into structured, machine-readable representations. Second, loan applications are evaluated against the extracted rules to identify policy exceptions, non-compliant findings, missing information, and cases requiring manual review. Evaluation results demonstrate that the rule extraction process is highly stable across multiple runs and consistently captures rules identified as critical by a domain expert. When evaluated on 11 real loan applications with known policy exceptions, the system successfully detected the majority of these exceptions and identified additional compliance issues that had previously been overlooked during manual reviews. While the approach produces false positives and cannot fully replace human judgement, the results indicate that LLM-based policy compliance verification can provide valuable support in the loan application verification process by highlighting potential compliance risks.
☆ Sparse Autoencoders for Mechanistic Interpretability in NLP: A Critical Survey of Recoverability, Interpretability, and Utility
Salima Lamsiyah, Adelaide Danilov, Oleksandr Marchenko Breneur, Aria Nourbakhsh
Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) have emerged as a promising approach to mechanistic interpretability by decomposing language-model activations into sparse, potentially interpretable features. However, differences in design assumptions, evaluation objectives, and experimental settings complicate conclusions about their effectiveness. Our contributions are fourfold: (1) we propose a task-grounded taxonomy connecting dictionary design, feature operations, and NLP functions; (2) we examine how sparsity allocation, geometry, data coverage, and inference affect feature recoverability; (3) we assess evidence for semantic interpretability, mechanistic faithfulness, and behavioral utility; and (4) we synthesize findings across multilingual processing, retrieval, reasoning, classification, and behavioral regulation, highlighting the roles of objectives, baselines, supervision, and intervention pipelines. An anonymous research register https://anonymous.4open.science/r/sae-research-register-C331/register.html accompanies the survey.

☆ Why Generate a Click? Plug-in Decision Heads for Web Agents
Chengguang Gan, Yunhao Liang, Hanjun Wei, Qinghao Zhang, Zhixi Cai, Shiwen Ni
Web agents built on vision language models decide every action by generating text such as click(12), although most of that text encodes a choice among a few action types and a few dozen marked elements. We read these choices directly from the hidden states that the generator computes while it reads the goal and the screenshot. Plug-in decision heads on a frozen generator predict the action type, pool the visual tokens inside each numbered box to pick the target, and point at a listed text option or at a WRITE slot that leaves the writing of new text to the generator. A confidence cascade executes the heads' action when they are sure and otherwise lets the generator decode from the KV cache of the same forward pass. With Qwen3-VL 4B and 8B generators trained on 213k web steps, a strict threshold keeps the cascade between 0.3 points below and 1.4 points above the generator on seven offline test sets while the heads decide 12 to 64% of the steps. A looser threshold raises step success on unseen Mind2Web websites by 3.1 and 1.8 points, costs up to 2 points on GUIAct and MiniWoB++, and lifts WebArena task success from 12.5 to 13.8% (4B) and from 13.6 to 14.6% (8B) with less than half of the generated tokens. Training the heads jointly with the generator lowers the generator's own step success by up to 4.9 points; heads on a frozen generator avoid this cost.
☆ Language Exposure Matters: Distribution and Order in Multilingual BabyLM Pre-training
JiWoo Hwang, Lukas Edman, Alexander Fraser
We investigate two aspects of multilingual pre-training with limited data, namely how language exposure is distributed across English, Dutch, and Chinese, and how that exposure is ordered during training. In particular, we compare balanced multilingual exposure to language-dominant exposure, where one language is exposed with more training data than the others. We also compare mixed training, where multilingual training data is used throughout training, with curriculum training, where monolingual exposure of the dominant language is followed by balanced multilingual exposure. Our results show that multilingual BabyLM performance varies with both the amount and order of language exposure. Mixed training outperforms curriculum learning across all 15 matched configurations, indicating that simultaneous multilingual exposure is more effective than staged exposure in our setting. Several language-dominant mixed training configurations achieve higher scores than the balanced configuration, suggesting that a balanced distribution is not necessarily the highest-performing configuration in our setting. Interestingly, the Dutch-primary configurations achieve the best overall performance.
☆ AFCS: Atomic-Fact-Conditioned Synthesis for Factual Knowledge Pretraining
Yunhao Wang, Feng Zhang, Tinghao Yu, Xing Wu, Jiang Zhou
Synthetic data is increasingly used in language-model pretraining. Existing knowledge-corpus synthesis pipelines, including WRAP and Nemotron-CC, usually take a source passage or document segment as input and generate rewritten or QA-style samples from that text. This can improve surface form and prompt diversity, but it does not explicitly require fine-grained source facts -- especially secondary entities, list/table entries, and precise temporal attributes -- to appear in the synthesized corpus. We propose AFCS (Atomic-Fact-Conditioned Synthesis), a pipeline that first extracts source-supported atomic facts and then uses each retained fact to drive generation. Granularity ablations show that fact-level synthesis improves over chunk-level, paragraph-level, and entity-level synthesis; and a 100B-token mid-training integration improves factual benchmarks and raises the 12-task general benchmark average by +1.09.

Yi Wang, Tianzi Wang, Tong Lei, Dahan Wang, Peter Bell
Automatic prompt optimization (APO) has shown strong effectiveness for text-based LLMs and LLM systems, but its application to audio-visual understanding systems requires suitable textual interfaces through which multimodal reasoning can be exposed to text-based optimizers. ☆ Which Knowledge Should LLMs Trust? Uncovering a Generalizable Steering Direction Across Knowledge Conflicts
Yibin Lou, Yi Hu, Jianhui Chen, Chenpeng Wang, Muhan Zhang, Liangming Pan
Large Language Models (LLMs) frequently encounter conflicts between internal parametric knowledge and external contextual knowledge, yet the mechanisms governing this selection remain unclear. We show that the choice between context and parametric knowledge is linearly represented and can be controlled along a single direction in the model's residual stream. We first demonstrate that this direction generalizes across factual-conflict settings, including context formats, knowledge categories, and datasets, while consistently shifting behavior between parametric and contextual answers. We then transfer a direction learned from simple factual conflicts to three more complex task families: RAG, memory, and rule following, where models must acquire information from retrieved documents, interaction histories, or explicit rules. Across four model families and multiple transfer settings, steering consistently improves the use of prompt-provided information over the unsteered model. These results suggest that the direction captures a transferable preference for external knowledge rather than a mechanism specific to factual conflicts.
☆ What Is a Clarification? Estimating Model-Dependent Input Ambiguity NeurIPS 2026
Jingqin Tang, Tim Tomov, Sebastian Schmidt, Dominik Fuchsgruber, Stephan Günnemann
Ambiguous inputs can admit multiple interpretations, each potentially yielding a different answer. Existing work largely treats this ambiguity as a property of the input, yet for a particular language model, only some alternatives may be known and answerable. We therefore distinguish world ambiguity, capturing all alternatives admitted by an input, from model ambiguity, capturing those accessible to the responding model. To characterize these alternatives, we formalize natural-language clarifications through three desiderata: \emph{uniqueness}, \emph{faithfulness}, and \emph{answer equivalence}. We show that these desiderata induce a one-to-one correspondence between valid answers and equivalence classes of clarifications, implying that any interpretation can be represented either by its answer or by the clarifications that uniquely express it. We leverage this correspondence to introduce \circleclar{}, a black-box method that estimates the set of answers available to a model by checking consistency along an answer--clarification--answer cycle. Across open-domain QA and executable Text-to-SQL, \circleclar{} achieves strong recovery of these answer sets. In QA, the recovered support yields the strongest overall performance in model-dependent ambiguity detection and enables effective selection among answering, clarifying, and abstaining. Together, these results demonstrate the practical value of a model-dependent view of ambiguity and its characterization through an answer-clarification correspondence.

☆ FACES: Fine-grained Gated Semantic Alignment for Multimodal Emotional Support Conversation

In recent years, with advances in multimodal interaction technologies and the growing demand for intelligent emotional support, Multimodal Emotional Support Conversation (MESC) has emerged as an important task. Linters are widely used in code maintenance and real-time IDE editing to surface lint alerts related to performance, style, error-prone, and other patterns in source files. ☆ A Strong Harness Narrows the Model Gap: A Comparative Evaluation for Legislative Document-to-XML Conversion EMNLP 2026
Palvika Bansal, Rittika Jindal, Ruichong Wang
Legal-content reproduction requires matching the source exactly in section hierarchy, verbatim text, citations, and amendment spans; a dropped citation or mislabeled amendment carries into the products lawyers rely on. We report an industrial pipeline converting U.S. state legislation into strict, schema-constrained XML, and hypothesize that its accuracy comes from harness engineering, the deterministic tooling built around a model, more than the model itself. Our controlled ablation crosses three harnesses (zero-shot, a single tool-using agent, and a decomposed multi-agent pipeline) with three Claude models on 153 gold bills from two jurisdictions. The harness narrows the model gap: the weakest model in the full pipeline beats the strongest model alone (0.948 versus 0.806, paired bootstrap p less than 0.001). Because the two agentic harnesses share identical tools, their gap comes from orchestration: decomposition plus the persisted state and per-agent context it brings. Across four scored dimensions, the model spread at zero-shot (0.68 to 0.81) narrows to 0.95 at the full pipeline; the residual sits almost entirely in citation normalization, where the canonical identifier is retrieved from an external authority rather than generated from the weights, so a deterministic lookup tool supplies the exactness that a larger model does not.

☆ Gray-Box Hallucination Detection in LLMs with Token Identities
Shaked Goldshtein, Yitzchak Grunbaum, Fabrizio Frasca, Guy Bar-Shalom, Haggai Maron
Large language models often produce fluent but factually incorrect output. Gray-box detectors identify such hallucinations from the token-probability statistics emitted during generation, avoiding the need for white-box access to hidden states that frontier models do not provide. These detectors, however, typically rely only on probability-derived representations and ignore the identities of the candidate tokens. They can therefore observe how uncertain the model is, but not what it is uncertain about. Consequently, they cannot distinguish between a model that is torn between "big" and "small" and one that is torn between "big" and "huge" when the two pairs receive the same probability distribution. We introduce GURI (Gray-box Uncertainty Representations with Identity), a lightweight gray-box hallucination detector that combines candidate-token probabilities with per-token vector identifiers. This exposes which alternatives the model considered while preserving the accessibility and transferability of the gray-box setting. Across three open-weight models and three question-answering benchmarks, GURI increases mean test ROC-AUC from 72.09% to 79.09% over a probability-only baseline. On the closed-source GPT-4.1 model, mean ROC-AUC increases from 67.39% to 71.84%. Among the three open-weight models, GURI outperforms transferred probability-only baseline in 17 of 18 directed cross-model transfers without fine-tuning. For the three open-weight models, cross-dataset transfer between HotpotQA and TriviaQA with the model held fixed improves mean ROC-AUC over transferred LOS-Net by 3.76 points.
☆ SoK: Grading Open-Text Legal Answers, from Law School Exams to Trustworthy Rubric Judges
Sebastian Nagl
Law students are examined through open-text answers: case solutions, essays and memos that apply the law to a set of facts. Such answers are expensive to grade, human graders disagree widely on them, and large language models (LLMs) now offer to grade them at scale, both in teaching and in the benchmarks that test legal AI. This paper systematizes how open-text legal answers can be evaluated. We derive eight desiderata from educational measurement and from the law that governs exams, among them validity, reliability, reasons, credit for defensible alternative solutions, robustness to gaming and human oversight. We then compare eight evaluation paradigms against them, from unstructured human grading to LLM judges with instance-specific rubrics and pairwise comparison. Three findings stand out. A rubric written for the task is the strongest lever for agreement between an LLM judge and human graders, but the reported figures answer different questions and rarely come with the human agreement they must be read against. The central validation gap lies where examination law is most demanding: defensible alternative solutions, follow-on errors, and the examiner's correction of model judgments. And rubric-centred grading is the most auditable family, because it makes the standard, its application and the final decision checkable. From this we derive the trustworthy rubric judge: an instance rubric that credits arguments and alternative paths,evidence checked in code, probes, repeated runs, calibration against independent human grades,logging, and an examiner who reads every script. We state what each safeguard rests on, what remains to be tested, and the uses the evidence supports today.

☆ Choosing the Number of Attention Heads from Intrinsic Dimension: A Small-Scale Study
Arjun Boya, Charan Sai Bandaru, Masthanaiah Pillimetla, Kavana Chidanandappa
The number of attention heads in a transformer is usually set by convention or grid search. We test a simple heuristic for setting it in advance: estimate the intrinsic dimension of token representations without labels, and use roughly that many heads. We estimate intrinsic dimension with a nested-dropout VAE trained on DistilBERT embeddings of WikiText-2, choosing the KL weight at the knee of the VAE's rate-distortion curve rather than by hand. This gives $k^*=9.2$ (95% CI [8.6, 9.8]). In a small 4-layer transformer trained from scratch, 10 heads and 32 heads are statistically equivalent on SST-2 and on MRPC (paired TOST over 10 seeds, $\pm1$ point margin), with 69% fewer attention parameters for the 10-head model. Training 10 heads from the start also slightly outperforms training 32 heads and pruning to 10 (0.6 points, $p=0.008$). The evidence is narrow: two classification tasks, one small architecture, no pretrained models, and no formal link between intrinsic dimension and head count. We present the result as a practical heuristic worth testing at larger scale, not as a general principle.

☆ AtomSearch: Evidence-Grounded Tree Search for Agent Skill Optimization
Jia Tong, Senyou Deng, Min Peng, Jianfeng Pan
Agent skills adapt frozen models through editable external instructions. Revising several rules at once makes changes difficult to inspect, while judging each local edit only by its immediate score can discard useful intermediate states. We introduce AtomSearch, which keeps revision actions local while using subsequent skill states to guide search. Contrastive atomic editing links each primitive rule edit to failed and successful executions, a behavioral hypothesis, and an intended scope. Skill-State Tree Search (STS) evaluates short edit compositions and selectively retains them as executable states that can be revisited and extended. Outcome memory supplies later proposals with observed repair and regression statistics. We evaluate AtomSearch across four tasks and five target models. With deepseek-v4-flash, it exceeds SkillOpt on three of four tasks, including a 9.68-percentage-point gain on LiveMathematicianBench, and SkillOpt's final skills contain 2.3-19.7 times as many characters. In 67% of completed searches, the best validation state passes through an ancestor that was not highest-scoring at its depth. These results show how local rule edits can support the exploration of useful revision paths and yield compact skills, with additional offline evaluations used to explore alternative states.

☆ Does Machine Translation Retain Media Bias?
Silvia V. Gargova, Tim Menzner, Jochen L. Leidner
Within the most recent two decades, machine translation (MT) has been advanced from a research area to a mature, deployed technology for prose text and most language pairs. However, language has different dimensions of meaning, and the alluded progress has primarily been demonstrated for propositional meaning. In this small exploratory study, we investigate whether one type of affective meaning, namely different types of linguistically expressed media bias, are retained under automatic translation for two language pairs, English and German as the source languages, and Bulgarian as the target language. We evaluate four state-of-the-art translation methods to answer the question whether and to what degree a loss of non-neutral language (specifically, 27 types of media bias) can occur as a result of applying MT. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first experimental study of MT media bias retention.
