☆ To Reason but Not to Stop: Understanding and Recovering Pruned Large Reasoning Models
Minh-Anh Nguyen, Nguyen T. Ha Lan, Xiyan Fu, Dung D. Le, Wei Lu
Weight pruning can degrade the accuracy of large reasoning models (LRMs), yet it remains unclear whether it damages reasoning itself. We find that much of this degradation stems from a failure to stop rather than to reason. As sparsity increases, pruned LRMs increasingly fail to terminate within the token budget, often after the correct answer has appeared in their reasoning, instead falling into repetitive loops, even though they still recognize where the dense model would stop. We further find that the hidden-state difference between terminating and looping continuations is consistent across pruned checkpoints, suggesting a shared direction associated with committing to an answer. Building on this, we propose Termination-Guided Activation Steering (TGAS), a training-free method that adds this direction to the residual stream at inference time. Across six LRMs, three pruning pipelines, and math and code benchmarks, TGAS improves termination and overall accuracy while reducing repetitive reasoning and generation length, showing that this failure is actionable.
☆ MemDuet: Learning Dual-Process Memory for Long-Context Reasoning
Jiangcheng Song, Haodong Jing, Yulin Mu, Lesong Tao, Jixin Wang, Jin Cui, Tianjing Ren, Runxuan Xie, Yongqiang Ma, Nanning Zheng
Reasoning over infinitely long documents is becoming a reality with recurrent memory agents. However, increasing context length exacerbates the trade-off between reasoning performance and inference efficiency. Inspired by Kahneman's account of fast and slow thinking, we propose MemDuet, a dual-process memory framework that learns to coordinate a small language model for fast, parallel evidence selection from raw context chunks (System 1) with a large language model for memory-update verification and memory updates in document order (System 2). We train MemDuet in three stages: supervised fine-tuning of System 1 for evidence selection, Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) of System 2 for verification and memory rewriting, and joint optimization of both policies to coordinate selection and updating for long-context reasoning. Experiments on diverse long-context reasoning benchmarks (RULER-HQA, LongBench v2, and 2WikiMultiHopQA) demonstrate a favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off as context length grows. Averaged across evaluation settings on the three datasets, MemDuet achieves an end-to-end inference speedup of 9.74x and an output-token reduction of 73.91% relative to RL-MemAgent.
☆ MolHop: Scaffold-Hopping Molecular Design with Agentic Reinforcement Learning ICLR 2027
Yanyi Su, Chenyan Wang, Yulei Gong, Yeyun Chen, Xiaohui Yang, Jun Cheng
Molecular design is often formulated as an optimization problem over existing molecular spaces. However, molecular optimization and the discovery of structurally distinct replacement molecules represent two fundamentally different search problems. LLM-based molecular design agents typically perform the former, conducting local refinement within the neighborhood of existing structures to improve target properties through structural modifications. In contrast, the latter requires exploration of a broader chemical space to discover molecules with entirely different scaffolds while preserving the functional characteristics of reference molecules. We propose MolHop, an agentic framework for cross-scaffold molecular discovery that enables models to navigate and edit within a pre-organized chemical space. MolHop organizes approximately 1.6 million molecular scaffolds from commercial molecules into a coarse-to-fine hierarchical action space. The agent selects new exploration regions through scaffold navigation actions and adjusts candidate properties through molecular editing. The framework maintains historical trajectory memory to support long-horizon search processes. On drug molecule and commercial compound tasks, connecting Grok-4.6 and DeepSeek-Flash-V4.1 to the MolHop framework substantially improves their cross-scaffold joint success rates. We develop MolHop-9B through agentic reinforcement learning, and MolHop-9B outperforms generative baselines including GPT-5.6-sol, Claude-5-Opus, and Gemini-3.1-Pro, while generating candidate molecules with larger scaffold differences from the reference molecules. These results demonstrate that effective molecular discovery beyond the traditional optimization paradigm requires structured chemical and action spaces, enabling agents to explore new structural regions rather than merely refine existing molecules.

☆ Not All Criteria Matter: Importance-Aware Criteria Weighting for Rubric-Based LLM Evaluation
Haocheng Yang, Licheng Pan, Yiming Dong, Xiaoxi Li, Yuan Lu, Zhenhao Chen, Jiajun Fan, Shuai Shao, Chunyuan Zheng, Hao Wang
Rubric-based evaluation assesses the responses of large language models (LLMs) by aggregating the verification results of explicit criteria, yet not all criteria matter equally. However, existing methods commonly aggregate the verification results with uniform weighting, which assigns minor and overlapping criteria the same weight as essential ones and thus produces misleading evaluation scores. Although some methods prompt an LLM to directly assign weights, direct weighting requires a holistic judgment over all criteria that LLMs struggle to make reliably. To address these limitations, we propose Importance-Aware Criteria Weighting (ICW), which derives the weights of criteria from pairwise importance comparisons made by an LLM. Specifically, ICW transforms the holistic judgment over all criteria into a series of pairwise importance comparisons, checks and revises inconsistent comparisons, and derives the weights from the principal eigenvector of the comparison matrix. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ICW consistently outperforms uniform weighting and competitive weighting methods, and translates into better policy models in downstream reinforcement learning.
☆ Beyond Benchmark Accuracy: A Diagnostic Test for LLM Chemical Prediction ICLR 2027
Yanyi Su, Yu Zeng, Hongshuai Wang, Chenyan Wang, Zhifeng Gao, Xiaohui Yang
LLMs have achieved strong performance on chemical prediction benchmarks. However, existing evaluation methods typically provide target entity identity information directly, making it difficult to determine whether model predictions arise from transferable structure-property reasoning capabilities or rely on target-related contextual information or memory retrieval. We propose the Identity-Blinded Reasoning Diagnostic framework to evaluate chemical prediction models when target entity identity information is hidden. In this framework, the target is no longer directly defined by its entity identity, but instead by a known source entity and an explicit structural edit operation. This setting preserves the information required to determine the target while reducing the opportunity for models to access target identity cues. We evaluate this framework across six tasks spanning odor prediction, molecular property prediction, retrosynthesis, and reaction yield prediction. The results show that models generally achieve better prediction performance when source and target entity properties are consistent. Providing explicit source entity labels further strengthens this dependence. Moreover, while keeping the target entity, structural change, and ground-truth label unchanged, replacing only the source entity label causes some model predictions to systematically shift toward the substituted source labels. These results indicate that high benchmark accuracy in chemical prediction tasks does not necessarily imply that LLMs possess reliable structure-property reasoning capabilities. Our diagnostic framework provides a controlled evaluation approach for testing whether models can genuinely update predictions according to entity changes. The framework can be extended to other AI for Science tasks, particularly those where targets can be defined through transformations of known entities.

☆ Frustratingly Easy Majority Voting Using Length Conditioning
Fiorenzo Parascandolo, Enver Sangineto, Xiaoyi Yu, Wenhui Tan, Jipei He, Ruihua Song, Rita Cucchiara
Majority Voting (MV) is a simple and widely used strategy which improves the reasoning accuracy of Large Language Models (LLMs) by allocating additional inference-time computation. Specifically, in MV multiple Chain-of-Thoughts (CoTs) are independently sampled and the most frequent final answer is selected. In this work, we propose Length-Conditioned Majority Voting (LCMV), a Test-Time Training approach which improves MV leveraging an empirical observation that has recently been established in several studies: longer CoTs tend to be associated with a lower probability of correctness. We formulate this inductive bias as a length-conditioned prior probability, which is modeled by a simple logistic function. Moreover, we adapt this prior at test time using only unlabeled testing data and without fine-tuning the LLM. Finally, the adapted prior is used to weight the vote of each sampled answer. Extensive experiments across multiple LLMs and reasoning benchmarks show that LCMV consistently improves over both standard MV and existing length-based and confidence-based aggregation strategies, while retaining the simplicity and model-agnostic nature of MV. Our code is available at: https://github.com/aimagelab/LCMV.
☆ RCCM: Diagnosing Gains and Harms in Reasoning-Prompt Transfer
Yuqi Zhang, Di Zhang
A reasoning prompt can improve one deployment and degrade another. RCCM (Reasoning-Control Capability Mismatch) examines these reversals by pairing answers to the same questions with records of how generation ends. Across five open-weight deployments and nine strategies, aggregate accuracy hides substantial question-level gains and harms; a common-budget MATH500 evaluation examines transfer on a separate sample. On 60 additional questions, concise reasoning improves Phi's automatic answer matching at a 2,048-token cap across three collections, but sharply reduces Qwen's. Author-reviewed final-answer commitments give a smaller, statistically unresolved Phi gain. Reserving an answer stage also improves matching, with additional input and call costs. These results show why a prompt benefit must be interpreted alongside its execution and evaluation: matching a reference answer need not mean delivering it, and an apparent compression benefit may admit another explanation. RCCM records this evidence for reproducible transfer comparisons.

☆ Rationale Distribution Distillation via Flow Matching for Distributional Continuous Latent Reasoning
Mingyeong Kim, Gyusang Cho, Juho Lee, Giung Nam
Sampling multiple chain-of-thought rationales from a language model can uncover correct solutions missed by a single attempt. However, these rationales are typically lengthy, making repeated autoregressive generation costly. Continuous latent reasoning offers a compact alternative, but point-valued methods condition repeated answer sampling on a single rationale, which can limit exploration of diverse paths and answer coverage. We thus propose rationale distribution distillation (RDD), training a conditional flow over fixed-length latent sequences by distilling from multiple textual reasoning paths. Furthermore, we expand the distillation targets through within-query mixup, interpolating between complete latent rationales for the same query. The flow nonautoregressively generates the entire latent sequence, and a frozen language model autoregressively decodes answers from sampled rationale contexts. Empirically, the resulting distributional reasoner achieves higher answer coverage than the evaluated point-valued latent reasoning baselines, particularly on advanced mathematical tasks.
☆ SEA-HELM Audio: Evaluating Multilingual ASR-LLM Pipeline Performance Across SEA-HELM Subsets
Yixian Tan, Aulia Adila, Jonathan Heng, Kunat Pipatanakul, Donghang Wu, Anocha Sutaveephamochanon
We introduce SEA-HELM Audio, a Southeast Asian (SEA) multilingual evaluation framework that extends the text-based SEA-HELM benchmark to assess performance degradation in cascaded automatic speech recognition and large language model (ASR-LLM) pipelines. Using synthesized speech, we compare speech-input and text-input performance across five languages (Chinese, English, Malay, Indonesian, and Tamil) on multiple-choice reasoning and instruction-following tasks. We evaluate open-weight and commercial ASR-LLM systems using identical verifiers and quantify the speech gap as the difference between text and speech accuracy. Results show substantial degradation, with accuracy drops of 9.00 to 18.50 percentage points for multiple-choice reasoning and 20.00 to 42.86 points for instruction following. Tamil exhibits the largest degradation, exceeding 40 percentage points. Our findings highlight the limitations of text-only benchmarks in predicting voice-agent reliability and demonstrate the value of SEA-HELM Audio for evaluating multilingual voice-agent systems.
♻ ☆ Does Pretraining Teach Chemical Language Models Chemistry?
Leif Sieben, Yoel Zimmermann
Chemical language models (CLMs) are pretrained on large corpora of molecules with the aim of learning representations that transfer to diverse downstream tasks. Here, we systematically study the effects of pretraining using a series of such CLMs across 64 molecular property prediction datasets. We find that the downstream gains of pretraining in CLMs do not scale and performance saturates below gradient-boosting baselines. Across the regimes evaluated, only supervised pretraining on traditional physico-chemical descriptors consistently improves downstream performance for the CLMs tested whereas some forms of pretraining result in models worse than a randomly initialized encoder. While models can resolve molecular structure well, they do so no better than a randomly initialized model, suggesting that, at best, CLMs learn descriptor-like properties in addition to low-level token statistics during pretraining, but surprisingly little chemistry beyond what classical molecular features already capture.
♻ ☆ What Does a Discrete Diffusion Model Learn? One Reverse Process in Three Coordinates NeurIPS 2026
Rodrigo Casado Noguerales, Bernhard Schölkopf, Thomas Hofmann, Aran Raoufi
What does a discrete diffusion model actually learn? The literature trains denoisers, bridge plug-ins, and concrete scores, each deriving its own ELBO objective under different conventions. This fragmentation makes the object being learned unclear and ELBO values difficult to interpret and compare. All three methods learn the same object in different coordinates: the exact time-reversal of the noising process. We first prove the Oracle Distance identity: for any noising process, the negative ELBO is not just a bound on the likelihood; it equals the data entropy plus the path KL divergence from the reversed noising process to the learned one. Next, for token-factorizing noise, we show that denoiser, bridge plug-in, and concrete score are three coordinates of this reverse process, derive exact conversions between them, and identify the probability distribution learned by each. In particular, contrary to its standard interpretation, the bridge plug-in learns not a denoiser but the cavity distribution: the clean token given the noisy sequence excluding its own position. We confirm the theory by training all three coordinates with masked, uniform, and GIDD kernels on OpenWebText under a shared recipe, and by training uniform-diffusion solvers on four logic tasks. The learned coordinates agree after conversion, while confusing the cavity with the denoiser can severely degrade sampling quality. Together with exact boundary-term formulas, closed-form NELBO calibrations at initialization, and a clock interpretation of time schedules, these results provide a conceptual framework and a mechanical recipe for deriving, implementing, and comparing discrete diffusion processes.

♻ ☆ Old Ideas, Novel Problems: The Instability of LLM-Based Novelty Evaluation
Noy Sternlicht, Simra Shahid, Peter Jansen, Daniel S. Weld, Pao Siangliulue, Tom Hope
AI-based ideation systems are often evaluated on the novelty of their ideas, and that judgment is increasingly delegated to large language models. Such judges are typically built ad hoc and validated, if at all, on human-authored papers rather than on the generated ideas they are meant to score. So, how do novelty judges perform? Not well. We present a systematic controlled study of novelty evaluation design choices. We build an evaluation set automatically, mining OpenReview for passages where reviewers explicitly affirm or dispute a paper's originality and keeping only submissions with unanimous agreement at the extremes of their research area; we pair these with ideas from a vanilla LLM generator. Across six judges, we find that seemingly small prompt design choices have large consequences; e.g., simply telling the judge that reviewers found one idea novel and the other not can change its verdict on more than half of the identical idea pairs it is shown, shifting pairwise accuracy by over 50 points and occasionally pushing it below chance. The same change helps one judge and hurts another. Retrieval and larger reasoning budgets help little, and two purpose-built novelty evaluators are outperformed by our cheapest prompted baseline. These results raise questions about reported novelty gains of automated ideation systems, and call for robust novelty evaluation methods.
♻ ☆ From Clicks to Intent: Cross-Platform Session Embeddings with LLM-Distilled Taxonomy for Financial Services Recommendations
Dianjing Fan, Yao Li, Kyaw Hpone Myint, Dwipam Katariya, Alexandre G. R. Day, Pranab Mohanty, Giri Iyengar
Sequential user behavior modeling is widely adopted in industrial recommender systems; however, significant gaps remain in financial services, where pre-login web interactions and authenticated in-app experiences differ drastically. Specifically, pre-login web users typically explore new products, whereas logged-in app users focus on account servicing. Due to the challenge of cross-channel entity resolution (e.g., matching anonymous web sessions to authenticated mobile accounts), web-based intent signals remain underutilized for post-authentication personalization. Existing methods for capturing web-based intent are often ad-hoc and narrow, lacking the flexibility to support both quantitative downstream recommendations and qualitative understanding at scale. In this work, we propose a scalable and dual-purpose intent prediction framework for web-based interactions and demonstrate its applicability for personalization. Our approach transforms raw web clickstreams into two outputs: a self-supervised Transformer encodes multi-modal clickstreams into a compact session embedding, while an LLM-based taxonomy generation and distillation pipeline produces interpretable intent labels. Our system demonstrates that self-supervised clickstream representations combined with LLM-distilled taxonomies can jointly serve quantitative tasks and qualitative understanding in production: on the mobile homepage tile ranking task, the session embedding improves macro Recall@1 by 1.88% and reduces Log Loss by 13.38% over production baselines. On the user conversion prediction task, the embedding outperforms the LLM labels by 4.3% on micro F1, while the distillation layer delivers interpretable labels at ultra-low latency with only a 7% performance drop.

♻ ☆ Ouvia: A User-centered Framework for Measuring Usability of Speech Translation in Real-World Communication Scenarios EMNLP 2026
Giuseppe Attanasio, Beatrice Savoldi, Daniel Chechelnitsky, Matteo Negri, Marine Carpuat, Maarten Sap, André F. T. Martins
Speech translation (ST) is increasingly adopted in user applications, yet its evaluation largely focuses on decontextualized testbeds and holistic quality, rather than end users' communication needs. We introduce Ouvia, an evaluation framework for measuring user-perceived usability of speech translation outputs in real-world settings. Ouvia focuses on one-to-one communication: an English speaker needs to convey a request to a Portuguese speaker, and the message is automatically translated. Through a custom web app and multi-phase study design, we collect more than 1,750 such interactions in healthcare and everyday situations, mediated by four ST systems, involving speakers from three English dialects and two genders. We find that modern ST serves people only to a limited extent---only around half of interactions are rated as usable---with significant gaps in reported usability across demographic groups. Moreover, among quality metrics, we find that QA-based evaluation is a substantially stronger predictor of real-world usability than standard approaches. Together, these findings stress the importance of situated, user-centered evaluation frameworks that go beyond holistic quality scores and attend to who the technology serves---and how well.

♻ ☆ RPRO: Ranked Preference Reinforcement Optimization for Enhancing Medical QA and Diagnostic Reasoning
Chia-Hsuan Hsu, Jun-En Ding, Hsin-Ling Hsu, Chih-Ho Hsu, Li-Hung Yao, Chun-Chieh Liao, Feng Liu, Fang-Ming Hung
Medical question answering requires advanced reasoning that integrates domain knowledge with logical inference. However, existing large language models (LLMs) often generate reasoning chains that lack factual accuracy and clinical reliability. We propose Ranked Preference Reinforcement Optimization (RPRO), a novel framework that combines reinforcement learning with preference-driven reasoning refinement to enhance clinical chain-of-thought (CoT) performance. RPRO distinguishes itself from prior approaches by employing task-adaptive reasoning templates and a probabilistic evaluation mechanism that aligns model outputs with established clinical workflows, while automatically identifying and correcting low-quality reasoning chains. Unlike traditional pairwise preference methods, RPRO introduces a groupwise ranking optimization based on the Bradley--Terry model and incorporates KL-divergence regularization for stable training. Experiments on PubMedQA, MedQA-USMLE, and a real-world clinical dataset from Far Eastern Memorial Hospital (FEMH) demonstrate consistent improvements over strong baselines. Remarkably, our 2B-parameter model outperforms much larger 7B--20B models, including medical-specialized variants. These findings demonstrate that combining preference optimization with quality-driven refinement provides a scalable and clinically grounded approach to building more reliable medical LLMs.
♻ ☆ HarnessSQL: Harness-Native Training for SQL Agents in Realistic Database Environments
Haolin Yang, Jipeng Zhang, Zhiqiang Qian, Jian Xie, Shuaishuai Gong, Yike Guo, Sirui Han
Text-to-SQL models are commonly trained to map questions directly to static queries, whereas real-world database agents operate through stateful, multi-turn interaction with live databases -- inspecting schemas, executing probe queries, diagnosing errors, and revising hypotheses. This creates a critical train-deploy mismatch, as the execution harness that mediates this interaction is introduced only at inference time. To bridge this gap, we propose HarnessSQL, a harness-native post-training framework that preserves the full interaction structure throughout both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. HarnessSQL builds isolated, executable database environments paired with hidden execution oracles, rolls out teachers directly inside the target SQL harness, and retains only verified trajectories for full-sequence SFT, followed by execution-reward RL. Across Spider 2.0-SQLite, HarnessSQL dramatically boosts the execution accuracy of compact models, raising Qwen3-8B from 15.5% to 45.2% and Qwen3-14B from 22.2% to 54.8%, while transferring effectively to out-of-distribution interactive benchmarks such as BIRD-Interact and LiveSQLBench. Our findings demonstrate that training database agents directly within their execution harness is essential for mastering complex, long-horizon database workflows.
♻ ☆ Readout Scaling Shapes Sparse Feature Recovery
Brian Xiao, Sarah Wilson
Readout scaling changes what a sparse autoencoder (SAE) learns, but the benefit of removing it depends on the data. We use readout-canonicalized decision-space (RC-DS) coordinates to remove singular-value gains while preserving retained decision information. At a fixed readout condition number, changing the generating distribution reverses which training coordinates recover known factors best. In our controlled coordinate-matching study, recovery is highest when training restores the space in which the factors were generated. When factors are generated before readout scaling, RC-DS increases mean sparse-factor recovery from 0.506 to 0.913 at condition number 100, compared with centered-logit training under the same budget. An exact wrapper reproduces RC-DS training to numerical precision, isolating training geometry rather than additional information. In neural policies, RC-DS improves mean logit reconstruction under the tested budgets. On three fixed language-model checkpoints, RC-DS has higher mean gain-free reconstruction in all 18 tested task-sparsity settings, while centered-logit SAEs reconstruct output logits better in 17. Small-label-head controls show that longer training reduces the reconstruction gap from 0.0198 to 0.0016. Together, these results show how readout scaling, the data distribution, and training shape sparse learning. RC-DS provides an information-preserving method for studying and controlling the readout's contribution. Separate label-detection and steering tests distinguish these reconstruction effects from task-specific feature usefulness.
♻ ☆ ConRAG: Consensus-Driven Evidence Flow for Multi-Hop Question Answering
Yikai Zhu, Kunfeng Chen, Qihuang Zhong, Juhua Liu, Bo Du
Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has emerged as a promising paradigm for enhancing large language models (LLMs) on multi-hop question answering (QA), which requires reasoning over evidence from multiple documents. However, current multi-hop RAG approaches still struggle with evidence selection and information transfer, i.e., retrieving irrelevant passages or failing to pass intermediate information to subsequent reasoning steps, thereby hindering coherent multi-hop reasoning. To this end, we propose ConRAG, a consensus-driven evidence flow framework that effectively boosts LLM performance on complex multi-hop QA. In short, the core of ConRAG is to select relevant evidence via consensus-driven multi-view ranking, and leverage a slot-based mechanism to transfer intermediate information between dependent reasoning steps. Extensive experiments across three multi-hop QA benchmarks show that ConRAG surpasses the baselines by a clear margin. Encouragingly, on Gemma-4-31B-it, ConRAG outperforms the strongest baseline by 5.8 points in average score, while reducing total end-to-end runtime and token consumption by 75.9% and 67.3%, respectively.
♻ ☆ Evaluating LLMs as Agentic Workflow Executors in Real-World Customer Support Skills
Md Tahmid Rahman Laskar, Gundeep Singh, Xue-Yong Fu, Karol Chang, Kevin Sanders, Shi Zong, Tania Habib, Julien Bouvier Tremblay, Shayna Gardiner, Harsh Saini, Matthias Lee, Elena Khasanova, Quinten McNamara, Shashi Bhushan TN
Enterprise customer-support agents increasingly rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) to execute bounded, tool-using workflows. We study this problem through the lens of Skill LLMs: specialized executors that operate within a selected skill context and must complete a narrow, stateful workflow under policy and tool constraints. We present a benchmark construction pipeline that mines recurrent workflows from real-world transcripts, converts them into structured skill descriptions, maps them to tool requirements, simulates environment state with Model Context Protocol (MCP)-compatible mocks, and generates multi-turn tool-using dialogues. Using this benchmark, we conduct a large-scale zero-shot evaluation of 17 language models spanning closed model families and deployment-oriented open-weight models from 1.7B to 14B parameters, each judged independently by five strong LLM judges, plus a deterministic rule-based evaluator that checks tool coverage, hallucinated tool use, and unsupported claims. We find a consistent ranking in which GPT-5.4 leads by a wide margin and QWEN3.5-9B is the strongest open-weight model (below 20B), outperforming several closed baselines under every judge. We also find that judge choice is not a negligible methodological detail: pairwise judge agreement on the pass/fail verdict averages Kappa=0.524 across 10 judge pairs. These results suggest that comprehensive zero-shot evaluation of skill execution requires judge reliability as carefully as model capability.
♻ ☆ Reward Design for Physical Reasoning in Vision-Language Models
Derek Lilienthal, Manisha Mukherjee, Sameera Horawalavithana
Physical reasoning over images requires integrating perception, domain knowledge, and multi-step symbolic inference, yet Vision-Language Models (VLMs) fall far short of human performance on physics benchmarks. Reinforcement learning can improve reasoning via reward signals, but it remains unclear whether these signals must come from outside the model or can be derived from the model itself. We compare external rewards, which rely on an outside verifier (rule-based format and answer-correctness checks, or an LLM-judge rubric), with an internal attention-based reward computed from the model's own attention over image regions, requiring no labels or judge. We find that external rewards drive most of the accuracy gains, while preserving more coherent reasoning when rewarded for reasoning tokens. The internal attention-based reward, provides an annotation-free grounding signal that improves spatial-relation reasoning and is complementary to external rewards: adding it to format and accuracy rewards, at no additional annotation cost, improves average accuracy on Molmo~2 while generalizing to out-of-distribution tasks. Our results suggest that internal attention rewards can supplement external supervision and lead to improved physical reasoning.
♻ ☆ Language Steering for Multilingual In-Context Learning
Neeraja Kirtane, Kuan-Hao Huang
If multilingual language model representations contain separable language-associated structure, then shifting between languages may require only simple activation-space interventions. To test this hypothesis, we study multilingual in-context learning (ICL) as a controlled setting: few-shot demonstrations are provided in English while the test query is in a target language, creating a clear cross-lingual transfer challenge. We propose language vectors, which are computed as mean activation differences between parallel source and target language examples at a particular layer and applied as inference-time offsets to the model's hidden states. Across three tasks spanning 19 languages and three models, our method improves over English few-shot prompting without any parameter updates. Beyond performance, the vectors encode interpretable linguistic structure: closely related languages form tight clusters, and vectors transfer effectively across tasks and languages, with closely related languages' vectors recovering more of the gain than distant ones. Together, these findings suggest that language occupies structured directions in multilingual activation space, and that inference-time interventions on English demonstrations can narrow the cross-lingual ICL gap.
♻ ☆ Reclaiming Low-Quality Instruction Data with Rule-Guided LLM Reconstruction
Zile Yang, Ling Li, Na Di, Jinlong Pang, Yao Zhou, Hao Cheng, Bo Han, Jiaheng Wei
Data selection for supervised fine-tuning (SFT) often discards low-scoring instruction examples that may retain useful supervision. We propose a rule-guided LLM reconstruction framework to reclaim this information. The framework identifies low-scoring samples through LLM-based rating and score correction, selects semantically representative sources, and reconstructs instruction-response pairs through strategy-guided generation, criteria-based validation, and failure-guided repair. From 123,786 low-scoring samples, it constructs 54,888 training examples. Across three 7B/8B base LLMs, the reconstructed data achieves higher average downstream performance than the low-scoring source set and all evaluated data-selection baselines. Despite using substantially fewer examples than the full source dataset (approximately 300K), it achieves higher reported average scores than full-data training across all three base models. Stepwise ablations identify rule-guided reconstruction as the largest source of observed improvements over direct fusion, while a manual audit reveals remaining limitations in source integration and answer faithfulness. These findings support reconstruction as a complementary approach to selection-based SFT data curation.
♻ ☆ Terminal Agents Suffice for Enterprise Automation
Patrice Bechard, Orlando Marquez Ayala, Emily Chen, Jordan Skelton, Sagar Davasam, Srinivas Sunkara, Vikas Yadav, Sai Rajeswar
There has been growing interest in building agents that can interact with digital platforms to execute meaningful enterprise tasks autonomously. Among the approaches explored are tool-augmented agents built on abstractions such as Model Context Protocol (MCP) and web agents that operate through graphical interfaces. Yet, it remains unclear whether such complex agentic systems are necessary given their cost and operational overhead. We argue that a coding agent equipped only with a terminal and a filesystem can solve many enterprise tasks more effectively by interacting directly with platform APIs. We evaluate this hypothesis across diverse real-world systems and show that these low-level terminal agents match or outperform more complex agent architectures at a fraction of the cost. Our findings suggest that simple, flexible programmatic interfaces combined with strong foundation models should be the backbone of enterprise automation.

♻ ☆ Explain Like I'm 5 or Whatever I Choose: Evaluating the Potential for Interactive Complexity in Language Model Responses
Indu Panigrahi, Tal August
Given the increased use of large language models (LLMs) for reading and seeking information and the varying needs and backgrounds of users, direct manipulation of text can be a valuable mechanism for end-user control. ♻ ☆ A Computational Framework for Modelling Organisation-Level Semantic Identity from Longitudinal Textual Data
Brinda Murali Krishna, Oktay Karakuş, Can Eyüpoğlu
Can longitudinal texts produced by organisationally affiliated contributors reveal collective semantic differentiation and change without obscuring differences among those contributors? We develop a computational social science framework that combines established semantic and graph measures into interpretable organisational fingerprints and temporal profiles. We use \emph{organisation-level semantic identity} to describe a corpus-dependent interpretation of recurring and changing features of affiliated textual output, rather than corporate culture or managerial intention. Drawing on an inherited semantic landscape, we examine 1,289 songs by 126 artists affiliated with four K-pop entertainment companies between 1997 and 2023. The evidence for differentiation is selective. After correction, all three novelty-related measures show artist-level differences, whereas the diversity and connectivity measures tested do not; only two company--community enrichments remain significant. Temporal trajectories reveal further distinctions between portfolios. Robustness analyses support the presence of continuous semantic structure more strongly than precise rankings. Together, these findings show how collective regularities can coexist with shared content, contributor heterogeneity and change over time, while leaving cross-domain generality and broader organisational construct validity unresolved.
♻ ☆ ProReviewer: Evidence-Driven Investigation for Scientific Peer Review
Haishuo Fang, Yue Feng, Iryna Gurevych
Effective peer review requires adaptive investigation that responds to evidence emerging from each paper. Existing LLM-based reviewers largely organize the review process around predefined workflows, leaving limited scope for emerging evidence to reshape investigation. We propose ProReviewer, an evidence-driven review agent that formulates reviewing as a Markov Decision Process, with a structured review log forming the core of its state. At each step, the agent uses accumulated findings to decide what to inspect next and when to conclude. As new evidence emerges, it updates the log by revising claim statuses, resolving open questions, and recording new findings. Each point in the final review cites supporting log entries, making its judgments traceable to the recorded evidence. On 1,000 held-out ICLR 2026 papers, the trained 8B ProReviewer achieves the highest average score among all compared methods, including those built on a 397B model; human evaluators prefer its reviews over all baselines on three of four review-quality dimensions.
♻ ☆ Latent Preference Modeling for Multi-Session Personalized Tool Calling
Yejin Yoon, Minseo Kim, Taeuk Kim
Users often omit essential details in their requests to LLM-based agents, resulting in under-specified inputs for tool use. This poses a fundamental challenge for tool-augmented agents, as API execution typically requires complete arguments, highlighting the need for personalized tool calling. To study this problem, we present Multi-Session Personalized Tool Calling (MPT), a benchmark comprising 4,695 instances over 459 multi-session interaction histories that cover three challenges: Preference Recall, Induction, and Transfer. We further propose PRefine, a test-time memory method that maintains the user's latent preference as a textual hypothesis revised through a generate--verify--refine loop. Across five LLMs, existing memory systems often fail to improve on full-history prompting; PRefine outperforms all baselines and alone surpasses it on Preference Transfer. These results indicate that memory for personalized agents must abstract behavior into preferences, rather than simply archive it.

♻ ☆ Refusing Intent, Not Form: Wrapper-Based Intent-Group Supervision for LLM Safety
Ping Wu, Haibo Tong, Feifei Zhao, Han Shen, Yu Shi, Yilin Zhao, Sicheng Shen, Guobin Shen, Yun Luo, Yi Zeng
A common way to improve jailbreak robustness is to train on harmful requests rewritten as role-play, translation, or other forms. When used only for harmful requests, these forms may become refusal shortcuts, weakening unseen-attack robustness and increasing benign over-refusal. We propose Wrapper-Based Intent-Form Augmentation (WIFA), which applies shared wrapper families to harmful and benign requests and groups variants by intent, encouraging decisions based on underlying intent rather than wrapper cues. WIFA supports two independent routes: WIFA-Boost prioritizes safety through two-stage training, while A-GCRT enforces consistent decisions across wrappers to mitigate over-refusal. Across three model families and two harmful training sources, both routes consistently improve safety while largely retaining capability. On Qwen--AdvBench, WIFA-Boost raises SB-avg5 from 22.1\% to 63.7\%, the best among compared methods; A-GCRT raises it to 46.7\% while reducing OR-Bench over-refusal from 25.7\% to 17.4\%, unlike reproduced defenses whose safety gains increase over-refusal. Across three AdvBench-trained backbones, both routes substantially reduce ASR on 15 unseen attack families and under model-adaptive GCG and RandomSearch. Overall, WIFA provides reusable intent-group supervision across models and training sources, supporting high safety through WIFA-Boost and substantial safety gains with less unnecessary refusal through A-GCRT.

♻ ☆ AlphaRLM: Aligning Language Models for Literature-Grounded Symbolic Factor Discovery
Jie Huang, Yuecheng Hong, Yao Chen, Hao Zhong, Linfeng Xiao, Xudong Liu, Hao Xia, Jianxuan Xin
Formulaic alpha mining seeks to discover executable expressions that capture predictive market signals in a vast, noisy search space. Recent LLM-based approaches use prompting, iterative search, or seed-factor evolution, leaving direct factor construction through policy learning less explored. To address this, we introduce AlphaRLM, a reinforcement-learning framework that trains LLMs to construct executable symbolic factors from financial literature without input factor expressions. AlphaRLM incorporates Literature-Guided Operator Assembly (LEGO), which conditions factor construction on literature-derived ideas and executable operators, providing semantic priors for exploration. It further aligns the generation policy with market feedback through executable-validity and grouped-return-separation rewards that capture non-monotonic structures across factor quantiles. Extensive experiments on CSI 300, CSI 1000, and S&P 500 show that AlphaRLM outperforms strong factor-mining and LLM-based baselines in annualized excess return. Notably, the 8B model achieves strong cross-market performance, highlighting the promise of literature-conditioned, market-aligned factor generation.

♻ ☆ TreeGraft: Adaptive Multi-Drafter Grafting for Tree-Based Speculative Decoding
Jiaming Fan, Daming Cao, Canchen Huang, Jiale Fu, Jin Zhang, Junjie Gao, Kai Yang, Xiangzhong Luo, Xu Yang
Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference through a draft-then-verify paradigm. Building on this, tree-structured methods improve inference by organizing proposals into multiple candidate paths, increasing the accepted length. However, existing tree-structured methods use a single drafter for all drafting steps, creating a dilemma: a smaller drafter is fast but yields lower-quality trees, whereas a larger drafter improves tree quality but suffers from high latency. To address this, we propose TreeGraft, a multi-drafter framework in which drafters of different costs jointly construct a shared draft tree. TreeGraft uses the stronger drafter to rescore candidates by updating scores assigned by the weaker drafter, reselect grafting positions, and recover promising paths left unexplored. It also integrates stronger drafter expansions non-destructively, preserving existing branches that may still be accepted by the target model. Together, these designs improve the quality of the shared draft tree. To control the drafting cost, TreeGraft introduces a lightweight scheduler distilled from an offline value system to decide when to call the stronger drafter. Across 10 model pairs and 6 benchmarks, TreeGraft outperforms the better of the two fixed single-drafter endpoint strategies by 15.1% on average, reaching a maximum gain of 26.6%. Our code is available at https://github.com/fjm9933/TreeGraft.
♻ ☆ Semantic Communities and Boundary-Spanning Lyrics in K-pop: A Graph-Based Unsupervised Analysis
Oktay Karakuş, Can Eyüpoğlu
We examine how lyric representation shapes computational similarity using four 384-dimensional multilingual MiniLM representations of 7,891 K-pop songs by 1,456 artists from charts spanning 2000 to October 2023. Whole-song truncated embeddings provide the strongest standardized separation between neighbouring and random song pairs (1.995), compared with 1.931 for chunked documents, although chunked representations show greater adjusted Rand agreement and line-based representations yield higher modularity. Using whole-song embeddings, we construct a weighted cosine nearest-neighbour graph ($k=30$). A representative Louvain partition identifies 21 operational communities, without a corresponding robust plateau in the Markov Stability analysis. AI-assisted examination of selected lyrics and terms suggests overlapping patterns of memory, separation, longing, self-reflection, attraction, and performance. Community membership is associated with character-script composition (Cramér's $V=0.294$; permutation $p=0.001$). High-participation songs exhibit broader cross-community connectivity, while participation has weak negative associations with embedding-relative novelty. These findings show how representation choices and textual characteristics influence the network organization of a cultural corpus, subject to limitations from truncation, graph construction, and the absence of independent human semantic validation.
♻ ☆ Algorithmic Unverifiability of Safety for Fixed and Recursively Self-Improving Systems
Jose Pascual Gumbau Mezquita
Existing impossibility results for AI verification concern a fixed system. Self-modifying systems (agents that rewrite their own code, recursive self-improvement loops) raise a different question: can a safety property that holds now be certified, in advance, to keep holding after the system rewrites itself? We model one step of self-modification as a total computable transformation of code, and preservation as 'safe now and safe after the step'. If the transformation depends only on behaviour, preservation is Rice's theorem one level up; if it reads the code, as self-modification does, preservation is no longer semantic, yet at least as hard as the halting problem, by the same s-m-n reduction carried out inside a class of behaviourally identical programs. One step is never harder than the property; persistence along the whole trajectory can be one arithmetical level higher. No tower of supervisors lowers the problem; every total supervisor errs on a set of systems it cannot locate. Every faithful scheme certifying on finite behavioural evidence admits, for properties not settled by any finite set of inputs, an evolution trace it certifies at every stage while the property is violated. Statically, we record the classical limits (Rice, Goedel, Trakhtenbrot) and add the resource face: over succinctly described finite environments, verifying a policy against an adversary is coNP-complete and synthesising one PSPACE-complete. These limits do not say self-modifying systems are unsafe; they locate where algorithmic guarantees break, at the expressivity a general system needs: Turing-completeness and unrestricted self-modification. What survives is exact: a monitor detects the first violation of a safety property, and no sound procedure certifies all safe systems; certification requires restricting rewrites by design; and a frozen reference keeps the question well posed.
comment: v4: restructured and shortened; static limits condensed into one context section; new remarks on properties that change and on positive/negative disruption; related work extended (Harrison-Ruzzo-Ullman, Cohen, Bonfante-Marion-Reynaud-Plantey, Wang et al., Cai-Shao-Vaynberg); supervision result restated; cites companion arXiv:2609.11326v3
♻ ☆ Hidden in Plain Sight: Advancing Harmful Content Understanding via Perception-Aware Adversarial Self-Play
Yisong Xiao, Benlei Cui, Haiwen Hong, Shiji Zhao, Zonghao Ying, Yaoyuan Zhang, Longtao Huang, Aishan Liu, Hui Xue, Ting Ma, Xianglong Liu
Despite significant advances in language understanding, Large Language Models (LLMs) can still miss meaning hidden in plain sight, failing to identify harmful intent in expressions readily understood by humans. Malicious users exploit this gap by strategically disguising harmful content to evade automated moderation, posing a serious threat to online platform safety. The vulnerability reflects a fundamental cognitive asymmetry between human readers, who integrate contextual, phonetic, and visual cues to decipher the intent behind deliberate obfuscation, and text-based LLMs, which rely primarily on token sequences and have limited exposure to evolving evasions. To bridge this asymmetry, we propose PASP (Perception-Aware Adversarial Self-Play), which combines multi-perceptual views with adversarial self-play to improve understanding of harmful intent as evasions evolve. Specifically, PASP constructs textual, phonetic, and visual views of each input, preserving its original context while exposing pronunciation and glyph cues obscured in token space. During adversarial self-play, an attacker uses defender feedback to generate intent-preserving evasions, while the defender is trained using an intent-invariance reward to understand harmful intent across changing surface forms. Extensive experiments show that PASP reduces adaptive attack success rates by up to 73.71% and 74.49% on ChineseHarm-Bench and EvoHarmBench, respectively, preserves model capabilities with minimal degradation, and effectively interprets real-world evasive expressions in e-commerce.
♻ ☆ IACM-RL: Intent-Aware Context Management and Reinforcement Learning for Complex Tool Invocation under Dynamic Intent Fluctuations
Dingwei Zhu, Jiahan Li, Chengjun Pan, Yunxian Yang, Yunbin Zhao, Yunke Zhang, Zhonghang Lu, Zhuohui Sheng, Chenhao Huang, Jiahang Lin, Yajie Yang, Junlin Shang, Shichun Liu, Yuhui Wang, Honglin Guo, Junjie Ye, Xin Guo, Jiazheng Zhang, Ming Zhang, Shihan Dou, Zhiheng Xi, Tao Gui, Qi Zhang, Xipeng Qiu, Xuanjing Huang
Executing long-horizon tool invocations in real-world environments is severely challenged by dynamic user intent noise. Existing methods attempt robustness via implicit history scanning or text compression, yet predominantly assume perfect instructions in simplistic scenarios. Inevitably, under fluctuating contexts, obsolete constraints dilute model attention, triggering intent deviation and repetitive API loops. To resolve this, we propose IACM-RL, a comprehensive framework for robust tool invocation. First, we introduce the DynamicIntent pipeline, synthesizing trajectories across several fine-grained fluctuation scenarios, paired with a five-dimensional diagnostic metric suite. Second, IACM-RL deploys a BeliefState-based Self-Generated Context Manager that proactively tracks shifting goals and isolates overwritten parameters using structural stale flags. To autonomously internalize this state-tracking capability, we optimize the policy using a hierarchical intent-driven reward alongside two auxiliary losses (CM extraction and state distillation). Experiments on DynamicIntent, BFCL-V3, and $\mathrmτ^2$-Bench demonstrate that IACM-RL outperforms baselines, reducing infinite loops and stale context errors while enhancing out-of-domain generalization.
♻ ☆ Review Rarely Beats the Stronger Model: Separating Verification from Capability Transfer in LLM Pipelines
Yirui Zhang, Yash Raj Shrestha
Qwen3-8B reviewing Llama-3.1-8B in math adds 33.2 points, yet Qwen3-8B alone is 6.4 points more accurate than the result. Reviewing another model's answer is usually judged by its gain over the generator. We show that this confuses verification, which adds accuracy beyond either model alone, with capability transfer, where a stronger reviewer supplies answers it could have given on its own. Measured against the stronger model alone, across 48 pairs of 12 open-weight models (3B to 72B) in math, knowledge, code and reasoning, review beats it in only 9 of 96 review directions, and does worse the larger the capability gap. The reason is anchoring: the reviewer mostly follows the generator, discarding its own correct answers. Review costs 2.6 to 2.8 times as much as the stronger model alone, while a selector using the same two calls is 2.7 to 4.2 points more accurate than review and cheaper. Studies of verification should report the stronger model alone, not only the gain over the generator.

♻ ☆ Geometric Alignment without Functional Equivalence: A Layer-wise Analysis of the Speech-Text Modality Gap NeurIPS 2026
Ming-Hao Hsu, Xiaohai Tian, Jun Zhang, Lu Lu, Yuxuan Wang, Zhizheng Wu
End-to-end speech large language models (LLMs) often answer the same question less accurately from speech than from text, even when the spoken content matches the text prompt. Existing analyses study this modality gap through the geometry of representations and measure how closely speech representations align with text. Geometric alignment gives the gap a measurable form, but it does not show whether the model uses aligned representations in the same way. This work connects representation geometry with answer formation and asks whether geometric alignment implies functional equivalence. We compare cross-layer Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) with layer-wise answer margins for four open-weight speech LLMs on SpeechMMLU and VoiceBench BBH. In Qwen2.5-Omni and MiniCPM-o, speech and text representations align in middle layers while their late-layer answer margins still differ, so mid-layer alignment does not imply functional equivalence. Qwen2-Audio instead shows weak alignment, which indicates a different failure mode. An attention intervention at fixed input and token count identifies a causal effect of late attention allocation on answer-code separation, a quantity distinct from answer correctness. Compressing the speech signal or its token sequence repairs a subset of the errors that occur only with speech.

♻ ☆ When Reasoning Supervision Hurts: TTCW-Based Long-Form Literary Review Generation NAACL 2027
Jinlong Liu, Mohammed Bahja, Mark Lee
Automatic evaluation of long-form literary writing remains challenging, particularly for creativity-related dimensions such as originality and flexibility. We address the lack of large-scale supervision for structured Torrance Test of Creative Writing (TTCW)-based evaluation by constructing a dataset of 263{,}911 long-form stories, each paired with scalar scores and meta-synthesised review comments across 14 TTCW-based dimensions. Using this dataset, we study whether reviewer-style reasoning traces improve structured literary review generation across Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, and Nemotron-8B. Across all three models, no-trace supervision yields higher score imitation fidelity and near-complete report generation, whereas trace supervision reduces completion to $85.9$--$90.9\%$. Failure analysis shows that $88.6\%$ of failed reasoning generations contain semantic repetition loops inside the trace. These loops concentrate in particular story--criterion interactions and progressively harden after onset, while comparable repetition is rare in the supervision data. Our results suggest that, for long-form tasks requiring strictly structured outputs, explicit reasoning traces can introduce generation instability that outweighs their overall benefit.

♻ ☆ BTS-AgentBench: A Deterministic, Replayable Pipeline from Read-Only Telemetry Logs to Agent Benchmarks
Jeong-Yoon Kim, Tae-Yoon Lee
Operational facilities accumulate extensive local telemetry, but agent-ready interactive evaluations with source-verifiable answers are costly to construct from such archives. Site-specific measurement histories also motivate a local conversion workflow that can operate where raw operational data must remain under organizational control. We introduce BTS-AgentBench, a deterministic compiler that transforms read-only telemetry and equipment metadata into tool-backed, multi-turn agent tasks, phase-level targets, evidence and executable evaluation contracts. The BTS release includes 532 episodes across nine families; a second-corpus port produces 204 XAI4HEAT episodes. Frozen-input replay reproduces released artifacts byte-for-byte, and independent raw-source checks cover all 89 BTS and 41 XAI4HEAT test episodes. Replayable provenance links 1,170 trial identities to recorded execution and scorer conditions, while 1,125 completed traces retain model responses and tool results. An obligation-level comparison of 216 scored failures found that linked records exposed additional recorded failed obligations in 202 cases (93.5\%), including tool arguments, stream identity, state carryover and missing interaction steps. The pipeline thereby supports source-grounded task generation, exact reconstruction and auditable diagnosis of agent executions. Code, released benchmarks, and reproduction instructions are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/telemetry-benchmark-reproduction-3D68
♻ ☆ TimeWarp: Evaluating Web Agents by Revisiting the Past NeurIPS 2026
Md Farhan Ishmam, Kenneth Marino
As web agents close the gap with humans on benchmarks, one question arises: Do today's agents perform just as well on tomorrow's web? We introduce TimeWarp, a benchmark that emulates the evolving web. TimeWarp consists of three web environments, each with six UI versions spanning UI design, frontend code, and workflows from different eras of the internet. We pair TimeWarp with a set of complex, realistic tasks covering different forms of web navigation. Our experiments reveal that vision-based agents are vulnerable to changes, while text-based agents become brittle once fine-tuned on a single version. To address this, we propose TimeTraj, a new annotation method that uses plan distillation to collect trajectories across multiple versions. By training agents on teacher rollouts using our BC-variant, we achieve substantial performance gains: 20.4% to 37.7% for Qwen-3 4B and 0% to 27.0% for Llama-3.1 8B models. Our work helps study generalization across web designs and opens a new paradigm for collecting plans rather than trajectories to improve the robustness of web agents.

♻ ☆ Who Sets the Pace of Judge Hacking? Judge Capability versus the Policy-Judge Gap
Haosen Wang
Under GRPO against a frozen, reference-free LLM judge, RL exploits the judge's false positives. Does the policy-judge capability gap or the judge itself set how fast and how far? We train 0.5B and 1.5B Qwen2.5 policies on GSM8K against four judges at least as large, and the 1.5B policy also against one smaller judge. In a post-hoc analysis, judge acceptance rose, but no cell of 12 gained more than 0.04 in seed-mean held-out greedy accuracy. A gold-reward control with the same recipe raised the 1.5B policy's accuracy by +0.054 (for the 0.5B policy, gold reward gave no detectable gain either). The depth of judge hacking tracks the judge: the plateau correlates with judge AUC (Spearman -0.69 over 12 cells), and within a family the larger judge is fooled less deeply. For the pace, two pre-specified pairs contradict the gap account: the cell with the larger gap was fooled 3.5x sooner at the pre-specified threshold. With 12 cells, however, we cannot formally separate judge AUC from the gap for either endpoint. A blind test of step-0 predictors on three held-out judges was inconclusive. At 7-8B, judges from three families plateaued between 0.37 and 0.85.

♻ ☆ Lost in the bf16 Cast: Exporting Ternary Language Models Can Revert Most Low-Learning-Rate Code Changes
Avichal Sahai, Nishant Raj, Animesh Srivastava
Ternary language models such as BitNet b1.58, Falcon-E and BitCPM are fine-tuned with higher-precision latent weights and deployed as ternary codes produced by an export step that, in the labs' documented pipelines, first casts the latents to bf16. We audit those pipelines across three labs. In released checkpoints, fp32 quantization of the shipped latents disagrees with the deployed codes on 0.83-1.77% of codes in Falcon-E and BitCPM and on 1.530% in BitNet 2B-4T; for Falcon-E and BitCPM most disagreements are products that bf16 rounding lands exactly on the threshold, which ties-to-even maps to zero, and the unmodified onebitllms exporter reproduces all four Falcon-E releases byte for byte. At fine-tuned endpoints, with learning rates selected to match a nominal learning-rate-to-bf16-ULP ratio, the documented export lowers greedy GSM8K strict accuracy from 58.79% to 0.78% for Falcon-E-1B-Base and from 36.13% to 0.39% for BitCPM-CANN-0.5B, and a bf16 save and reload lowers BitNet 2B-4T's strict accuracy by 27.54 points while its last-number accuracy rises. Two compatibility remedies, writing the training quantizer's codes directly or adjusting the bf16 inputs until the unchanged tools emit them, each met a 4-point strict-accuracy non-inferiority criterion against online evaluation in all three models. In a prospectively frozen study of bf16 latents during fine-tuning (one task, one recipe and trajectory per arm), native bf16 writes left 2B-4T's codes unchanged at the endpoint and changed one Falcon-E code, while emulated stochastic-rounding and compensated writers changed codes and were non-inferior to fp32 training at the same margin in both models. In two model families, randomized interventions on the initial distance from the threshold support distance-dependent selection of the codes that fine-tuning changes.

♻ ☆ Fish Audio Drama 3 Technical Report
Dawei Han, Shidong Li, Shijia Liao, Jiahua Liu, Songting Liu, Qingzheng Wang, Yuxuan Wang, Xin Chen, Yifan Cheng, Tianyu Li, Gangin Park, Xuehao Yang, Chang Zeng, Haoyang Zhang, Ruoyi Zhang, Xiangyu Zhang
We introduce Fish Audio Drama 3, an open-source speech generation model that turns text into directed performances, built on a 35B-parameter mixture-of-experts backbone that activates 3B parameters per token. It clones voices from multiple references, follows natural-language direction, revises a take when the next turn gives a note, voices multi-character scenes, edits existing speech by infilling, and synthesizes long-form and streaming audio. Multi-turn revision, scene dubbing, editing by infilling, and streaming text input are new in Drama 3, all in one model. Drama 3 pairs the hybrid linear-attention backbone with a new codebook multi-token-prediction audio decoder that predicts the acoustic codebooks of each frame in dedicated layers that attend over the entire sequence. The model is trained with a three-stage pre-training recipe, instruction fine-tuning, and large-batch reinforcement learning whose only reward is a single learned model of human preference. Its inference engine decodes a single stream at 271 frames per second on one H200, against 177 for Fish Audio S2, although Drama 3 has more than seven times as many parameters. The model weights are available on GitHub (https://github.com/fishaudio/fish-speech) and Hugging Face (https://huggingface.co/fishaudio/drama-3), and inference is available through SGLang-Omni (https://github.com/sgl-project/sglang-omni). ♻ ☆ DEEPRUBRIC: Evidence-Tree Rubric Supervision for Efficient Reinforcement Learning of Deep Research Agents EMNLP 2026
Minghang Zhu, Chuyang Wei, Junhao Xu, Yilin Cheng, Zhumin Chen, Jiyan He
Deep research agents synthesize long-form reports by searching and reasoning over retrieved evidence. Reinforcement learning with rubric-based rewards improves these agents by optimizing them against checkable criteria that translate report quality into reward signals, but its efficiency depends on whether those criteria reliably capture the task scope and evidence needs. Most existing studies ask an LLM to generate rubrics for a given query, but when the model fails to infer the underlying information needs, the generated rubrics may be incomplete and reduce RL efficiency. To obtain more reliable query--rubric supervision, we introduce DeepRubric, a data construction framework that reverses this process: instead of inferring evaluation criteria for a given query, it first determines what an evidence-backed report should be evaluated on and then synthesizes aligned query--rubric pairs from those evaluation targets. Starting from a sampled seed topic, DeepRubric builds an evidence tree by recursively expanding evidence-backed sub-questions, whose leaves serve as atomic and verifiable evaluation targets. It then uses the evidence tree to synthesize the training query and rubrics, ensuring that the reward evaluates exactly the information requested by the query. Using DeepRubric, we construct 9K query--rubric supervision examples and train DeepRubric-8B with rubric-based GRPO, achieving comparable performance to prior open state-of-the-art deep research models across three benchmarks with roughly 13x fewer RL GPU-hours.

☆ ChunkAct: Action Chunking for Agentic Robotics
Bingxin Xu, Wenrui Bao, Xinxin Liu, Jiawei Zhou, Yuzhang Shang, Emilio Ferrara
Agentic robotics places a large language model (LLM) agent in the robot control loop, where it composes perception, motion, and vision-language-action (VLA) tools under the guidance of a memory and thereby generalizes to new tasks without retraining. This generality comes at a steep price in efficiency. Every primitive requires a round of LLM reasoning over the full interaction history, so the cost of an episode grows with the number of primitives. We find this reasoning largely redundant: once a sub-goal is chosen, its primitives follow a routine already prescribed by memory. We present ChunkAct, which introduces action chunking to agentic robotics. Online, a single call launches a chunk of chained primitives, which executes autonomously and returns control only upon failure. Offline, ChunkAct compiles the memory into these chunks, so execution is memory-free at test time and the planner is reserved for decisions that require reasoning. LLM computation therefore scales with the number of decisions rather than the number of primitives. On LIBERO-Pro, ChunkAct improves task success by 5.0 points over the state of the art in agentic robotics while requiring 2.9x fewer LLM calls, 4.4x fewer input tokens, and 2.3x fewer output tokens.
☆ Tev: Scaling Long-Horizon Agentic Environments with Dense Progress Rewards
Wenbo Hu, Shraman Pramanick, Krishna Kumar Singh, Nanyun Peng, Kai-Wei Chang, Yong Jae Lee
Long-horizon agents have drawn growing attention for their ability to work autonomously -- from hours to days -- on economically valuable tasks, a capability essential to automated discovery and recursive self-improvement (RSI). Yet open-source models still struggle on long-horizon tasks, largely for lack of both training data and a working recipe, and scaling both is hard: long, complex tasks are difficult to curate and verify, and their rewards are sparse. We address both. First, we introduce Tev-Gym, the first long-horizon agent training dataset with verifiable, dense, progressive rewards -- 1,170 verified task environments across 7 domains. It is built on a latent-world-first approach that first constructs a latent world: a complete and correct hidden specification of the task domain, workflow, and expected state after each step. Each task is then progressively rendered from this specification into an agent-facing environment, enabling controllable complexity and a natural progress metric. Second, we turn this metric into a training recipe: via progress-potential reward shaping (PPRS), we convert progress into a dense per-step reward that improves training efficiency and makes otherwise-intractable tasks trainable. Training with this recipe yields Tev-27B, which achieves substantial gains on the Agents' Last Exam computing and math sciences domains (ALE-CM), matching GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5 at Pass@5. Despite training only on ALE-related data, it generalizes to the Terminal-Bench (TB) 3.0 and 4.0 subsets, surpassing GLM 5.2 and Gemini 3.7 Flash, and reaches Claude Opus 4.8-level performance on middle-horizon tasks such as TB 2.1.

☆ DoubtLess: Training-Free Contrastive Decoding for Efficient Reasoning
Minghao Luo, Liang Chen
Reasoning models achieve strong performance through long chains of thought (CoT), but often spend more tokens than needed to solve a problem. Existing ways to shorten the CoT require additional training for each model, add a fixed steering vector to the model's hidden states, or use decoding heuristics such as stopping early or banning words like "wait", which can reduce accuracy. We propose DoubtLess, a training-free decoding method in which a much smaller reasoning model steers the large one towards brevity. The small model reads the large model's CoT so far twice, once prompted to be brief (the brief thinker) and once prompted to be verbose (the verbose thinker), and the difference between its two logit vectors, scaled by a strength, is added to the large model's logits at every step of the CoT. On seven benchmarks of competition mathematics, graduate-level science and competitive programming, DoubtLess shortens the CoT of Qwen3-32B by 31% with unchanged accuracy, outperforming five inference-time baselines. We also show that DoubtLess reduces thinking tokens per turn by 20% in a self-debugging coding agent with unchanged task performance. It also generalizes to two other model families with unchanged accuracy. When combined with training-based compression, it further shortens the CoT of a model already trained for brevity. Ablations and behavioral analysis show that DoubtLess reduces repeated checking, with most token savings coming after the correct answer first appears in the CoT. Our code is available at https://github.com/leoluolol/DoubtLess.
☆ Hybrid Reasoning Agents
Jiaru Zou, Yunzhe Qi, Sirui Chen, Federico Bianchi, Shang Zhu, Pan Lu, Jingrui He, James Zou
How can we build an agent that thinks beyond human language, yet still acts through it in the external environment? Modern LLM agents still reason and interact through text, while latent reasoning offers an efficient internal medium but lacks native tool access and reasoning transparency. We bridge these two directions with Hybrid Reasoning Agents (HybridAgents), which dynamically navigate between latent reasoning and text-based interaction throughout a unified generation process. Architecturally, a lightweight HybridLink enables seamless transitions between text and latent modes, while the agent's continuous working memory preserves lossless hybrid context across multiple turns. These designs preserve tool-integrated agentic capabilities while making latent thoughts directly inspectable. Building on this architecture, we further develop dedicated post-training algorithms for hybrid reasoning: HybridSFT stabilizes tool-integrated hybrid generation by teaching the backbone to coordinate with HybridLink, then HybridRL scales reasoning by learning when to think in latent space and when to act through text. We evaluate HybridAgent at 3 model scales across 11 benchmarks covering mathematics, science, medicine, search, and agentic reasoning. Compared with frontier LLM Agents and agentic frameworks spanning both text-based and latent methods, HybridAgent consistently improves performance by 10.4%, while delivering 35.5%-54.7% token reduction, 1.8x-2.6x faster inference, and 2.7x-3.7x cost savings. Code and data are available at https://github.com/togethercomputer/HybridAgent.

☆ The Last Remote Sensing Model Built by Humans? Casting Any Earth Observation Task as Recursive Self-Improvement
Sijie Zhao, Zeqi Zhou, Heli Qi, Xuanchao Fu, Zhuo Zheng, Junshi Xia, Junjue Wang, Weihao Xuan, Naoto Yokoya
Earth observation (EO) models are still built by hand. For each new task, experts design a training recipe and tune it against a benchmark. We show that this loop can be handed to an agent. Any verifiable task can be cast as a harness and a verifier, where the harness defines what the agent may use and the verifier defines what counts as success. Every EO task can be made verifiable, through labeled benchmarks, independent sensors, physical models or the repeated observation of the same place. Given only these two components, a general-purpose coding agent builds the EO model and improves it recursively on its own outputs. Settings long treated as separate research problems, from learning with a handful of labels to adapting across domains, become choices of harness and verifier. On four EO benchmarks spanning optical, SAR and multispectral time-series imagery, agent-built models surpass the state of the art and keep most of that accuracy from about 1% of the training labels. This establishes a new paradigm for EO, in which people design harnesses and verifiers and agents build the models, so EO models improve as agents do.

☆ OmniCompass: A Holistic Benchmark for Omni-Modal Video Grounding and Understanding
Xuyang Liu, Shihao Wang, Xinyu Wei, Minghan Li, Zeqing Wang, Zuhao Yang, Zichen Wen, Lei Zhang
With the rapid development of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), evaluating their video understanding capabilities has become increasingly important. Existing video understanding benchmarks cover only part of the evaluation dimensions, limiting systematic diagnosis of omni-modal video understanding. We introduce OmniCompass, a holistic benchmark for omni-modal video grounding and understanding. OmniCompass defines two evaluation tracks: OmniCompass-VL assesses vision-language models on tasks requiring visual evidence, while OmniCompass-AV assesses omni-modal models on tasks requiring joint audio-visual evidence. Each track includes three complementary tasks: temporal grounding (TG), spatial grounding (SG), and question answering (QA), assessing when a referenced target appears, where it is, and what happens. QA covers five perception and three reasoning categories in OmniCompass-VL, and three perception and two reasoning categories in OmniCompass-AV. OmniCompass-VL contains 820 videos and 2,460 instances across domains and three duration types: short, medium, and long. A narrative- and audio-rich subset forms OmniCompass-AV with 240 videos and 720 instances. Each instance is evaluated under two paired settings: text-only and text+image, specifying the same target through text and visual-language guidance. All instances are manually annotated with multi-round quality verification. Evaluations of 50 MLLMs show that open-source models still lag behind proprietary counterparts, particularly in audio-visual understanding. Precise temporal and spatial grounding remains challenging even for leading models, and their predictions still exhibit inconsistencies across the paired target specifications.

☆ Read the Room Before Your Model Generates AACL
Muyao Kong, Zhihao Lin
Large language models (LLMs) generally rely on sampling temperature to regulate the amount of randomness in their outputs. Mainstream methods for selecting temperature typically involve fixed, heuristic global settings and context or token-level calibration, rather than a geometrically interpretable representation of the communicative scenario. In this work, we introduce a simple and interpretable temperature selection framework, the \textbf{Scenario-Actor}, which adapts decoding to the inferred situational characteristics. Scenario-Actor extracts scenario-relevant structures from Transformer hidden states and determines the temperature from scenario embedding, a structured geometric representation of the communicative setting. Empirical results show that on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Scenario-Actor achieves strong goal-completion performance in interactive social scenarios, significant gains on open-ended instruction following, and competitive performance on multi-turn conversation compared to multiple baselines.

☆ Back-Translation Still Matters: Leveraging Monolingual Data for Low-Resource Machine Translation with LLMs
Divyanshu Bathla, Parag Singla, Jitin Singla
Pre-trained Large Language Model (LLMs) are effective on many tasks in zero-shot and few-shot settings. For machine translation of low-resource languages, the obvious options are few-shot prompting and fine-tuning on a small parallel set. We ask: what if moderate amounts of low-resource monolingual data are also available? We argue that, rather than continued pretraining, a more effective use of this data is the pre-LLM era technique of back-translation (BT). The current fine-tuned LLM translates monolingual sentences in each language, and the resulting pairs train it in the reverse direction. Updates on the parallel set are interleaved for stable learning. With 1K parallel pairs on Odia and Swahili, BT beats continued pretraining into the low-resource language by 7.7 to 16.4 XCOMET-XXL points in domain. With beam-decoded intermediate translations, it also beats a model with 10 time more parallel data. In-context examples retrieved from the parallel data further improve BT. We also analyze the source, quality and amount of monolingual text. Across five low-resource languages, with Hindi as a high-resource control, and two 3B backbones, BT improves translation into the low-resource language by 16 to 173% XCOMET-XXL over parallel-only fine-tuning. Blind human evaluation on Odia and Sanskrit agrees with the automatic metrics.
☆ Self-Evolving Agent Harness
Chuyang Wei, Haoxiang Guan, Minghang Zhu, Kexin Zhou, Jiyan He, Xiren Zhou, Huanhuan Chen, Yifei Shen
Harness self-evolution revises an agent's software scaffolding, including prompts, memory, workflows, and executable components, while keeping model weights fixed. We formalize a feedback-driven process in which accepted revisions persist across runs. The framework separates update targets from proposal and admission mechanisms. It distinguishes changes to interpreted state from revisions to tools and code, repository-level control logic, and plugin composition. We characterize search through evolutionary optimization and examine its reliability through continual PAC-learning. Under explicit assumptions, the analysis yields an expected generalization bound, a retention bound conditional on historical regression budgets, and a high-probability admission rule using fresh, independent validation data. We examine how persistent harness revisions affect knowledge sharing and organizational workflows, with implications for scientific reliability, physical safety, and institutional accountability. Cases from prior work illustrate model--harness co-design, distributed execution, and plugin reuse. Reliable deployment requires continual validation as task distributions and evaluators change, with independent safeguards for learning, runtime privileges, and consequential actions. We provide a comprehensive 66-page discussion of related work, theory, algorithms, evaluation, applications, and the broader impact of Harness RSI, together with continuously updated code and supplementary materials, at https://github.com/zgcagi/Harness-RSI.
