☆ SkillScaffold: Scaffolding Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Policy-Adaptive Skill Credit Assignment and Selective Injection
Wenlin Zhong, Hao Wang, Kun Yuan, Fan Yang, Jinluan Yang, Dian Jin, Xu Tan, Yiquan Wu, Kun Kuang
Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) enables large language models (LLMs) to learn from multi-turn interactions with verifiable outcomes. Textual skills can improve exploration by distilling prior interactions into reusable guidance. However, existing skill-based RL approaches often either assume skill injection is beneficial or rely on indirect feedback or rollout-intensive evaluation to assess its utility. Our pilot studies show that rollout-based utility estimation for skill credit assignment is costly and unstable under limited sampling, while skill benefits vary across candidates and depend on the current policy's competence on each query. To address these challenges, we introduce SkillScaffold, a policy-adaptive training scaffold that combines low-cost skill-utility estimation with selective skill injection. By reusing outcome-verified training trajectories, SkillScaffold generates candidate skills and estimates their utility through outcome-aware likelihood comparisons, without candidate-specific environment rollouts. The resulting utility guides whether and which skill to inject, retaining skill-free execution when no candidate is estimated to help. Joint training enables the Generator to produce skills tailored to the evolving Executor. Scaffolded RL encourages skill internalization for skill-free inference. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, ScienceWorld, and $τ^2$-Bench demonstrate gains over strong agentic-RL and skill-based RL baselines across both short- and long-horizon tasks. Ablations and further analyses support the importance of utility-based selection and abstention. Our code is available at https://github.com/Vaidurya00/SkillScaffold.
☆ SEAR: Self-Evolving Agentic Robotics
Xinyi Wang, Bangzheng Li, Hanyu Wang, Lin Zhang, Andrea Yaoyun Cui, Zhenggang Tang, Shyamal Buch, Dejia Xu
Frontier language-model agents can now improve robots on their own: they write control code, test it, and keep what works as skills and memories for the next task. We introduce SEAR (Self-Evolving Agentic Robotics), a framework and benchmark built around two questions. Is the agent really learning, or just trying more often? And does what it learns still help on new tasks, in a new simulator, and on a real robot? The framework runs an agent in three modes: it writes code that becomes the policy (code-as-policy), acts live in the control loop (agent-as-policy), or works as a researcher that fine-tunes a vision-language-action model (auto-research). The benchmark adds controls that separate retained experience from repeated attempts, familiar tasks, privileged simulator state, larger budgets, and luck. Across seven frontier language models, 330 simulated and five physical tasks, and over four years of agent runtime, we highlight three findings. First, the learning curve of a robotic agent depends both on the overall time budget and the task difficulty relative to model capability. Harder tasks make longer sessions worthwhile; on easier tasks, re-rolling short attempts does just as well. Second, control code and shared routines transfer across tasks and simulators, while task-specific solutions do not. On a real robot, we observe a performance gap between agent-designed practice and novel human-designed scenes. Third, different models evolve more effectively in different ways. Some do best acting live, others writing code first. And a text-only model keeps pace with a comparable multimodal one: perception helps with error assessment, but agentic reasoning and tool use can matter more. We release the benchmark and codebase to support future research.

☆ Where Do LLM Ensemble Gains Come From? Decomposing Candidate Value, Diversity, and Aggregation
Donghwan Kim
LLM ensemble gains are often measured against a single baseline, but the value attributed to additional candidates can change with the comparison used. We study an LLM aggregator under four matched conditions: Direct (no candidates), Single-slot (one candidate in a multi-slot interface), Duplicate (one candidate repeated), and Multi-candidate (separately generated candidates). Under either non-diverse control, overall candidate gain (CIV) splits algebraically into an additional-candidate contrast (DSV) and a candidate-context contrast (CCV); the split, but not CIV, depends on the chosen control. On MultiHopRAG model diversity (k=2), the repetition gap Rep=-8.33 pp [-13.25, -3.75] quantifies the difference between gains measured against Duplicate and Single-slot. On a matched FRAMES stronger-panel extension (n=200, k=3, aggregator output limit 4096), paired Delta Rep=+9.67 pp [+6.00, +13.50]; within panels, Rep changes sign (original -6.33; stronger +3.33). In FRAMES evidence aggregation, candidate-context contrasts remain strongly negative under both non-diverse controls. Our results show why ensemble evaluations should state the reference control before attributing gains to additional candidates.

☆ Pack Light, CarryOn: Building Object-Oriented Context for Long-Horizon Agents
Yuchen Lu, Julian McAuley, Lei Zhang
Long-horizon agents accumulate progress in persistent task state, yet repeatedly process interaction histories describing how that progress was achieved. This raises a natural question: can long-horizon context instead be organized around reusable progress rather than the history that produced it? In this paper, we introduce CarryOn, an object-oriented context runtime that organizes continuation around reusable task objects. Rather than treating compressed histories or reconstructed accounts of progress as the primary basis for continuation, CarryOn builds a context view from continuation carriers, complementary execution context, and a live tail of recent interactions. A core challenge is keeping this reusable basis valid after its surrounding history is retired: how can CarryOn update the basis without reconstructing the full representation at every fold? CarryOn maintains carrier records across folds, revising affected records against bound evidence and applicable environment checks while carrying forward unaffected records and jointly updating execution context. Experiments on OSWorld 2.0 and BrowseComp-Plus show improved gains of 5.8% and 7.0% while reducing total token usage by 51.9% and 22.9%, respectively. Project page: https://ethannju.github.io/carryon

☆ BLADE: Auditing Information Use for Failure Attribution in LLM Multi-Agent Systems
Xiaoguang Guo, Yaohao Long, Chuxu Zhang
Large language model multi-agent systems extend individual agents' capabilities through collaboration across specialized roles, tools, and workflows. Yet collaboration makes failures harder to diagnose: one agent may misinterpret a correct result while others carry the mistake forward. Failure attribution therefore seeks to identify the responsible agent and decisive error step. Recent methods trace dependencies, but knowing where information came from is not the same as knowing where it went wrong. We introduce BLADE (Blame Localization via Audited Dependency Evidence), a training-free framework that moves beyond tracing information to auditing its use. Using Jev for structured probabilistic judgments, BLADE uses dependency probabilities to select relevant source-decision handoffs for auditing. At these handoffs, it examines whether receiving decisions use their sources faithfully and within task requirements, distinguishing error introduction, propagation, and repair. These findings inform a global diagnosis that considers all execution steps. Without reference answers, BLADE achieves the best or tied-best step accuracy among eight strategies under a shared Jev decision interface on 1,228 failed runs across five Who&When and Who&When Pro subsets, outperforming All-at-once by 2.2-11.1 percentage points. Making responsibility evidence explicit offers a path toward more interpretable and reliable agentic systems.

☆ Rubrics from the Task, Verdicts from Evidence: Pointwise Rubric Rewards with Tool-Augmented Judges
Tianci Liu, Haoyu Wang
Rubric-based reward models (RMs) evaluate responses against explicit criteria. However, existing methods often derive criteria from observed response candidates, introducing response-induced bias that may limit rubric generalizability. Their judges also struggle with mechanical constraints when relying on reading alone, and commonly used pairwise judges provide only relative preferences, complicating their direct use for policy optimization. We propose ReVeR, a pointwise rubric RM that derives hard rules and graded principles by reasoning about the instruction alone and evaluates responses independently through tool-augmented judging. Both components are trained through label-blind distillation, in which preference labels only filter a teacher's outputs, yielding ReVeR-Traces, a large-scale dataset of rubric-reasoning and judging trajectories. Experiments demonstrate that ReVeR outperforms pairwise rubric RMs in average preference accuracy, most clearly on instruction following, as well as in test-time scaling and downstream policy optimization. We further find that generator RL improves preference accuracy while reducing rubric fidelity, and that higher preference accuracy does not necessarily translate into better downstream policies. These findings highlight that rubric fidelity, preference accuracy, and downstream utility are distinct properties, shedding light on future RM research.

Sachin Kumar
Evaluation for automatic crisis reporting should measure what practitioners need, not what is convenient to compare. Through LLM-assisted, author-approved content analysis, we derive a 15-slot information schema from professional humanitarian situation reports using qualitative content analysis, then evaluate 121 human and 261 LLM-generated reports across five disasters and three hazard types. ☆ PreciseCoder: Faithful and Generalizable Debugging Beyond Unit-Test Rewards
Yunkai Zhan, Miaosen Chai, Shangshang Wang, Jike Zhong, Yuqing Yang, Deqing Fu, Serina Chang, Wang Bill Zhu
Reinforcement learning (RL) from unit tests is the common recipe for post-training code models, but a unit test only asks whether the final program passes. We analyze what this unit-test-only reward teaches a model during debugging and find that, while it raises pass rates, it also teaches the model to edit more and to hallucinate bugs in code that is already correct. We introduce PreciseCoder, a training algorithm that augments the unit-test reward with a signal for semantically verified, localized edits, which correlates with smaller edits and higher pass rates. On 4B, 9B, and 27B models, PreciseCoder reduces the average number of edit lines from 7-15to 3 at a pass rate on par with unit-test-only training. Trained only on single-line bugs, PreciseCoder can generalize to repository-level program repair, where it resolves more issues with smaller patches. Its precision also transfers from weaker to stronger models: given the reasoning trace of a 9B PreciseCoder model, frontier models such as GPT-5.6 Sol and Qwen3.8-Max gain 4.6-12.4 points in edit precision with no additional training. Linguistic analysis of the thinking traces shows that our reward encourages the model to reason about individual lines, name the buggy ones, and form consistent bug hypotheses.

☆ On the Monitorability of Natural Language Autoencoders: Identification vs Discrimination
Kevin Yandoka Denamganaï
Natural Language Autoencoders (NLAs) are proposed as oversight tools for language models: an Activation Verbaliser (AV) writes an English explanation of an activation vector, and an Activation Reconstructor (AR) maps the explanation back to a vector. Their value for oversight depends on a human monitor being able to tell a true account of an activation from a false one, given the Av's explanation. We formalise NLA training and NLA oversight deployment as two different referential games from Emergent Communication: training is a generative referential game, while oversight is a discriminative, multi-modal, partially-observable referential game played over a many-to-many relation between the monitored language model's activations and the accounts that are true of them. The gap between the two games predicts that AV's explanations will identify which activation they describe without discriminating its true account from closely matched false ones, since training never asks explanations to rule anything out. Using the NLA's own AR as a maximally-sympathetic surrogate monitor model, we experimentally confirm this theoretical insight on one released checkpoint: true accounts beat accounts of other activations in all 320 comparisons, but beat fluent rivals differing in a single claim only 60.2% of the time (std.err. 6.7%, chance at 50%, $n = 35$). A meaning-preserving rewrite by an uninvolved model costs only 2.1% of the distance to another activation's account, so the failure is not due to the NLA pair having developed private conventions in the AV's explanations. Our rival accounts test only transcript-level truths, which the monitored language model's conversation settles, so these results bound the easiest case and leave claims about the monitored model's own processing untested. We state conjectures that would make both results consequences of the framework.

☆ Code-Switching Detection via Language-Selective Dimensions in Speech Foundation Models ICASSP 2027
Jie Chi, Zakaria Aldeneh, Masha Fedzechkina, Maureen de Seyssel, Natalie Schluter
Code-switching (CS), the alternation between languages within an utterance, is common in multilingual speech but underrepresented in pretraining corpora. Increasing CS coverage requires finding relevant utterances in large speech collections so that they can be included in training data. One way to find such utterances is to transcribe the speech and detect language alternation in the text, but this depends on obtaining accurate multilingual transcriptions. We propose a lightweight method that detects CS directly from speech. It uses frame-level language information in pretrained speech encoders to determine whether both languages are present within an utterance. Specifically, we identify language-selective hidden-state dimensions using monolingual recordings and aggregate their frame-level activations into a CS prediction. With a small CS set used to tune a decision rule, we show that the Whisper-small encoder achieves a mean F1 of $0.798$ across 14 language pairs in CS-FLEURS, compared with $0.776$ for a CNN and $0.751$ for a HuBERT linear baseline trained for monolingual language identification. Our findings suggest that pretrained speech encoders already encode the information needed to identify CS utterances, and they provide a lightweight tool for building more representative multilingual corpora.

☆ From Relation-Specific Heads to Shared Interventions: Contextual Entrainment in Language Models AACL
Jinghan Zheng, Jiajun Shi
Large language models rely on prompt context to perform in-context learning, follow formats, and adapt to local evidence. This ability, however, also raises a robustness concern: models may reuse irrelevant tokens from earlier context when answering a later query. Prior work identifies this behavior as contextual entrainment and finds attention heads whose ablation reduces it, arguing that these heads are largely task- or relation-specific. But are the mechanisms behind contextual entrainment truly isolated by relation, or do relation-conditioned searches recover reusable components shared across relations? Using factual-distractor relation-completion prompts across 19 lexical-semantic subtypes, we identify attention heads whose ablation reduces entrainment and test how the resulting head sets transfer recovery ability across exact relations, semantic families, and mixed relation distributions. Same-family transfer exceeds different-family transfer, family residuals weaken once globally shared heads are removed, and subtype-specific searches repeatedly rediscover many global heads. Clean-completion and context-use probes further suggest that this shared intervention can reduce harmful copying while preserving ordinary relation completion and simple in-context abilities. Our findings provide a more precise perspective on contextual entrainment and its mitigation: harmful context following is not best addressed by searching for isolated relation-specific circuits, but by using heterogeneous attack prompts to reveal reusable intervention targets whose effects are shaped by semantic relation structure.

☆ SMI: Ontology-Grounded Semantic IDs for Hierarchy-Aware Medical Concept Representation NeurIPS 2026
Ziyang Song, Lia Shen, Yixuan Li, Qincheng Lu, Weihao Li, Haoran Xu, He Zhu, Xinyu Wang, Yue Li
Biomedical language models (LMs) provide strong representations for clinical tasks, but they often fail to capture the hierarchical structure among medical concepts. This limitation is especially pronounced for rare and fine-grained clinical codes in long-tailed vocabularies, where hierarchy-agnostic LM representations become less informative as concept frequency decreases. We introduce Semantic Medical Identifier (SMI), a training-free framework for constructing ontology-grounded Semantic IDs and hierarchy-aware medical concept representations. For each diagnosis, procedure, or medication concept, SMI defines its Semantic ID as the coarse-to-fine path through an expert-defined medical ontology. SMI then aggregates frozen biomedical LM embeddings across the corresponding ontology levels to form hierarchy-aware medical concept representations that preserve coarse-to-fine clinical semantics. The base SMI uses uniform aggregation across ontology levels, while a level-weighted variant selects global level weights on validation data. Our theoretical analysis shows that SMI yields more informative medical concept representations than hierarchy-agnostic LM embeddings, particularly for rare and fine-grained clinical concepts. Experiments show that SMI captures meaningful hierarchical structure and improves clinical relation prediction. On MIMIC-IV, SMI improves mortality prediction, readmission prediction, drug recommendation, and rare drug prediction. On eICU, SMI yields more robust patient representations under cross-hospital distribution shift.

☆ Known-Voice Cloning and the Limits of Personality Transfer for Character Design
Johanna Magdalena Kuch, Maud van Lier, Daksitha Senel Withanage Don, Patrick Gebhard, Tanja Schneeberger, Elisabeth André
Recent advances in zero-shot voice cloning systems make it possible to reproduce a recognizable voice from a short recording and attach it to arbitrary virtual characters. Prior work suggests that familiar vocal identity can carry over associated personality impressions to new targets such as robots or virtual characters. However, these works have mainly examined familiarity and personalization effects rather than directly testing whether a cloned familiar voice systematically transfers personality impressions to an embodied virtual character. Such transference raises the concern that cloned voices could be used to manipulate people into engaging with virtual characters as capable social actors. Across two studies, we investigated whether personality impressions transfer when a virtual character speaks with the cloned voice of a familiar person. Study 1 examined this question in a real-world university setting using the cloned voice of a lecturer familiar to the participating students. Study 2 examined the same question online using voices of social media content creators and members of their communities. Drawing on the concepts of identity activation, trait transfer, and person reconstruction, we show that evidence of personality transfer was limited in both studies. Observed effects were selective and did not generalize across traits or study contexts. In the online study in particular, recognizable voice identity cues activated expectations about the speaker but did not reconstruct the broader person impression. Qualitative responses further indicated that judgments were frequently shaped by perceived artificiality and by whether voice and appearance fit together. Our findings moderate common concerns that cloned voices straightforwardly manipulate people into perceiving artificial characters as capable social actors.

☆ Bayesian Test-Time Inference of Task-Aligned Similarity from Weak Interactive Feedback NeurIPS 2026
Weida Liang, Kenji Kawaguchi
Language model systems repeatedly make test-time decisions by comparing similarity: they retrieve exemplars, rank candidates, and revise responses. Yet similarity is not unique: lexical, semantic, structural, and stylistic resemblance can point to different candidates, and the task-aligned notion can change across instances. We introduce BASIS (Bayesian Axis Selection via Interactive Signals), a training-free Bayesian method that uses weak test-time feedback to infer which criterion in a supplied, task-specific library governs the current decision. It maintains a joint posterior over candidate targets and criteria, updates it from scalar scores, pairwise preferences, or verifier outputs, and selects probes by expected information gain. In a finite noisy model, we characterize when the target-criterion pair is recoverable and show why fixed-criterion and fixed-mixture rules can fail under criterion shift. Across concept recovery, reasoning exemplar selection, and response refinement, BASIS improves six-probe concept-recovery Success@1 from 56.0% to 68.1%, reasoning accuracy from 72.3% to 73.1%, and refinement win rate from 61.4% to 63.8% over tuned static mixtures. It also exceeds compute-matched best-of-N selection on reasoning (72.1%) and refinement (61.2%). Gains are largest under criterion mismatch and with larger candidate pools, and matched controls indicate that adaptive criterion weighting explains much of the benefit, with joint target-criterion inference adding further value. BASIS selects only among supplied criteria and does not discover new ones.

☆ Does Pretraining Teach Chemical Language Models Chemistry?
Leif Sieben, Yoel Zimmermann
Chemical language models (CLMs) are pretrained on large corpora of molecules with the aim of learning representations that transfer to diverse downstream tasks. Here, we systematically study the effects of pretraining using a series of such CLMs across 64 molecular property prediction datasets. We find that the downstream gains of pretraining in CLMs do not scale and performance saturates below gradient-boosting baselines. Across the regimes evaluated, only supervised pretraining on traditional physico-chemical descriptors consistently improves downstream performance for the CLMs tested whereas some forms of pretraining result in models worse than a randomly initialized encoder. While models can resolve molecular structure well, they do so no better than a randomly initialized model, suggesting that, at best, CLMs learn descriptor-like properties in addition to low-level token statistics during pretraining, but surprisingly little chemistry beyond what classical molecular features already capture.
☆ To Align or Not To Align: Check Your COMPASS Before You Train NeurIPS 2026
Fabian Gröger, Petar Damjanović, Maria Brbić
Combining pretrained models is a common strategy for building AI systems, whether by aligning a vision encoder with a language model or merging fine-tuned checkpoints. However, finding compatible pairs typically requires costly empirical search, as compatibility is known only after alignment or merging. We propose COMPASS (COMPatibility ASsessment Score), a theoretically motivated diagnostic that estimates whether a model pair will be compatible prior to alignment or merging. Building on the observation that representational convergence is most robust at the level of local neighborhoods, COMPASS decomposes model compatibility into two cheap-to-compute axes: a pair-specific ease, defined as the $k$-NN overlap between the two encoders, and a paradigm-level ceiling, capturing the maximum overlap their training paradigms allow. Together, these two axes provide a ranking of candidate model pairs and prescribe how to act on each pair: (i) prioritize pairs that already agree and whose paradigms are compatible, (ii) invest additional compute on pairs with compatible paradigms but low initial agreement, (iii) switch the pretraining paradigm when it cannot support sufficient agreement, or (iv) deprioritize pairs that score poorly on both axes. We apply COMPASS to $72$ cross-modal alignment experiments and $392$ task-arithmetic model merges and show that compatibility estimates by COMPASS strongly correlate with downstream success, achieving average Pearson correlation of $0.91$ on cross-modal alignment tasks and $0.70$ on model merging.

☆ Breakdown of Conversational Involvement: A Joint Multi-Modal Analysis of Conversation Floors and F-formation Detection with Multiple Features
Zonghuan Li, Chirag Raman, Hayley Hung
Interactions happen universally and spontaneously in daily lives. Studying involvement in interactions helps us analyze who can influence who and has a wide potential in applications. Specifically, we consider conversational involvement in free-standing conversations. We study two operationalizations of an FCG: F-formations and conversation floors, and propose that current assumption that F-formations capture single conversations is insufficient. To illustrate how different implementations could impact the detected F-formations, we employ three analyses. First, we propose that speaking status can be used for estimating conversation floors, and leveraged it to analyze floors within F-formations. Second, we analyze spatial characteristics of the detection results by applying different feature sets on chosen baselines, and find that groups resulting from head features are more likely to be subsumed by groups detected using other features. Finally, we do a joint analysis which combines the spatial features and floor patterns in the conversation. We conclude that a more detailed theory and framework is needed to holistically demonstrate conversational involvement within interactions.
☆ Knowledge Graphs as Files: An Agent Harness Where Tool Creation and Tool Use Co-Evolve
Yuyang Bai, Yu Wang, Yu Zhang
Searching text-rich knowledge graphs requires following relations while examining textual evidence. Predefined graph interfaces constrain what evidence agents can access, even when they write code to combine tool calls or process retrieved subgraphs. We propose direct knowledge graph interaction (DKGI), a harness in which language model agents create graph access tools directly over complete node and edge files. The agent writes Python code to follow relations, combine node sets, and select textual evidence, interleaving execution with shell search and file inspection. This lets agents process large node sets in bulk while returning selected text for the model to assess. Execution feedback guides tool creation and revision, while the evolving tools determine what evidence the agent gathers next. On benchmark datasets spanning three domains, training-free DKGI exceeds the strongest published baseline by at least 6.7 Hit@1 points on every graph, even with a minimal harness. Controlled ablations show the benefits of multi-turn programming over shell-only access and predefined graph tools, while trajectories show agents revising code to extend searches or address errors. These findings highlight the value of allowing tool creation and tool use to co-evolve during graph search. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/leopoldwhite/DKGI.

☆ Does self-distillation improve reasoning?
Jason Ken Adhinarta, Raina Wu, Ashutosh Sharma, Avinash Balakrishnan, Rameswar Panda, Polina Golland, Yoon Kim, Zhang-Wei Hong
Self-distillation is an emerging approach that trains a model to imitate its own predictions when conditioned on privileged information, such as correct answers or environmental feedback. Recent methods have reported better sample efficiency and generalization than reinforcement learning (RL), but it remains unclear whether these gains extend beyond in-distribution evaluations and whether they stem from privileged distillation. In this paper, we revisit two representative methods, Self-Distillation Policy Optimization (SDPO) and On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD), across code, science, tool use, and math reasoning tasks. We find that their reported gains do not consistently generalize to unseen problems and are largely explained by overfitting or transferring existing reasoning behavior, rather than effectively using privileged information. Moreover, self-distillation gradients show weak alignment with policy gradients that directly optimize expected reward, questioning whether they provide an effective direction for policy improvement. Our experiments across Qwen3 models (1.7B-14B) and OLMo-3-7B suggest that effectively leveraging privileged information to improve reasoning remains an open challenge. Our code is available at https://github.com/sleepypandu/does-sd-improve-reasoning

☆ Legal-R1: Agentic Retrieval for Evidence-Based Legal Reasoning
Huaiwen Pang, Jiale Han, Junying Chen, Wenjun Xiao, Benyou Wang
Legal question answering requires answers that are not only plausible, but also grounded in legally valid and citable evidence. Standard retrieval-augmented generation often relies on one-shot semantic matching, which is insufficient when legal evidence must be selected according to authority, applicability, and diversity across heterogeneous sources. We introduce Legal-R1, an agentic retrieval framework for evidence-based legal reasoning. Legal-R1 decomposes legal reasoning into a multi-agent workflow, where a Lawyer agent learns when to retrieve, which legal source to query, what evidence to seek, and when the collected evidence is sufficient. A Legal Assistant agent retrieves from normative legal sources and precedent cases with authority-aware and diversity-aware selection, while a Judge agent generates the final answer from the complete evidence trajectory. ☆ Jev-ToM: Evaluating Theory of Mind in Typed Decision Models
Kawai Chung, Chunkit Chan, Yuxuan Liu, Yauwai Yim, Xi Yang, Wenbin Hu, Huihao Jing, Chiu Yeung Tang, Changxuan Fan, Ki Sen Hung, Tsun On Kwok, Haoran Li
Dialogue and collaborative assistants repeatedly judge what a partner knows, believes, or intends. Typed decision models offer a potential alternative to generative large language models for bounded Theory of Mind (ToM) selection. We present Jev-ToM, comparing five typed decision models and seven generative baselines on 11,117 English text questions from nine ToM benchmarks across six capability groups. TypeSafe Jev reaches 73.2% source-balanced accuracy including native controls and ranks first or second across all six capabilities, leading beliefs, goals/desires/intentions, and emotions. Other typed models show distinct strengths across tasks. Separate resource comparisons suggest that Jev could reduce the cost and waiting time of repeated ToM selection. Confidence-based selection remains benchmark-dependent, and supplied reasons can repair errors or overturn correct answers depending on their attachment. Candidate expansion reveals distinct selection and output-validity problems. Related-question cues account for most of Jev's downstream gains, with task-dependent effects from predicted states. The findings motivate testing Jev as a ToM selection component in assistants, with task-specific confidence calibration, candidate design, and output validation as integration priorities.

☆ Beyond Answer Voting: Fine-Grained Correction of LLM Reasoning through Graph Alignment across Sampled Traces
Haotian Liu, Yingjian Chen, Xiaohang Xu, Lang Huang, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo, Irene Li
Large language models increasingly tackle tasks that require multi-step reasoning, where errors in intermediate claims can affect the final answer. Self-consistency (SC) improves reasoning by voting over sampled final answers, but its vote leaves intermediate disagreements unresolved. To address this problem, we introduce DDRG (Disagreement-Driven Verification Guided by Reasoning Graphs), a framework for selective correction from a frozen bank of reasoning traces. Our insight is that graph alignment can expose claim-level disagreements and provide correction evidence without expanding the sampled trace bank. DDRG builds source-grounded claim graphs and sends localized conflicts to an answer-blind verifier. A verifier-supported refutation that reaches the majority answer's supporting path triggers one focused repair; otherwise, DDRG returns the exact SC vote. Experimental results across five benchmarks show that native zero-shot DDRG improves macro-average accuracy over direct answering by 8.65 and 15.83 percentage points for Qwen2.5-7B and Llama3.1-8B. It also exceeds CoT, the evaluated GoT and GoV question-answering adaptations, and SC on this macro metric. DDRG links local verification to selective correction without enlarging the initial candidate bank.

☆ From Hindsight to Foresight: Dissecting Clinical Evidence Acquisition in Large Language Models
Ziyang Ling
Large language models (LLMs) often diagnose full sight, static clinical cases well yet struggle in foresight, dynamic diagnosis. Prior work has improved this limitation by quantifying the information models acquire and by developing stronger follow-up strategies to improve diagnostic accuracy. In contrast, we introduce a distinctive hindsight to systematically dissect why LLMs fail to acquire diagnostically critical evidence, tracing the failure from evidence identification, utilization and downstream diagnostic updating through not only diagnostic accuracy, but also evidence coverage, trajectory dynamics, and elicited probability and information entropy. Starting experiments from the breakdown at the level of capabilities, to the diagnosis of the pre-event state before the evidence arrives, and to the post-event state transition after the evidence arrives, our research has revealed the profound limitations and internal causes existing in the dynamic diagnostic process. At the same time, we have also conducted sufficient verification and generalization experiments to prove that these limitations are widespread. Together, these findings shift the central challenge from whether LLMs possess relevant clinical knowledge to whether they can identify, acquire, and effectively use the right evidence under clinical diagnosis and conduct a more detailed mechanism analysis to identify the exact location of the capability gap.
☆ Rethinking Projector Training in Multimodal LLMs NeurIPS 2026
Fabian Gröger, Naël Ouerghemi, Shuo Wen, Maria Brbić
Multimodal LLMs are typically trained by first aligning a vision encoder with a language model via projector training on large image-caption datasets, followed by joint training of the full multimodal model. Despite its widespread use, the role of the projector training stage remains poorly understood. We find that (i) using only $10$ to $20\%$ of the data for training the projector already recovers most downstream performance gains, and (ii) projectors trained on different datasets can be linearly interpolated while largely preserving performance. These results suggest that projector training mainly performs a coarse alignment, which many different projectors achieve equally well. Motivated by these findings, we propose PORTAL, a training-free projector initialization that requires neither paired image-caption data nor gradient-based optimization, but only per-modality summary statistics. PORTAL computes an optimal-transport-based map between Gaussian approximations of the vision and language feature distributions on a shared principal subspace, so that the projector training stage can be skipped entirely. Without any paired data, PORTAL matches the standard pipeline trained on 558k image-caption pairs across six LLM backbones, two vision encoders, and 16 benchmarks. It outperforms that pipeline in $3$ of $7$ (vision encoder, LLM) settings and reaches an average $98\%$ of its score across the other four.

☆ Decomposing and Predicting Annotation Disagreement in Harmful Content Across Languages
Irem Ecem Yelkanat, Faeze Ghorbanpour, Alexander Fraser
Annotator disagreement is increasingly recognized as a meaningful property of subjective tasks, rather than merely noise to be removed. Predicting which items are likely to be contested could help allocate annotation effort and route difficult cases to human review. We propose predicting disagreement from interpretable semantic properties of the text. An LLM answers a set of binary questions covering harmful-content characteristics, disagreement-related cues such as borderline severity and missing context, and language-sensitive cues such as local slurs. A classifier predicts disagreement from these answers, and we analyze which questions contribute most to the predictions. Across four datasets in English, German, and Turkish and three open-weight LLMs, our structured question features outperform direct prompting by 10.5 ROC-AUC points and the strongest text-based baselines by 5.0 points. A compact subset of eight questions performs within 1.5 ROC-AUC points of the full twenty-question set. In leave-one-dataset-out evaluation, models retain roughly 90-95% of their in-domain ROC-AUC. Across models and datasets, questions about derogation, missing context, and decision boundaries serve as the strongest predictors, suggesting that what makes harmful content contested is partly observable in the text.
☆ The Many Morals of AI: The cautionary bias of North American news narratives in global perspective
Andrew Piper, Edie Pearman
The stories told about artificial intelligence (AI) play a central role in shaping public understanding, policy debates, and expectations about technological change. News media are especially influential in this process. Using a cross-regional corpus of 12,924 English-language news articles about AI published since 2022, we examine how storytelling about AI varies across media systems in four regions: North America, China, India, and the Middle East. Leveraging large language models, we extract the implicit narrative lessons conveyed by each article---commonly known as story morals---capturing value-laden interpretations that extend beyond thematic content or sentiment. We find that North American AI coverage is uniquely characterized by narratives of caution, while under-emphasizing narratives of innovation and collaboration that are more prevalent elsewhere. However, this pattern is largely explained by a broader regional negativity bias in North American news sentiment, rather than by heightened concern about AI itself. Our findings bring into view the culturally conditioned nature of AI news narratives.

☆ CREATE: Creativity Evaluation of VLMs using the Visual Remote Associates Test
Ziqing Yang, Anubhav Jangra, Smaranda Muresan
Vision language models (VLMs) are used in creative tasks such as visual storytelling, design ideation, art interpretation, and advertising. Yet we lack principled ways to measure the lateral reasoning of these models compared to human potential. We introduce CREATE, a semi-automatically constructed benchmark containing 155 visual Remote Associates Test problems. These problems require a solver to find an underlying connection among three seemingly dissimilar images. We conduct a human study (N=25) to validate and ground the quality of our benchmark, and evaluate 11 VLMs. Our findings show that most VLMs underperform humans on this task. Our experiments indicate that the associative ability of VLMs depends on the cue concepts provided in the text and does not universally extend to visual puzzles.

☆ Empathy Is Not the Scapegoat for Declines in Factuality and Reliability AACL 2026
Letian Shi, Kanwal Mehreen, Deheng Zhang, Yuxia Wang
Language models are increasingly expected to support users in stressful or uncertain situations, where warmth and empathy are desirable. While recent work associates warmer models with reduced reliability, we show that this conclusion is incomplete. Prior studies often train primarily on empathetic dialogue, making it difficult to separate tone effects from SFT-induced distribution shift. We revisit this issue at both the model and evaluation levels. We study prompting, SFT, and RL for inducing warm behavior, and comprehensively evaluate capability preservation using an 11-dataset benchmark suite and EMOFACT under emotional contexts. Across multiple model families, we find that warmth effects are task- and model-dependent, while task-aligned adaptive tone can improve empathy and targeted reliability, although some capability trade-offs remain. ☆ How Reliable Are System-One Decisions in Medicine? A Comparative Study of Jev, Jev-like Models, and LLMs
Yanrui Du, Danyang Zhao, Sendong Zhao, Wenhao Li, Yibo Gao, Haochun Wang, Zhuoer Xu, Shiwen Cui, Bing Qin
Jev has attracted considerable attention for its rapid, generalizable decision-making, yet its medical reliability remains under explored. To address this gap, our study compares various types of system-one decision models across 15 medical datasets comprising 8,742 instances under a unified protocol. Beyond accuracy, we assess decision stability and the ability to recognize unreliable decisions. Our results show that Jev rivals leading models on primarily text-driven tasks but is less effective on numerically driven tasks, revealing a distinct capability boundary. Under question-format variations, Jev demonstrates strong robustness, achieving the lowest decision reversal rate and relatively small accuracy fluctuations. Among evaluated open-source alternatives, only StartLux exhibits comparable robustness. But these strengths do not extend to reliable abstention awareness: most evaluated models show limitations in both recognizing missing correct answers and using optional abstention. Moreover, we find that Jev's probability-based answer selection can be improved by changing how decisions are scored: normalizing option-wise binary scores yields higher retained accuracy than choice scoring. Taken together, our findings provide early guidance for improving and applying decision models in medicine.
☆ Retrieval Disagreement as a Signal: Diagnosing and Steering Multilingual Dense Embedders
Faeze Ghorbanpour, Shaghayegh Kolli, Jana Diesner, Alexander Fraser
Multilingual dense retrievers place all languages in one shared space so that a question finds relevant documents, regardless of which language they are written in. We show that the same question, asked in different languages, retrieves substantially different documents, and this disagreement is not because of weak language understanding. Each language performs well on its own and ranks candidates consistently with the other languages; divergence appears only when languages share an index. We trace the mechanism: each language keeps its own ranking, but in the shared index, its correct answers are pushed far down, buried under non-relevant documents that are either in the query's own language or in English, depending on the model. We introduce disagreement-guided steering: for each language, we contrast the documents that other languages agree on for a question against those that language retrieves idiosyncratically, and take the difference of their representations as a language-specific direction, added to that language's queries at inference. Against baselines of language-mean removal, subspace removal, and per-language rotation, it most strongly reduces same-language crowding while remaining competitive on recall, trading off against mean removal, which better preserves ranking quality.
☆ The Geometry of Language Model Logits: Generic Structure and Learned Departures
Swathi Shree Narashiman, Aravind Ramana Venkatramanan, Nambi Seshadri
Autoregressive language models produce, at every prediction step, a vocabulary-sized logit vector whose structure determines the conditional next-token distribution. We empirically identify that the ordered vocabulary-head logits follow a remarkably consistent log-linear relation \[ z_{(r)} \approx μ- α\log r. \] across prediction contexts, architectures, and scales, emerging progressively during pretraining. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic analysis of the logit geometry underlying the distributional assumptions used in the prior decoding work. While controlled order-statistic baselines spanning bounded, light-tailed, and heavier-tailed distributions can largely explain the observed log-linear geometry, they do not explain the systematic shift in the slope $α$: trained language models exhibit $α\approx 1.4$--$1.7$, sitting well above the initialization baseline ($α\approx 0.2$) . Moreover, $α$ correlates with predictive performance, suggesting that it captures learned structure beyond generic extreme-value effects.
☆ Imprinting: Knowledge Distillation Can Shift Student Behavior
Yu-Chien Tang, An-Zi Yen
Knowledge distillation trains student models on teacher-generated responses to transfer task capability, but those responses may also carry behavioral tendencies of their source. We investigate whether the source teacher can shape student behavior beyond the capability that distillation is intended to transfer. We construct harm-accepting and harm-avoiding variants of the same base teacher, use them to generate responses to ordinary reasoning and knowledge questions unrelated to safety, and distill those responses into students with different base models. Students generally acquire task capability while their downstream decisions shift toward the source teacher's safety-pragmatism disposition, a phenomenon we call imprinting. Distillation from the harm-accepting teacher reduces harm avoidance, whereas distillation from the harm-avoiding teacher increases it. Imprinting varies across teacher-student pairs and does not extend uniformly across different forms of safety behavior. Excluding a sparse set of divergence tokens reduces imprinting, whereas response rewriting preserves the source-dependent effect and can introduce additional rewriter-dependent shifts. Post-distillation preference optimization raises harm avoidance but does not eliminate the source-dependent difference. These results show that behavioral tendencies can be intentionally imprinted through the source teacher, even with ordinary task supervision.
☆ The Last Mile of Coding Agents for Long-Document Tasks: An Empirical Study on Clinical Trial Protocol Digitization
Jiacheng Lin, Geoffrey Low, Nishit Agarwal, Rahul Jain, Afrah Shafquat, Mandis Beigi, Jimeng Sun
Clinical trial protocols serve as the source of truth for study design, but are mostly written and used as unstructured PDF documents. Digitizing protocols turns static content into structured, machine-readable data that clinical software can analyze, validate, and execute. Coding agents, which select tools, execute code, and revise their approach as they work, are increasingly applied to such long-document tasks, yet it remains unclear how reliably they turn complete documents into accurate structured outputs. We study this question using protocol digitization as a testbed. We introduce TrialPD-Bench, an expert-annotated benchmark of 150 publicly available protocol PDFs spanning four task categories. Beyond final-answer performance, our evaluation measures evidence recall from agent traces, agent behavior, and cost. Across nine proprietary and open-weight models in five coding-agent harnesses, even the strongest agents do not fully solve protocol digitization. Our analysis further shows that agents usually locate the required evidence but fail in the last mile of transcribing and structuring it. We further develop TrialScribe, a domain-specific harness that equips coding agents with task skills and a verification loop. TrialScribe improves a locally served open-weight model by 6.2-59.4 points across eight metrics, enabling it to match or surpass proprietary models such as GPT-5.6-Sol.
☆ Aligning Faithfulness for Theory-Scale Autoformalization
Yuming Feng, Tadd Mao, Dhruva Arekar, Tianjun Zhong, Jiani Huang, Xujie Si, Ziyang Li
Autoformalization (AF) requires more than compilation: formal statements must preserve the meaning of their informal sources, a property known as faithfulness. An emerging frontier is the automated evaluation of this property, yet most existing methods operate at the statement level and produce whole-item verdicts, failing to reveal how individual expressions correspond or which contextual interpretations support the judgment. This omission is consequential because AF is inherently theory-scale: target statements derive their meaning from surrounding mathematical material and unstated conventions. We formulate faithfulness evaluation as a context-aware align-then-label problem: identifying phrase-level correspondences between informal and formal statements and assigning a faithfulness label to each under explicitly specified contextual interpretations and assumptions. We also introduce FaithAlign, the first theory-scale faithfulness benchmark comprising 460 items and 4,982 annotated phrase-level links, alongside FaithAlign-Gap, a companion set of 146 items with informal context and assumptions withheld. Each FaithAlign item includes the target statement pair, relevant context, formal dependencies, and, where needed, assumptions specifying one given interpretation. Across 8 models, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest defect F1 at 47.8% and identifies all defects in only 4.5% of items with two or more defects. On FaithAlign-Gap, Astra identifies only 28.2% of undecidable items as such. These results show the difficulty of recovering context-dependent correspondences, establishing FaithAlign as a testbed for fine-grained, context-aware faithfulness evaluation.
☆ Judges Follow the Reference: The Derivation Gap in Verifying Tool-Call Arguments
Linhai Ma, Yan Wang, Lingfei Qian, Yupeng Cao, Victor Gutierrez Basulto
Tool-call judges evaluate argument values proposed by LLM agents. We find that they accept a correct value less often when their input holds no reference for it, that is, no text that says what the value should be. This happens when the value is a default value from the function schema that the user did not mention, when it has to be inferred, or when the text it comes from was cut from the judge input. We call this drop in acceptance the derivation gap. It appears across all seven judges we evaluate, including System One models CLM and Jev. The gap is not only a truncation artifact: correct default values have the largest gap on a benchmark with no truncation. A controlled source-relocation test shows that judges follow the reference in their input: they favor a visible incorrect value over a correct value whose source is hidden. We introduce DeRef (Derived Reference): a separate model reads the full history without seeing the candidate and writes a reference for the judge. Across three benchmarks, DeRef improves overall argument AUROC for all judges tested. On the controlled test, it raises AUROC from chance or below to between 98.40 and 99.59.

☆ How Safe Is ChatGPT Health Advice? Evidence from Real User Conversations in Four Global South Countries
Shreyasi Roy Chowdhury, Kiran Garimella
People increasingly bring their health questions to ChatGPT, yet safety evaluations of LLM health advice rest almost entirely on clinician-authored vignettes, synthetic prompts, or simulated user profiles. The gap is widest for the Global South, where concerns about demographic bias and unsafe recommendations are well documented but have never been tested on real usage. We present, to our knowledge, the first demographically grounded safety evaluation of real-world health conversations with ChatGPT. From a consent-based data-donation corpus of 202,590 conversations from 1,252 users in India, Nigeria, Brazil, and Pakistan (December 2022 to February 2026), each linked to self-reported age and gender, we identify 15,555 health conversations, classify their health intent using an LLM classifier adapted from an established taxonomy, and score response safety under both a context-blind and a user-context-aware rubric. Roughly one in five health conversations receives a high-risk rating. Risk concentrates in symptom and condition queries and recurs through identifiable failure modes: unsafe medication or dosage guidance, harmful self-diagnosis, missing safety warnings, encouraging over-reliance on the model, and failure to refer users to professionals. Supplying the user's own accumulated context raises the high-risk share by 13-17 percentage points, and about one in four conversations rated safe in isolation turns out to be unsafe for the specific person asking. Safety evaluations built on synthetic cases are likely to miss these harms.

☆ Readout Scaling Shapes Sparse Feature Recovery
Brain Xiao, Sarah Wilson
Readout scaling changes what a sparse autoencoder (SAE) learns, but the benefit of removing it depends on the data. We use readout-canonicalized decision-space (RC-DS) coordinates to remove singular-value gains while preserving retained decision information. At a fixed readout condition number, changing the generating distribution reverses which training coordinates recover known factors best. In our controlled coordinate-matching study, recovery is highest when training restores the space in which the factors were generated. When factors are generated before readout scaling, RC-DS increases mean sparse-factor recovery from 0.506 to 0.913 at condition number 100, compared with centered-logit training under the same budget. An exact wrapper reproduces RC-DS training to numerical precision, isolating training geometry rather than additional information. In neural policies, RC-DS improves mean logit reconstruction under the tested budgets. On three fixed language-model checkpoints, RC-DS has higher mean gain-free reconstruction in all 18 tested task-sparsity settings, while centered-logit SAEs reconstruct output logits better in 17. Small-label-head controls show that longer training reduces the reconstruction gap from 0.0198 to 0.0016. Together, these results show how readout scaling, the data distribution, and training shape sparse learning. RC-DS provides an information-preserving method for studying and controlling the readout's contribution. Separate label-detection and steering tests distinguish these reconstruction effects from task-specific feature usefulness.
☆ Teacher-Guided Tool Learning for Small Language Models via Bidirectional KL Constraints
Haoxuan Gao, Qin-Wen Luo, Xiang Chen, Yu Tian, Zequn Sun, Xiaofeng Cao, Jiangchao Yao, Sheng-Jun Huang
External tools can extend the capabilities of small language models (SLMs), enabling their deployment in resource constrained environments. However, their limited capabilities make it difficult for SLMs to discover effective tool use behaviors through reinforcement learning alone, while direct teacher supervision suffers from teacher student behavioral distribution mismatch and may propagate erroneous preferences. To address these challenges, we propose Selective Teacher guided Policy Optimization (SeTPO), which coordinates student exploration with selective teacher guidance and persistent demonstration supervision. Specifically, SeTPO incorporates reverse KL derived token level teacher feedback into policy advantages on low reward trajectories, while auxiliary forward KL supervision preserves teacher demonstrated behaviors throughout training. Under an idealized bidirectional KL objective, we theoretically establish that the optimal policy achieves expected reward no lower than the teacher's while retaining a guaranteed fraction of the teacher's probability mass. Extensive experiments on BFCL v4 and API Bank demonstrate that SeTPO achieves superior overall performance over supervised fine tuning, reinforcement learning, and distillation baselines across all three student scales.
☆ Copying Is Not Grounding: Training Faithful Long-Form RAG with Authored-Sentence Credit
Hoang Pham, Duc Anh Vu, Duy Tung Doan, Cong-Duy T Nguyen, Anh Tuan Luu
In search engines and assistants, long-form RAG answers can mislead readers with claims their sources do not support. Faithfulness rewards, the share of supported sentences, train models toward fewer such claims. We show that these rewards are hacked by copying. A sentence copied from the passages is supported almost by construction, so trained models learn to copy rather than ground what they write. Common defenses only move the hack: a KL or copy penalty leaves copying in place or trades it for shorter, emptier answers and refusals. GRPO, the standard RL algorithm, makes it worse by giving every sentence its answer's advantage, reinforcing copied and grounded sentences alike. We propose Authored-Sentence Credit (ASC) , which fixes both by moving GRPO's reward and advantage from the answer to the sentence level. The reward credits only sentences that are authored rather than copied, say more than the question, and are supported by the passages. The advantage compares each sentence with every sentence sampled for the question, so one that earns no credit ranks last and is never reinforced. Across four models and two in-domain, three held-out corpora, only ASC lowers the error rate of authored sentences and writes more supported ones in every setting, by 38% and 46% at the median under held-out evaluators, giving readers more grounded information. It takes none of these shortcuts, and two LLM judges comparing whole answers prefer no baseline to the base model but rate ASC's answers on par with it. We conclude that a faithfulness reward should credit what a model writes, not what it copies.
☆ Estimating Surprisal from the Speech Signal
Zachary Hopton, Samuel Kiegeland, Francesco Ignazio Re, Patrick Haller, Matthew B. Winn, Ryan Cotterell, Eleanor Chodroff
Although language is predominantly spoken, empirical tests of surprisal theory rely almost entirely on written text. In practice, surprisal is estimated using language models (LMs) trained on text and applied to transcriptions, creating a text bottleneck that limits evaluation of the theory in the spoken modality. We remove this bottleneck by estimating surprisal directly from the speech signal using a speech LM. This raises a key question: should surprisal be defined over realization-specific acoustic inputs or over abstract linguistic units? Since a single unit can have many distinct acoustic realizations, each assigned a different probability by the model, defining surprisal over abstract units is difficult. To address this, we introduce an importance sampling estimator that approximates the probability of an abstract unit by marginalizing over realizations generated by a text-to-speech model. Using pupillometry data from a listening study, we evaluate surprisal theory in this text-free setting at multiple levels of granularity. Obtaining surprisal from several speech LMs, we consistently find that surprisal estimated via importance sampling better predicts pupil dilation than realization-specific surprisal for words. We also find that surprisal values for sub-lexical units are predictive of pupil dilation. While we find that surprisal can be estimated without recourse to text, and that marginalizing over distinct realizations of words is more predictive of lexical processing effort, surprisal as a whole is not a strong predictor of change in pupil size, regardless of its source.
☆ Reliable Memory from Unstable Growth: Curating Self-Written Agent Playbooks with Randomized Experiments
Hoang Pham, Cong-Duy Nguyen, Dong Le, Ponhvoan Srey, Anh Tuan Luu
Persistent textual memory lets language-model agents reuse lessons from past tasks to improve on future multi-step tasks without retraining. Systems such as ACE and Dynamic Cheatsheet keep it as a playbook: lessons the agent writes after each task and reads in its prompt. A key challenge is to decide which lessons improve later tasks. Current systems leave the decision to the agent itself, which makes performance unstable: the same procedure can write a playbook that helps or one that falls up to 69.6 points below no playbook. These findings motivate valuing lessons by their measured effect on task success rather than by the agent's judgment. We propose Randomized Block Curation (RBC), which measures this effect with a randomized experiment on practice tasks. It groups related lessons into blocks large enough to move success measurably and, on each practice task, includes or leaves out every block at random. Random inclusion makes tasks with and without a block comparable, so the gap in their success estimates the block's effect. RBC keeps the blocks that help and, since the same procedure can write a helpful or a harmful playbook, grows several and serves whichever curated, whole or no playbook scores best on fresh practice episodes. Across two memory systems, three models and three benchmarks, RBC raises average success to 66.9, against 55.5 with the whole playbook, 54.1 with no playbook and 53.8 when the agent's own judgment picks the lessons to keep.
☆ Receiver Decides Agent-to-Agent Negotiations
Fardeen Sadab, Alif Hasnain, Afsana Shehrin, Md. Azam Hossain, Abu Raihan Mostofa Kamal
When one language-model agent asks another for money, which of the two decides how much changes hands? We ran a round-robin tournament of 1,049 negotiations among fifteen agents. In each game a requester tries to raise money from a holder that has been told to keep a $100 endowment. Knowing which agent holds the money explains 47% of the variance in the amount transferred; knowing which agent asks explains 2%. Dropping the most extreme agents narrows the split to 22% against 4%. Changing the requester's appeal from one named person to a group changed what requesters wrote, but the amounts transferred under the two framings are statistically equivalent. The holders that lose least are the ones that turn down the opening request, and their turn-1 refusal rate ranks them by total losses (Spearman correlation = -0.73). Training moves this behaviour in both directions. Fine-tuning Qwen3-8B on a persuasion corpus with almost no refusals raised what it gave away as a holder from 12% to 84% of the endowment. Distilling two never-losing holders into a 4B student cut its losses from 95% to 1%. Evaluations of agents that hold resources should test them in the holder role, including after persuasion fine-tuning.

☆ Agaram 2.5B: The First Tamil-First Language Model Trained from Scratch with Tool Calling. A Playbook for Under-Represented Languages
Ramchand Kumaresan, Madhusudhanan G, Kaushik Kezendirane
We trained Agaram 2.5B, a 2.5-billion-parameter Tamil-English language model, from scratch on about 50 billion tokens (58% Tamil, the rest English, math, code and parallel text) on one node of eight H100 GPUs in about six days, with a new tokenizer that, unlike our first model's, keeps line breaks. Supervised fine-tuning on 134,428 conversations, whose replies an open-weight teacher model wrote, and three short targeted rounds turned it into a chat model that answers in Tamil script, romanized Tamil (Tanglish) and English and can call tools: on 25 tool prompts whose schemas appear in training it picks the right tool 23 times and gives exactly the expected arguments 13 times. The released model translates better than Sarvam-1, a 2B Indic base model, in all six FLORES and IN22 directions (FLORES chrF 54.3 into Tamil and 55.2 into English, against 49.2 and 44.2), and nearly doubles its base model on native-Tamil IndicIFEval (19.3% from 10.0%). It is weak on knowledge: reading-comprehension and knowledge benchmarks sit near chance, and on 50 held-out everyday questions written by one native speaker, one LLM judge rated 46% of its replies usable (22-24 of 50 over three passes; 95% interval for 23 of 50, 33-60%), with nearly every failure a wrong fact. Our two further SFT experiments changed answer format but not the knowledge measures. We also describe our first model, Agaram 1.5B, whose failures shaped the choices here; how the judge's scores varied between passes on identical replies; and, item by item, what the targeted rounds trained against and where frontier-model text entered the data. The project ran on compute credits and our own funds, about USD 13,300 across both models; the weights are released (licence to be announced after legal review), with GGUF files for llama.cpp, at https://huggingface.co/murai-labs/agaram-2.5b-instruct; code at https://github.com/Murai-Labs/Agaram2.5b

☆ Do Factuality Methods Make LLMs More Factual? Reproducibility and Fair Comparison
Pratuat Amatya, Vinay Setty, V Venktesh, Avishek Anand
Large Language Models (LLMs) are aligned to human preferences, after pre-training and supervised fine-tuning steps. However, standard RL alignment steps such as RLHF do not focus on factuality of LLMs, hence they may generate non-factual information, which could result in unintended consequences. In response, a number of factuality alignment approaches have been proposed in the literature which claim to improve factuality of LLMs on their own benchmarks. However, these methods have not been reproduced independently and are also not comparable since each uses a different base model, training data, evaluation set, judge, and metric. In this paper, we present the first independent reproduction of five factuality alignment methods, which span on-policy RL (PPO, GRPO, and Dr.~GRPO) and offline preference optimization (DPO and KTO), and compare them on a common benchmark with common evaluation metrics. We found that none of these methods reproduces in full, and on the common benchmark none of the methods significantly outperforms the base model. On closer inspection, the rewards optimize for format, length, or brevity rather than factuality and relevance, whereas the choice between on-policy and offline training matters little. Furthermore, the evaluation metrics and benchmarks are also part of the problem since they do not measure relevance of text to the prompt. Since there is no ground truth associated with the prompts, the reliance on LLM judges and retrieval for verification also makes the evaluation fragile. We release our code, data, and checkpoints.
☆ Revisiting the Estimation of Unigram Surprisal in Computational Psycholinguistics
Zachary Hopton, Francesco Ignazio Re, Samuel Kiegeland, Andreas Opedal, Eleanor Chodroff, Ryan Cotterell
Contextual and unigram surprisal are standard predictors of reading time in computational psycholinguistics. While contextual surprisal is typically derived from neural language models, unigram surprisal is usually obtained from independent corpus counts. Although both quantities are deterministic functionals of a single underlying distribution---capturing conditional and marginal structure respectively---this practice breaks their probabilistic link and may introduce estimation error from the mismatch. We propose three principles for estimating unigram surprisal in psycholinguistic research, including the requirement that both quantities be derived from the same language model. We operationalize this principle by introducing a Monte Carlo estimator of unigram surprisal from a language model, along with a marginalized variant that has lower variance empirically. When unigram surprisal is re-estimated under this unified framework, we find evidence that predictors change in their predictive contribution to reading time. We also confirm prior findings that frequency can be a stronger predictor of processing effort than contextual surprisal under this unified framework.
☆ Not All Drift Is a Mechanism: Tracing Prompt Adaptation in LLMs
Mikhail Aleksandrov, Nail Bashirov, Maksim Ivanov, Andrew Tsukanov, Timofey Karyagin, Alexander Miasnikov, Gleb Molodtsov, Valentin Samokhin, Aleksandr Beznosikov
Prompt-based adaptation offers several ways to specialize a frozen language model, ranging from natural-language prompt optimization to learned continuous prompts. These methods are usually compared through downstream performance, although similar outputs need not arise from similar internal computations. We study how discrete prompt optimization, prompt tuning, and prefix tuning alter the processing of dense and mixture-of-experts language models. Our analysis shows that the location of the adaptation predicts how task information is carried to the output: optimized text primarily changes response construction, input-level prompts write their influence into content representations, and layer-wise prefixes maintain direct control through attention. In MoE models, these differences further induce distinct expert-routing patterns. At the same time, common representation-level measurements respond strongly to generic context changes and do not reliably distinguish useful adaptations from matched placebos. We argue that understanding prompt adaptation requires separating representation drift from the part of that drift that is aligned with, and causally used by, the model's task computation. This perspective provides practical guidance for selecting, evaluating, and interpreting prompt adaptation methods.
☆ Prompt-Injection Probes Learn the Conversation, Not Just the Injection
Kaan Kale, Sriram Vishwanath
Linear probes on a model's hidden states are used as cheap monitors for indirect prompt injection: they screen the tool outputs an agent reads and send suspicious ones to a slower second check, so every false alarm has a cost. But a probe learns whatever separates its training examples. The standard evaluation of injection probes can be passed without the model: conversation position and text lengths alone reach 0.91-0.98 AUROC. When each attack is compared with the same conversation without the injection, or with harmless text formatted like an injection, standard probes flag most of these harmless cases. We add such cases to the training data as matched negatives. Across six open models, in applications seen during training and at the default threshold, changing only the training data cuts false alarms on injection-free conversations from 41-80% to 4-27%, and on harmless look-alikes from 92-98% to 3-16%. With a threshold set on normal traffic, our probe still catches a median 95% of the attacks that fooled the agent (91% without our data). In new applications, false alarms stay low, but detection depends on the model and on how the threshold is set. The same data also helps a text-only filter.

☆ Multi-Stream Residual Computation in Diffusion Language Models
Ekaterina Alimaskina, Suren Kazarian, Gleb Molodtsov, Aleksandr Beznosikov
Diffusion language models reconstruct masked tokens in parallel, offering an alternative to autoregressive generation. Yet their Transformer backbones typically carry visible evidence and evolving masked predictions through a single residual stream. We equip masked diffusion models with multiple residual streams that persist through depth and adaptive routing between them, then investigate how they support these complementary roles. On the pretraining task, two streams reduce reconstruction perplexity by 5.71% on validation data and 5.13% on average across external corpora compared with standard residual connections. We observe that, without predefined roles, visible and masked positions learn distinct routing patterns. Causal interventions show that this distinction organizes read and write routing, while token-specific write strength remains important within each role. Guided by these findings, a model trained with role-conditioned routing recovers half the validation gain from adding a second stream. State interventions explain the benefit of the additional stream: it preserves early token representations for later reuse while evidence accumulates at masked positions. This mechanism recurs at a larger model scale, showing how persistent streams support context preservation alongside prediction refinement.
