☆ PRISM: Calibrating Black-Box Judges with Counterfactual Probes
Kakei Yamamoto, So Kuroki
Large language models are increasingly used as judges to reduce reliance on costly human evaluation, yet their scores can systematically disagree with human judgments. We ask whether responses to an explicitly imposed evaluative bias can help correct a judge's implicit scoring biases. We introduce PRISM (Probe-based Residual Inference of Scoring Misalignment), which holds the evaluated content fixed and measures score changes when attributes are emphasized or de-emphasized. A local sensitivity model motivates representing these responses as a surrogate Jacobian. Ridge regression on human labels learns a correction to the baseline score. The resulting scoring protocol is rubric-free: it uses overall scores without requesting criterion-level ratings. In the main Jev and Sol comparisons on SummEval summarization and HANNA story evaluation, PRISM and its learned-baseline variant significantly improve agreement with human rankings over uncalibrated scores while remaining competitive with rubric-based methods. A controlled SummEval comparison yields similar agreement for rubric-derived quality probes and style probes, supporting calibration without reproducing rubric criteria. The learned coefficients reveal shared and opposing correction directions across judges.
☆ Pin It on Anyone: Certified Targeted Displacement of Multi-Bit LLM Watermarks Without the Key
Paweł Batorski, Przemysław Spurek, Paul Swoboda
Multi-bit watermarking embeds a message, typically an account identifier, into LLM output so that harmful text can be traced to its origin. We show an attack technique that allows to alter the watermarking message. This allows to, for example, exchange the embedded account ID to an arbitrary different one. The attacker mainly needs access to a few accounts for generating LLM output and model logits, but crucially not the secret key used to watermark. The attacker then rewrites the text, keeping its meaning, so that the watermarking message decoder recovers the watermarking message embedded by the attacker. We are, to our knowledge, the first to propose such an attack, which we call a targeted displacement. Theoretically, we present upper and lower bounds on the probability of our attack succeeding. This bound depends on the number of accounts the attacker has access to, the length of the message and some characteristics of the given text. Our attack can succeed whenever we can bound the ambiguity of the generated text well enough. Empirically, we test our attack technique against five watermarking methods and four LLMs. Attack success tracks our predicted bounds, allowing us to alter watermarking messages in almost all cases with high accuracy, given enough accounts. Our code can be found at https://github.com/Batorskq/PinIt.
☆ Lightweight but Linguistically Fair? Quantisation Robustness across Bavarian, German, and English
Jophin John, Michael Hoffmann, Piroska Lendvai, Florent Dufour
Post-training quantisation reduces the memory cost of large language models, but it remains unclear whether compression preserves language skills of regional varieties as reliably as their standard-language counterparts. We study this question for Bavarian, Standard German, and English using BAVGROUND, a multiple-choice benchmark, where 206 source questions are aligned across all three languages. Across 14 open-weight 7B--10B instruction models, we compare BF16 with 8-bit GPTQ and two 4-bit settings, GPTQ and AWQ. Eight-bit GPTQ remains close to BF16 and largely preserves the relative behaviour of the three languages, while 4-bit quantisation was found to be much less stable. The two 4-bit methods reduce average accuracy by about 1.4--1.5 percentage points and change 11.9--16.0% of BF16 decisions, with changes concentrated on examples where the BF16 model is already uncertain. This instability does not translate into a consistent Bavarian disadvantage. Although Bavarian, a low-resource dialect, has lower baseline accuracy than German and English, the language most affected at 4 bits varies across models and quantisation methods. Calibration further shapes these cross-language effects: the relative Bavarian--German effect changes with calibration language, while changing the calibration source has little average effect on GPTQ-4 but can affect AWQ-4 much more strongly. Free-form evaluation on five models further shows that 4-bit compression can degrade generated responses even in settings where multiple-choice accuracy changes only modestly, while 8-bit GPTQ remains comparatively stable. Overall, our results show that quantisation robustness for regional language varieties depends on the model, quantisation method, calibration data, and evaluation setting, and cannot be measured by aggregate multilingual accuracy alone.
☆ Probing the Geometry of Color Representations in Multilingual Language Models
Pablo Loyola, Edison Marrese-Taylor, Andrés Hoyos-Idrobo
Do multilingual language models learn a universal internal geometry for color, and is it perceptually grounded or primarily linguistic? We decompose color representations into two geometric axes: (P1) alignment to perceptual color space, and (P2) cross-lingual symmetry. Analyzing layerwise embeddings across models and multilingual datasets, we find that perceptual grounding is weak: alignment to CIE-LAB peaks in intermediate layers, then collapses toward output layers. Cross-lingual symmetry degrades with depth, suggesting later layers encode language-specific structure. Functionally, we show that geometric properties predict task performance: models achieve 96.7% accuracy on color classification at layers where P1 alignment peaks, with P1 alignment (r=0.70) significantly correlating with downstream capabilities. Our results reveal a dissociation: text-only LMs learn universal color-language geometry, not universal color-perception geometry, providing diagnostics for where perceptual structure is preserved in language models.
☆ Jev-Guard: Fast Routing and Auditing Agent Actions over Editable Policy Trees
Wenbin Hu, Huihao Jing, Haochen Shi, Yuxuan Liu, Changxuan Fan, Sirui Zhang, Kawai Chung, Hanyu Yang, Zhenhuan Wang, Ziyi Chen, Haoran Li, Yangqiu Song
Agent harnesses require a low-latency guardrail model together with customizable audit policies. However, deploying an auto-regressive guardrail model remains challenging: such models incur high inference latency and are often poorly aligned with customized policies. To address these problems, we present Jev-Guard, a unified model for safety classification and policy routing. We further construct a policy tree over which Jev-Guard performs routing, spanning diverse domains including content moderation, cybersecurity, privacy, and legal compliance. Specifically, Jev-Guard first navigates the policy tree to select the applicable audit policy given the caller's audit context, and then performs safety classification conditioned on the retrieved policies. On 7,033 evaluation cases, Jev-Guard achieves 93.72\% safety accuracy, a mean latency of 58ms, and 93.10\% hierarchical routing accuracy. The full routing-and-classification pipeline averages 240ms. Furthermore, we integrate Jev-Guard into the Hermes harness framework. Across agent trajectories, its separate permission gate cuts mean review time per task from 4.80s for an SFT-LLM judge to 1.33s, with no policy violations are observed for either guard.
☆ Prompt or Policy? Optimizing Physician Slate Presentations with LLM User Simulators
Andrei Niculae, Adrian Cosma, Emilian Radoi
Choosing the right physician is a key challenge for patients seeking medical care. Physician recommendation systems identify relevant doctors, but patients still find it difficult to compare their profiles to make a decision. We formulate slate presentation optimization: given a patient's medical case and a fixed, ranked list of physicians, we generate short, case-specific summaries that make doctors easier to compare, while remaining grounded in the information in their profiles. To optimize physician presentations without relying on real patient interactions, we develop an offline framework that uses persona-conditioned LLMs to simulate patient choices. We generate patient personas from attributes extracted from each medical case and evaluate presentations for factual accuracy, relevance, naturalness and simulated patient choice. We also use a second LLM judge, unseen during optimization, to assess simulator-specific overfitting. We compare automatic prompt-level optimization with GEPA, parameter-level optimization with GRPO, and their combination across two model sizes. GRPO substantially outperforms GEPA in satisfying the full set of constraints, and its gains in simulated conversion persist under the unseen judge. GEPA alone brings smaller improvements, but using its optimized prompt to initialize GRPO speeds up training and achieves the highest simulated conversion. These results show that prompt-level and parameter-level optimization play complementary roles in improving how physician recommendations are presented.
☆ SimInter: Evidence-Grounded User Simulation with Interview-Based Profile Enrichment for Conversational Recommender Systems
Erfan Shahabi, Amirhossein Roshandel, Mohammad Sadegh Sadeghi, Azadeh Shakery
Conversational Recommender Systems (CRSs) require reliable user simulators for cost-effective evaluation. ☆ LegalRewardBench: Benchmarking Reward Models for Legal Preference Evaluation
Hui Huang, Jie Zhou, Hongli Zhou, Qundong Shi, Shuyuan Zheng, Yini Su, Shaochun Wang, Biyuan Lin, Muyun Yang, Tiejun Zhao

☆ JevGPT: Thinking Fast and Slow in Autonomous Research
Yasheng Sun, Yanning Dai, Yimeng Chen, Nanbo Li, Firas Laakom, Yifan Xu, Piotr Piękos, Mateusz Ostaszewski, Wenyi Wang, Cheng Luo, Yuhui Wang, Jürgen Schmidhuber
Autonomous research agents must acquire and reason over evidence within a limited budget. ☆ PAINT: A Framework for Multilingual Pain Representation from Medical Licensing Exams to Real-World Clinical Texts EMNLP 2026
Boya Zhang, Yuanchao Ma, Jiageng Wu, Krittaphas Chaisutyakorn, Rodrigo Gameiro, Ahram Han, Chien-Yu Huang, Nikhil Jaiswal, Marianna Leite, Miriam Mora-Mau, Jaime Pineda-Moreno, Siena Placino, Ryohei Yamamoto, Yugang Jia, Jie Yang, Leo Anthony Celi, Douglas Teodoro
Pain is one of the most common and clinically important symptoms, reflecting disease onset, progression, and treatment response. ☆ Encrypted Skills Can Become New Security Exploits If AI Agents Know How to Decipher
Yan-Lun Chen, Pin-Yu Chen, Chia-Mu Yu
Third-party skills can expose LLM agents to malicious instructions hidden by encoding. We introduce Ciphered Skill Injection (CSI), which embeds such instructions in skill files for agents to decode and follow. We evaluate Caesar and layered variants across eight models and 139 injection-task pairs, separating instruction recovery from attack success. In the primary setting with shortened skills, the variants achieve attack success rates of up to 47.5% and 40.3%, respectively. ☆ Are We Testing Too Much? A Survey of Benchmark Compression for Efficient Evaluation of Large Language Models
Guoming Ling, Yupei Lin, Zhongzhan Huang, Junxin Li, Junjie Liu, Shanshan Zhong, Hefeng Wu, Liang Lin
Benchmarks are essential for evaluating and improving large language models (LLMs). Yet many widely used benchmarks are redundant, making evaluation unnecessarily expensive. Benchmark compression lowers this cost by estimating full-benchmark scores and rankings from a partial evaluation. Research in this area has grown rapidly since 2024, but to our knowledge no survey has systematically organized its methods and findings. We review 119 papers to explain how methods choose what to evaluate, recover full-benchmark results, and verify their reliability. From this analysis, we develop a taxonomy covering compression within individual benchmarks, across benchmark suites, and during adaptive evaluation, together with work on redundancy and reliability. We compare their information needs, total costs, reliability checks, and limitations. Methods reduce evaluation costs by selecting representative subsets, using cheaper or reusable information, and testing adaptively, but risk poor coverage, inaccurate estimates, and sequential delays. The resulting taxonomy and practical guide help researchers choose, validate, and deploy methods for more efficient and reliable LLM evaluation.
☆ PACT: A Pragmatic Prompting Framework for Expressive and Voice-Preserving Speech-to-Speech Translation

☆ Consider, Then Choose: Order-Invariant Decisions in System One Models
Jianguo Huang, Yao Long Teng, Yanchen Deng, Bo An
System One models answer typed questions in a single forward pass and promise the same answer for the same input. For choice questions, this promise fails: the answer depends on the order of the options, increasingly so as they grow in number, and averaging over their cyclic shifts reduces this dependence without removing it. Scoring each option alone, which the order cannot affect, shows that the order changes the answer mostly when the model is unsure among its top few options, even though the listwise answer looks confident. We therefore propose $\textbf{Runoff}$, which considers, then chooses: it scores each option alone to keep a few candidates, then compares them in listings where each candidate takes every position. It needs no training and can run in two forward passes, and its answer does not depend on the option order by construction. On two open models and five datasets, it changes no answer under shuffling, against 9.3% for cyclic averaging, and with 20 options it is about as accurate and three to four times faster. Wrapped around eight System One models, it removes the order dependence of the five benchmarks, while cyclic averaging changes up to 15.4%.
☆ TextualRL: Textual Reinforcement Learning for Context Management
Tianyu Huai, Jie Zhou, Bo Mao, Qin Chen, Liang He, Xipeng Qiu
LLM agents can adapt to task feedback by revising the textual context that guides their behavior, without changing their model parameters. However, turning execution rewards into reusable context updates is challenging: individual trajectories offer limited comparative evidence about what to change, while task-specific fixes may overgeneralize when incorporated into a shared context. We introduce TextualRL, a gradient-free reinforcement learning framework that treats a group of rollouts from the same task under the same context, rather than an isolated trajectory, as the unit of policy critique. Groupwise Policy Critique contrasts successful and failed rollouts within a task; when outcomes are uniform, it draws on cross-task evidence to identify behaviors worth preserving or formulate repair hypotheses. Cross-Group Policy Update reviews the resulting proposals against evidence pooled across groups, including observations that yield no proposed edit, to refine their content and applicability. Reviewed proposals form a bounded textual patch, with adoption of the candidate context determined by held-out validation rewards. Together, these components establish a reward-driven policy optimization loop in which multiple attempts jointly inform reusable context updates. We evaluate TextualRL with five target agents on six benchmarks. Averaged over the six benchmarks, TextualRL improves test accuracy over the base model by 24.4 and 15.3 percentage points for GPT-5.5 and Qwen3.8-27B, respectively.

Dianjing Fan, Yao Li, Kyaw Hpone Myint, Dwipam Katariya, Alexandre G. R. Day, Pranab Mohanty, Giri Iyengar

♻ ☆ LoopVL: Recurrent Visual Intelligence
Zhe Qian, Ziyang Gong, Zhongxing Xu, Hehan Li, Zhonghua Wang, Hanmeng Liu, Liwei Qian, Xin Pei, Peizhi Xu, Fei Luo, Mingxuan Wang, Xue Yang, Shiwei liu, Yanbiao Ma, Junchi Yan, Jungong Han

♻ ☆ FinTradeBench: A Financial Reasoning Benchmark for LLMs
Yogesh Agrawal, Aniruddha Dutta, Md Mahadi Hasan, Santu Karmaker, Aritra Dutta
Financial analysis requires reasoning over heterogeneous signals, including company fundamentals from regulatory filings and trading signals derived from market prices. However, existing financial benchmarks focus almost exclusively on regulatory filings, neglecting market dynamics and cross-signal interactions. We introduce FinTradeBench, a benchmark of 1,400 questions covering NASDAQ-100 stocks over a ten-year window. To holistically evaluate financial reasoning, FinTradeBench includes questions in three reasoning categories: fundamentals-focused, trading-signal-focused, and hybrid questions that require cross-signal reasoning. Our benchmark starts with 150 expert-authored seed questions, which are then scaled to 1400. To ensure reliable evaluation, we create a Core400 subset in which every item is expert-reviewed. In zero-shot (No-RAG) response generation, we observe that all models perform substantially worse on trading-focused questions compared to fundamental-focused questions. To identify failure causes, we evaluate LLMs under controlled settings including retrieval-based baselines, tool-assisted computation, oracle conditions, and time-series-specialized pipelines. LLMs' capabilities to do computation and evidence selection contribute most to reasoning failures. Additionally, retrieval significantly improves trading-signal reasoning but does not improve fundamental reasoning, where identifying relevant signals remains the dominant failure mode. Finally, rather than relying on a small, fine-tuned model for time-series reasoning, it is better to summarize the time-series data and leverage LLMs' reasoning capabilities.

♻ ☆ LLM-as-Jev: LLMs Are Already Jev-Style Decision Models -- When and How to Fine-Tune Them
Yinheng Li, Justin Wagle
Jev-style decision models have gained surging traction by making decision-making dramatically faster and more efficient, returning categorical probability distributions over predefined options without generating free-form text. However, because Jev's architecture and training methodologies remain proprietary, how to replicate these capabilities effectively remains an open question. In this work, we investigate the extent to which general-purpose LLMs already possess this capability out of the box, and when fine-tuning is actually necessary. We present LLM-as-Jev, an architecture-preserving framework offering both a training-free inference recipe and a fine-tuning objective with KL-divergence anchors. Across Qwen3.5-4B and Qwen3-0.6B, we find that modern LLMs are inherently effective decision models: off the shelf, frozen models match or exceed existing training-free readouts on the same 4B backbone, scale to arbitrary option counts, and natively handle multimodal decisions. They also seamlessly inherit the backbone's thinking mode: while Jev was introduced as a System 1 model, "Thinking Jev" leverages budgeted test-time reasoning to boost accuracy on challenging tasks without training. Fine-tuning provides targeted gains for smaller models and complex routing, while our KL anchors prevent behavioral degradation in general text generation.
♻ ☆ Source or It Didn't Happen: A Multi-Agent Framework for Citation Hallucination Detection
Mingzhe Li, Zhiqiang Lin, Shiqing Ma
Large language models are increasingly used in scientific writing, yet they can fabricate citation-shaped references that appear plausible but fail bibliographic verification. Existing detectors often reduce verification to binary found/not-found decisions and rely on brittle parsing or incomplete retrieval, offering little field-level signal to auditors. We reframe citation hallucination detection as taxonomy-aligned field-level adjudication and introduce a 12-code taxonomy spanning Real, Potential, and Hallucinated citations. Based on this taxonomy, we build CiteTracer, a cascading multi-agent detector that extracts structured citations from PDF and BibTeX, retrieves evidence through cache lookup, URL fetch, scholar connectors, and web search, applies deterministic field matching, and routes ambiguous cases to class-specialist judgers. We release a benchmark of 2,450 synthetic citations built from real seeds with controlled LLM mutations, paired with 957 real-world fabricated citations drawn from desk-rejected submissions at ICLR 2026 and an anonymous conference. CiteTracer reaches 97.1% accuracy on the synthetic benchmark, with class-level F1 scores of 97.0, 95.8, and 98.5 for Real, Potential, and Hallucinated, respectively, and detects 97.1% of fabrications on the real-world set without abstaining. ♻ ☆ End-to-End Spoken Grammatical Error Correction
Mengjie Qian, Rao Ma, Stefano Bannò, Mark J. F. Gales, Kate M. Knill
Grammatical Error Correction (GEC) and feedback play a vital role in supporting second language (L2) learners, educators, and examiners. While written GEC is well-established, spoken GEC (SGEC), aiming to provide feedback based on learners' speech, poses additional challenges due to disfluencies, transcription errors, and the lack of structured input. SGEC systems typically follow a cascaded pipeline consisting of Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR), disfluency detection, and GEC, making them vulnerable to error propagation across modules. This work examines an End-to-End (E2E) framework for SGEC and feedback generation, highlighting challenges and possible solutions when developing these systems. Cascaded, partial-cascaded and E2E architectures are compared, all built on the Whisper foundation model. A challenge for E2E systems is the scarcity of GEC labeled spoken data. To address this, an automatic pseudo-labeling framework is examined, increasing the training data from 77 to over 2500 hours. To improve the accuracy of the SGEC system, additional contextual information, exploiting the ASR output, is investigated. Candidate feedback of their mistakes is an essential step to improving performance. In E2E systems the SGEC output must be compared with an estimate of the fluent transcription to obtain the feedback. To improve the precision of this feedback, a novel reference alignment process is proposed that aims to remove hypothesised edits that results from fluent transcription errors. Finally, these approaches are combined with an edit confidence estimation approach, to exclude low-confidence edits. Experiments on the in-house Linguaskill (LNG) corpora and the publicly available Speak & Improve (S&I) corpus show that the proposed approaches significantly boost E2E SGEC performance.

♻ ☆ One Skill, Two Masters: Serving Users and Attackers through Skill Injection
Haochang Hao, Dehai Min, Zhifang Zhang, Yunbei Zhang, Miao Xu, Yingqiang Ge, Philip S. Yu, Lu Cheng
Large language model agents use skills that package reusable instructions, code, and resources. Existing skill-injection attacks exploit trust in these packages by adding malicious content. As agents become more capable, however, they can skip unnecessary steps, inspect code, and reject unrelated actions. We introduce PERSIST, which connects attacker-chosen actions to useful functions for recurring tasks while reducing source inspection and expressed suspicion. It builds these helpers from simulated executions without access to real evaluation tasks or feedback. Skill descriptions introduce the helpers, while usage instructions explain how to call them. The extra action resides in an imported module separate from the helper entry file. Executing the helper can then serve both the user's task and the attacker's objective. Experiments on SkillsBench and Skill-Inject show that frozen packages transfer across four agent--model configurations and generalize to multiple unseen tasks without revision. Harmful effects can occur alongside successful task completion. A separate matched comparison shows higher harmful-effect rates with less source inspection and expressed suspicion than two baselines. Paired case studies illustrate how PERSIST can evade rejection, while ablations confirm the contributions of documentation guidance and module separation. Code: https://github.com/liofoil/SkillSafety.
comment: Title changed from "Poise: Position-Aware One-Instruction Skill Injection for Silent Execution on LLM Agents" to "One Skill, Two Masters: Serving Users and Attackers through Skill Injection"; the manuscript, figures, appendices, and reproducibility details have been updated
♻ ☆ Write, Execute, Refine: From Skill Follower to Skill Optimizer via Reinforcement Learning from Execution Feedback
Kang Peng, Zhiwei Zhang, Yichen Zhang, Zezhong Wang, Yiming Du, Geng Tu, Baojun Wang, Bin Liang, Ruifeng Xu, Kam-Fai Wong
Expert-written natural language skills can improve tool-using agents, yet agent-authored skills perform 8--11 points worse than using no skill. While inference-time loops can repair individual skills per task, they leave the underlying generator's refinement capability unimproved. We study how to organize execution experience from intermediate skills into training states for an optimizer. We introduce WER(Write, Execute, and Refine), a multi-phase framework that trains a Skill Optimizer outside a frozen executor. The optimizer proposes skills, a frozen agent executes each repeatedly, and a programmatic verifier scores the outcomes. These scores serve as group-relative rewards and filter for informative, mixed-outcome instances. Matched successful and failed trajectories from these records form the next phase's refinement states, so the optimizer learns from the consequences of its earlier outputs. On BFCL v4 multi-turn and tau2-bench, WER improves average Pass@1 over the no-skill baseline by 7.80 and 6.06 points, respectively. Under an identical refinement workflow, it outperforms the same backbone without optimizer training by 10.02 and 12.50 points. Notably, our trained 4B optimizer achieves 76.63\% on BFCL v4, outperforming all evaluated off-the-shelf general-purpose LLMs in the same role on average. Our code is available at https://github.com/littlepkk/wer-training.
♻ ☆ GALA: Generation-Aware Cross-Modal Alignment for Text-to-Time-Series Synthesis
Haochen Zhang, Gengwei Zhang, Laura Yao, Nicholas Konz, Tianlong Chen
Synthesizing time series from natural language is emerging as the most expressive form of controllable time series generation. However, existing text-conditioned generators either take caption embeddings frozen from off-the-shelf text encoders, or adapt the encoder end-to-end, letting the denoising loss shape the embeddings only as a by-product. In either case, the conditioning representation is never deliberately matched to the signal modality, leaving it ill-suited to guide generation. We address this by introducing GALA: Generation-Aware cross-modaL Alignment for text conditional time series generation. GALA is a two-stage approach that first contrastively couples a pretrained text encoder with a time-series foundation model into a shared embedding space with both encoders adapted to generation by an auxiliary generative loss, and then freezes the resulting caption embedding to drive a flow-matching generator. On TSFragment-600K, spanning four domains and three fragment lengths, GALA sets a new state of the art, ranking first in 30 of 36 metric columns and reaching an average rank of 1.08/1.08/1.42 at lengths 24/48/96 against 1.92/2.00/1.75 for the strongest baseline. We further find that generator-internal text encoders force a trade-off between fidelity and caption adherence, whereas conditioning on the aligned embedding breaks it: FID, CTTP, and JFTSD all improve at once. Ablating the auxiliary loss degrades FID, CTTP and JFTSD together, it indicates the generative term is a necessary component of the alignment rather than an add-on.

♻ ☆ VQEL: Enabling Self-Play in Emergent Language Games via Agent-Internal Vector Quantization
Mohammad Mahdi Samiei Paqaleh, Mehdi Jamalkhah, Mahdieh Soleymani Baghshah
Emergent Language (EL) focuses on the emergence of communication among artificial agents. Although symbolic communication channels more closely mirror the discrete nature of human language, learning such protocols remains fundamentally difficult due to the non-differentiability of symbol sampling. Existing approaches typically rely on high-variance gradient estimators such as REINFORCE or on continuous relaxations such as Gumbel-Softmax, both of which suffer from limitations in training stability and scalability. Motivated by cognitive theories that emphasize intrapersonal processes preceding communication, we explore self-play as a substrate for language emergence prior to mutual interaction. We introduce Vector Quantized Emergent Language (VQEL), a novel architecture that incorporates vector quantization into the message generation process. VQEL enables agents to perform self-play using discrete internal representations derived from a learned codebook while preserving end-to-end differentiability. Moreover, the resulting vector-quantized codebook naturally induces a symbolic vocabulary that can be directly transferred and aligned during subsequent mutual play with other agents. Empirical results show that agents pretrained via VQEL self-play achieve more consistent symbol alignment and higher task success when later engaged in mutual interaction. These findings position self-play as a principled and effective mechanism for learning discrete communication protocols, addressing key optimization and representational challenges in emergent language systems.
♻ ☆ TontaubeV1: Streaming Text-to-Speech with Hierarchical Codec Modeling and Bounded Context
Fritz Cremer, Jonathan Cremer
Text-to-speech systems often face a trade-off between natural prosody and efficient inference: higher perceptual quality usually comes at increased computational cost and latency. We present TontaubeV1, a model that preserves natural prosody while enabling streaming from a single consumer GPU. Speech is encoded by the hierarchical DualCodec representation at 12.5 Hz, which separates a semantic stream from successive acoustic refinements. Our design assumes that prosodic structure is largely established when the semantic stream is generated, and allocates capacity accordingly: a Qwen3-1.7B-derived transformer predicts that stream and thereby the utterance duration, while three progressively smaller Qwen3-0.6B-derived transformers each add one acoustic refinement. Text is tokenized per character rather than by subword. Paired text and audio markers at shared positions support long-form generation with bounded context, and overlapping DualCodec reconstructions are mapped into the VibeVoice acoustic latent space and decoded causally, enabling streaming despite DualCodec's noncausal decoder. The model accepts up to one minute of reference audio for voice conditioning and is designed primarily for English and German, with additional multilingual support. The four predictors total 2.9B parameters; on a single RTX 5090 the streaming path reaches about 200 ms to first audio. In separate non-streaming measurements, the end-to-end real-time factor (RTF) is 0.08 for one input and the aggregate RTF is 0.02 across eight concurrent inputs. In a blinded study, 40 listeners preferred TontaubeV1's prosody over Fish Audio S2 Pro in 70.25% of 400 comparisons (95% CI: 64.2-75.7%). On an LLM-as-a-judge benchmark, it matches ElevenLabs Flash v2.5 and outperforms the April 2026 Gradium API and Cartesia Sonic 3 on prosody. The weights are released on Hugging Face under the Tontaube Community Model License 1.0.

♻ ☆ Rewarding Efficient Reasoning Improves Abstention on Underspecified Tasks in Reasoning Models
Polina Tsvilodub, Max Höth, Michael Franke, Björn Deiseroth, Carina Kauf
While modern large reasoning models (LRMs) excel at providing correct answers in many tasks, we provide additional evidence that they often struggle with a critical capability: knowing when to abstain from answering. We analyze this gap by comparing LRM behavior to results from an exploratory human study, revealing that human reasoning effort on unanswerable tasks isn't different from answerable tasks, whereas LRMs waste computational resources by generating longer Chains of Thought (CoTs) on unanswerable than on answerable prompts. To overcome this inefficiency, we take inspiration from a resource-rational perspective on human cognition and introduce a novel GRPO reward that encourages efficient reasoning about whether the task contains all the information needed to solve it. Fine-tuning several 4B and an 8B LRM with this reward leads to human-like abstention performance gains (+14.7% on average) while retaining answering capabilities and boosting the models' efficiency (44% shorter CoTs on average).

♻ ☆ Verifiable Retrieval for Legal Reasoning through Structure-driven Knowledge Graph ISWC 2026
Ibrahim Siddig, Sviatoslav Tugeev, Munir Georges
Any automation within reliability-critical contexts such as medicine or law has to be transparent and verifiable. Despite their advantages, current LLM-based systems do not meet these requirements due to their inherent black-box nature. Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) attempts to ground the generative process in the relevant interpretable documents, thereby reducing the risk of AI 'hallucinations' and potentially enabling verification of the initial generation context. However, as the number and size of potential sources increases, naive retrieval approaches tend to perform worse, ignoring critical context elements. In this paper we aim to tackle this problem in the context of German Legal Code. Using structure-driven parsing to create a Knowledge Graph (KG) for the source document navigation and retrieval, we compare several KG-informed chunking and retrieval strategies and evaluate their effect on the accuracy of retrieved citations.

♻ ☆ Factorized Hypothesis Search for Evidence-to-Taxonomy Retrieval
Linhai Ma, Ethan F. Wei, Xueqing Peng, Yan Wang, Lingfei Qian, Víctor Gutiérrez-Basulto
Large-taxonomy retrieval often assumes that the input already expresses the target concept. In many settings, however, the input is indirect evidence, such as a table cell whose meaning depends on its row, column, datatype, and context. We call this mismatch the retrieval readiness gap. Our analysis shows that the current index retrieves the target reliably when its semantics are explicit, while raw evidence often leaves it deep in the ranking. We propose Factorized Hypothesis Search (FHS), which maintains multiple partial interpretations over named semantic dimensions. These hypotheses support structured query rendering, multi-hypothesis retrieval, and dimension-level candidate verification. On both financial taxonomy tagging and CodiEsp clinical coding tasks, FHS achieves the best Recall@1, MRR, and final accuracy among the non-oracle methods. Replacing the factorized hypothesis path with a free-text ensemble causes the largest drop in head-ranking performance, while sequential refinement provides no additional gain over FHS's strong parallel first round.

♻ ☆ The Shared Latent Structure of LLM Psychometric Responses
Hubert Plisiecki, Sabina Siudaj, Kacper Dudzic, Anna Sterna, Maciej Gorski, Karolina Drozdz, Marcin Moskalewicz
Human psychometric questionnaires are increasingly used to characterize large language models (LLMs), but it remains unclear what exactly their responses reflect. To answer this question we analyze responses from 50 LLMs across 45 questionnaires spanning multiple psychological domains using psychometric methodology. We found a dominant principal component explaining 47.1% of variance across standardized, response-derived questionnaire-level primary scores, revealing substantial shared structure across instruments. We investigate its meaning through semantic analysis, respondent framing, and human annotations. Semantic analysis suggests that the component partly reflects experiential self-attribution: how readily models endorse statements describing subjective states and bodily experiences. A model score emphasizing items whose disagreement decreases under human simulation correlates strongly with the component ($r=0.864$). Human ratings of 289 matched items show that inner-life content is positively associated, and social desirability negatively associated, with item-component correlations after mutual adjustment and controls for other semantic properties. Together, six rated properties explain 22.1% of variance in Fisher-transformed item-component correlations, supporting a partial interpretation involving both experiential self-attribution and socially desirable responding. These findings suggest that LLM psychometric profiles share response tendencies that require interpretation beyond the human traits their questionnaires were designed to measure.
♻ ☆ LLM Alignment--Utility Asymmetry under Semantic-Preserving Transformations NeurIPS 2026
Mohan Li, Chengyu Yu, Francesco Sovrano, Marc Langheinrich, Martin Gjoreski
Large Language Model (LLM) alignment is intended to ensure that models remain helpful and safe, but its stability under input distributional shift is not yet fully understood. Prior work shows that aligned models can fail under jailbreak prompts, alternative encodings, and cross-lingual transfer, yet these failures are usually studied as attacks rather than controlled probes of alignment generalization. Moreover, existing evidence is largely grounded in natural language variation already represented during pretraining, leaving unresolved whether alignment generalizes with semantic content or remains tied to superficial surface patterns. In this paper, we study this question using synthetic semantic-preserving transformations that are rule-based and invertible, preserving task-relevant meaning while shifting inputs beyond standard linguistic variation. Across four open-weight and four commercial models, under both fine-tuning and in-context learning, we use these transformations as a probe of alignment generalization and identify an empirical pattern we term Alignment--utility asymmetry: once models can operate effectively on transformed inputs, task utility is often substantially retained while alignment failure increases more sharply. For example, adapted GPT-4.1 mini shows only limited utility degradation under transformation while its harmful rate rises from 13.3 to 74.3; Gemini 3 Flash similarly retains near-original utility while its harmful rate increases from 2.3 to 43.0. Taken together, these results suggest that semantic-preserving distribution shifts can expose a recurring gap in how utility and alignment generalize in current LLMs.

♻ ☆ Toward Guarantees for Clinical Reasoning in Vision Language Models via Formal Verification
Vikash Singh, Debargha Ganguly, Haotian Yu, Chengwei Zhou, Prerna Singh, Brandon Lee, Vipin Chaudhary, Gourav Datta
Vision-language models (VLMs) show promise in drafting radiology reports, yet they frequently suffer from logical inconsistencies, generating diagnostic impressions unsupported by their own perceptual findings or missing logically entailed conclusions. Standard lexical metrics heavily penalize clinical paraphrasing and fail to capture these deductive failures in reference-free settings. Toward guarantees for clinical reasoning, we introduce a neurosymbolic verification framework that deterministically audits the internal consistency of VLM-generated reports. Our pipeline autoformalizes free-text radiographic findings into structured propositional evidence, utilizing an SMT solver (Z3) and a clinical knowledge base to verify whether each diagnostic claim is mathematically entailed, hallucinated, or omitted. Evaluating seven VLMs across five chest X-ray benchmarks, our verifier exposes distinct reasoning failure modes, such as conservative observation and stochastic hallucination, that remain invisible to traditional metrics. On labeled datasets, enforcing solver-backed entailment acts as a rigorous post-hoc guarantee, systematically eliminating unsupported hallucinations to significantly increase diagnostic soundness and precision in generative clinical assistants.
♻ ☆ One Size Does Not Fit All: Distribution-Guided Tensor-Wise Adaptive Sparsification for Model Merging
Yingfeng Luo, Dingyang Lin, Junxin Wang, Kaiyan Chang, Ziqiang Xu, Weiqiao Shan, Yinqiao Li, Tong Zheng, Bei Li, Anxiang Ma, Tong Xiao, Zhengtao Yu, Jingbo Zhu
Merging multiple task-specific fine-tuned checkpoints into a single multi-task model provides an attractive alternative to joint multi-task training. To mitigate cross-task interference, a common strategy is to sparsify task vectors before aggregation. However, many widely used methods rely on a uniform global sparsity ratio, overlooking differences in pruning sensitivity across tasks and parameter tensors. Consequently, such uniform sparsification may remove useful parameter updates from sensitive tensors while retaining redundant updates in others. To address this limitation, we propose TADrop, a plug-and-play sparsifier that independently determines tensor-specific keep ratios from their magnitude distributions using a scale-invariant quantile ratio. TADrop operates in a data-free manner by default, while offering optional lightweight module-level calibration when a small validation set is available. Experiments across vision, language, and multimodal benchmarks, including LLM and LoRA merging, demonstrate consistent improvements across multiple host merging methods, including a 4.99-point gain over EMR-Merging on RoBERTa GLUE. In many-task ViT-B/16 merging, the improvement increases with the number of tasks, reaching 2.26 points when merging 30 tasks. Further analyses show that TADrop preserves more task-associated neurons under matched overall sparsity. Together, these findings highlight the value of distribution-guided sparsification at the tensor level for improving model merging.

♻ ☆ Selective Latent Thinking: Adaptive Compression of LLM Reasoning Chains
Hui Xie, Jie Liu, Ziyue Qiao, Joaquin Vanschore
Explicit chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning substantially improves the reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs), but incurs high inference cost due to lengthy autoregressive traces. Existing latent reasoning methods offer a promis- ing alternative, yet they often treat reasoning as uniformly compressible, causing precision-critical intermediate steps to be overly compressed and thereby degrading reasoning accuracy. In this work, we propose Selective Latent Thinking (SLT), a reasoning paradigm that selectively compresses redundant reasoning spans into latent representations while preserving precision-critical spans as explicit CoT within the same reasoning trajectory. Our primary instantiation uses a lightweight decoder to anticipate a short upcoming reasoning span and applies confidence- based gating to determine the longest span that can be reliably compressed; the resulting selective compression policy is further optimized with trajectory-level reinforcement learning to balance answer correctness against reasoning cost. Ex- periments across four mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that which spans are compressed is critical: with the same compressor, compressing arbitrary spans reduces the GSM-Hard accuracy of Qwen3-4B from 54.7% to 17.3%, whereas keeping numerical tokens explicit retains 51.8%. SLT reduces explicit reasoning length by 58.4% with only a 2.8-point accuracy drop compared to explicit CoT on Llama-3.2-1B. Notably, the principle extends beyond this design: instantiated within existing latent reasoning models via a learned compression gate, it improves accuracy at equal reasoning length (+2.6 points over Latent-SFT) and at shorter length (+5.6 points over Latent-GRPO with 13% fewer tokens).
♻ ☆ MUSE: A Theory-Harnessed Story Engine for Vibe Narrativizing
Jianxiang Ma, Xiaocui Yang, Daling Wang, Mingfu Zhang, Zijing Wang, Yuesong Hou, Yichen Gao, Junzhao Huang
LLMs have been able to generate fluent prose, but high-quality stories also require coordinated decisions about plot, character, and language across planning, drafting, and revision. We formulate Vibe Narrativizing as turning natural-language writing requirements into a finished story. MUSE, a Theory-Harnessed Story Engine, addresses two bottlenecks: rule quality and sustained rule realization. Story theory supplies the rules, and a practical agent harness puts them to work. Knowledge engineering organizes Robert McKee's theory through rule atomization, semantic consolidation, mechanism abstraction, a single source of truth, and layered disclosure; typical examples clarify judgments that depend on context and aesthetic purpose. The harness preserves story decisions in intermediate deliverables across design, character performance, scene composition, and revision. Context engineering supplies each role with relevant guidance and decisions; a masterwork corpus provides inspiration and prose references. A worked example follows a requested object from its thematic role to climactic actions. Across four base models, MUSE improves WritingBench by 1.1 to 6.2 points over zero-shot generation; it is the only multi-stage system in our comparison to do so. It also raises LongStoryEval by more than ten points on three of the four models. ConStory-Bench consistency error density remains in the low single digits for all four models, below every reproduced story-system baseline on three of the four models. Ablations locate the largest quality contribution in structural design, voice-specific effects in the character path, and further gains in revision.

♻ ☆ FailureSensorIQ: A Multi-Choice QA Dataset for Understanding Sensor Relationships and Failure Modes NeurIPS 2025
Christodoulos Constantinides, Dhaval Patel, Shuxin Lin, Claudio Guerrero, Sunil Dagajirao Patil, Jayant Kalagnanam
We introduce FailureSensorIQ, a novel Multi-Choice Question-Answering (MCQA) benchmarking system designed to assess the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to reason and understand complex, domain-specific scenarios in Industry 4.0. Unlike traditional QA benchmarks, our system focuses on multiple aspects of reasoning through failure modes, sensor data, and the relationships between them across various industrial assets. Through this work, we envision a paradigm shift where modeling decisions are not only data-driven using statistical tools like correlation analysis and significance tests, but also domain-driven by specialized LLMs which can reason about the key contributors and useful patterns that can be captured with feature engineering. We evaluate the Industrial knowledge of over a dozen LLMs-including GPT-4, Llama, and Mistral-on FailureSensorIQ from different lens using Perturbation-Uncertainty-Complexity analysis, Expert Evaluation study, Asset-Specific Knowledge Gap analysis, ReAct agent using external knowledge-bases. Even though closed-source models with strong reasoning capabilities approach expert-level performance, the comprehensive benchmark reveals a significant drop in performance that is fragile to perturbations, distractions, and inherent knowledge gaps in the models. We also provide a real-world case study of how LLMs can drive the modeling decisions on 3 different failure prediction datasets related to various assets. We release: (a) expert-curated MCQA for various industrial assets, (b) FailureSensorIQ benchmark and Hugging Face leaderboard based on MCQA built from non-textual data found in ISO documents, and (c) LLMFeatureSelector, an LLM-based feature selection scikit-learn pipeline. The software is available at https://github.com/IBM/FailureSensorIQ.

♻ ☆ Auditing a KB Elicitation of Frontier LLM Knowledge: A Multi-dimensional Analysis of GPTKB v1.5 AKBC
Shrestha Ghosh, Luca Giordano, Yujia Hu, Tuan-Phong Nguyen, Simon Razniewski
LLMs are remarkable artifacts that have revolutionized a range of knowledge-intensive tasks. A significant contributor is their factual knowledge, which, to date, remains poorly understood, and is usually analyzed from biased samples. In this paper, we provide a framework and the results of a multi-dimensional analysis of GPTKB v1.5 (Hu et al., 2025a), a recursively elicited Knowledge Base (KB) of 100 million facts (or beliefs) of a frontier LLM, namely, GPT-4.1. Given the scale of the elicited facts, we provide a multi-dimensional approach to qualitatively and quantitatively analyze these facts as opposed to the mainstream fact completion benchmarks, which are prone to availability bias. We find that the models' factual knowledge differs quite significantly from established knowledge bases, and that its accuracy is significantly lower than indicated by previous benchmarks. We also find that inconsistency, ambiguity and hallucinations are major issues, shedding light on future research opportunities in neuro-symbolic AI concerning extraction, consolidation and verification of factual LLM knowledge.

♻ ☆ Where is the multimodal goal post? On the Ability of Foundation Models to Recognize Highlights in Videos
Aditya K Surikuchi, Raquel Fernández, Sandro Pezzelle
Foundation models are used for many real-world applications involving language understanding and generation from temporally-ordered multimodal events. In this work, we study the ability of models to identify the most important sub-events in a video, which is a fundamental prerequisite for summarizing, reasoning over, or narrating multimodal events. Specifically, we focus on football games and evaluate models on their ability to distinguish between important and non-important sub-events in a game. To this end, we construct a new dataset by leveraging publicly available football highlight reels containing the most important moments of a game according to professional experts, without any additional annotation costs. Using our dataset, we compare several state-of-the-art multimodal models and show that they fail to reliably capture contextual importance. Analyses of models beyond standard evaluation metrics reveal their tendency to rely on a single dominant modality and their ineffectiveness in synthesizing necessary information from multiple sources. Our findings underline the importance of modular architectures that dynamically adapt to multimodal heterogeneity and the need for complementary training procedures that maximize cross-modal synergy.
♻ ☆ RCStat: A Statistical Framework for using Relative Contextualization in Transformers
Debabrata Mahapatra, Shubham Agarwal, Apoorv Saxena, Subrata Mitra
Identifying which tokens and activations truly influence model predictions is critical for both the efficiency and interpretability of large auto-regressive language models. Yet in practice, token importance is typically inferred from attention distributions that entangle contextual relevance with softmax-normalization effects, obscuring relative influence across tokens and heads. We propose $\textbf{RCStat}$, a statistical framework for quantifying contextual influence in attention mechanisms. At its core $\textbf{Relative Contextualization (RC)}$ is a random variable that measures how strongly one subset of tokens contributes to another under the model's internal scoring scheme. RCStat admits computationally efficient bounds on expected influence that can be estimated at inference time. No retraining is required. We apply RCStat to two tasks. For $\textbf{attribution}$, attention heads with high expected RC accurately identify the prompt spans that drive generation, enabling reliable span-level explanations. For $\textbf{key-value cache eviction}$, RC-based adaptive thresholding selectively evicts low-impact KV entries, substantially reducing cache size while preserving generation quality. Across question answering, summarization, and attribution benchmarks, RCStat achieves consistent gains, improving generation quality by 15-40% with upto 36% error reduction for KV eviction, and attribution accuracy by 2-16%. These results demonstrate that explicitly modeling contextual influence provides a principled and practical alternative to attention-based heuristics.

♻ ☆ Contextual Representation Flow Across Depth in State-Space and Transformer Architectures
Nhat M. Hoang, Do Xuan Long, Cong-Duy Nguyen, Chunyan Miao, Min-Yen Kan, Luu Anh Tuan
State Space Models (SSMs) have emerged as efficient, linear-time alternatives to Transformer-Based Models (TBMs) for long-context processing. However, their different mechanisms raise an important question: how does contextual representation flow across depth differ between these model families and their hybrids? We examine this question across twenty-one pretrained models, combining token-wise spectral diversity, layer-wise variation on seven datasets, and answer-location probing on two retrieval tasks. This joint analysis reveals that TBMs retain most of their token diversity at the output, whereas SSMs reach richer intermediate diversity but retain less of it on average, with late-layer compression. Comparisons with reinitialized weights reveal that these profiles depend on pretrained weights, while hybrids combine strong answer-location accessibility with architecture-dependent geometry, showing that high accessibility need not require the steadiest representation flow. Finally, we conduct theoretical case studies connecting these contrasts to mechanism-level differences: query-time token mixing in TBMs, repeated recurrent updates in SSMs, and preserved attention updates in hybrids.
♻ ☆ ContinualSkillBench: Can LLM Agents Truly Evolve Their Capabilities?
Tianyi Guan, Yiding Wang, Haotong Yang, Siyuan Cao, Shirui Liu, Yi Hu, Jiaqi Li, Muhan Zhang
Modern agent frameworks equip large language models with external skill libraries to solve complex tasks. However, it remains unclear whether these systems can effectively evolve their skills and whether the resulting skills improve task-solving capabilities. To bridge this gap, we introduce ContinualSkillBench, a dynamic evaluation framework for in-context continual skill learning. It covers five domains, each with 100 tasks ordered by difficulty and opportunities for skill transfer. Our experiments show that sequential execution generally improves performance, but the gains vary substantially across models and domains. Moreover, across two models and three domains, retaining context and feedback without skill maintenance yields nearly the same mean performance as maintaining an explicit skill library. Explicit skills nevertheless provide selective benefits for tasks requiring reusable procedures or precise outputs. We further find that the weaker model (GPT-4o) accumulates more skills but reuses them less often, suggesting difficulty consolidating task-specific experience into reusable procedures. These findings show that current in-context skill evolution mechanisms can support continual adaptation, but still struggle to consistently consolidate experience into robust and transferable skills.
♻ ☆ Mechanistic Knobs in LLMs: Retrieving and Steering High-Order Semantic Features via Sparse Autoencoders
Ruikang Zhang, Shuo Wang, Qi Su
Recent work in Mechanistic Interpretability (MI) has enabled the identification and intervention of internal features in Large Language Models (LLMs). However, a persistent challenge lies in linking such internal features to the reliable control of complex, behavior-level semantic attributes in language generation. In this paper, we propose a Sparse Autoencoder-based framework for retrieving and steering semantically interpretable internal features associated with high-level linguistic behaviors. Our method employs a contrastive feature retrieval pipeline based on controlled semantic oppositions, combing statistical activation analysis and generation-based validation to distill monosemantic functional features from sparse activation spaces. Using the Big Five personality traits as a case study, we demonstrate that our method enables precise, bidirectional steering of model behavior while maintaining superior stability and performance compared to existing activation steering methods like Contrastive Activation Addition (CAA). We further identify an empirical effect, which we term Functional Faithfulness, whereby intervening on a specific internal feature induces coherent and predictable shifts across multiple linguistic dimensions aligned with the target semantic attribute. Our findings suggest that LLMs internalize deeply integrated representations of high-order concepts, and provide a novel, robust mechanistic path for the regulation of complex AI behaviors.

♻ ☆ Activation Steering with Jungian Cognitive Functions
Liu Zai, Yumeng Wang, Junchen Fu, Suzan Verberne, Joemon M. Jose
Controlling combinations of personality-related tendencies is important for adapting large language models to diverse interaction needs. But successful single-trait steering does not establish how to construct interventions for joint targets, or whether existing trait directions adequately represent them. We introduce a target-first framework that directly extracts steering directions from joint personality targets and then examines their decomposition into individual directions. To support this framework, we construct NarrationDB, linking character self-narrations to automatically evaluated joint scores across eight Jungian-inspired functions, a correspondence not directly available in existing Big Five- or MBTI-oriented resources. Experiments on three models demonstrate questionnaire-score controllability across the eight functions and coordinated changes in a tested two-function target. Directly extracted joint directions are better approximated by their corresponding individual directions than by unrelated pairings, but retain substantial residual components. Our work provides a method and resource for investigating joint personality control, with evidence that individual steering directions capture part, but not all, of the structure of joint targets.
